Improved Approximation Schemes for (Un-)Bounded Subset-Sum and Partition
Xiaoyu Wu Lin Chen

Abstract
We consider the SUBSET SUM problem and its important variants in this paper. In the SUBSET SUM problem, a (multi-)set X X of n n positive numbers and a target number t t are given, and the task is to find a subset of X X with the maximal sum that does not exceed t t . It is well known that this problem is NP-hard and admits fully polynomial-time approximation schemes (FPTASs). In recent years, it has been shown that there does not exist an FPTAS of running time 𝒪 ~ ​ ( 1 / ϵ 2 − δ ) \tilde{\mathcal{O}}(1/\epsilon^{2-\delta}) for arbitrary small δ > 0 \delta>0 assuming ( min \min ,+)-convolution conjecture [ 5 ] . However, the lower bound can be bypassed if we relax the constraint such that the task is to find a subset of X X that can slightly exceed the threshold t t by ϵ \epsilon times, and the sum of numbers within the subset is at least 1 − 𝒪 ~ ​ ( ϵ ) 1-\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon) times the optimal objective value that respects the constraint. Approximation schemes that may violate the constraint are also known as weak approximation schemes. For the SUBSET SUM problem, there is a randomized weak approximation scheme running in time 𝒪 ~ ​ ( n + 1 / ϵ 5 / 3 ) \tilde{\mathcal{O}}(n+1/\epsilon^{5/3}) [Mucha et al.’19]. For the special case where the target t t is half of the summation of all input numbers, weak approximation schemes are equivalent to approximation schemes that do not violate the constraint, and the best-known algorithm runs in 𝒪 ~ ​ ( n + 1 / ϵ 3 / 2 ) \tilde{\mathcal{O}}(n+1/\epsilon^{{3}/{2}}) time [Bringmann and Nakos’21]. In this paper, we substantially improve the state-of-art results. We derive a deterministic weak approximation scheme of running time 𝒪 ~ ​ ( n + 1 / ϵ 3 / 2 ) \tilde{\mathcal{O}}(n+1/\epsilon^{3/2}) for the SUBSET SUM problem, which is the first deterministic approximation scheme of subquadratic running time. For unbounded SUBSET SUM where each input number can be used arbitrarily many times, we obtain an 𝒪 ~ ​ ( n + 1 / ϵ ) \tilde{\mathcal{O}}(n+1/\epsilon) -time deterministic weak approximation scheme, which is the best possible. For PARTITION, we improve the existing result by establishing an 𝒪 ~ ​ ( n + 1 / ϵ 5 / 4 ) \tilde{\mathcal{O}}(n+1/\epsilon^{5/4}) -time deterministic approximation scheme. These results are built upon our main technical contributions: i). a number-theoretic rounding mechanism that leverages a number-theoretic property of integers called smoothness, and ii). a divide-and-conquer based framework that combines FFT (Fast Fourier Transform) with the number-theoretic property.



Keywords:
Approximation scheme; Combinatorial optimization; Partition; Subset-Sum

1
Introduction

We study approximation algorithms for the fundamental problem SUBSET SUM and its special cases in this paper. We first introduce the problems.

Let
\Sigma(Y)
denote the sum of elements in a (multi-)set
Y
. SUBSET SUM is defined as follows:

Definition 1

(SUBSET SUM)
.

Given a (multi-)set
X
of
n
positive integers and a target
t>0
, find a subset
X^{\prime}\subset X
which achieves the maximum sum among all subsets summing up to
t
. Formally, the task is to find
X^{\prime}\subset X
such that

\Sigma(X^{\prime})=\max\{\Sigma{(Y)}\ |\ Y\subset X,\Sigma(Y)\leq t\},

SUBSET SUM problem is a fundamental optimization problem in computer science and is one of Karp’s initial list of 21 NP-complete problems
[
21
]
. An important field of study on NP-hard problems is finding efficient approximation algorithms. In particular, a Fully Polynomial Time Approximation Scheme (FPTAS) for a maximization problem is an algorithm that, given an instance of size
n
and a parameter
\epsilon>0
, returns a solution whose value is at least
1-\epsilon
times the optimal solution. Importantly, the run-time of an FPTAS is polynomial of
n
and
1/\epsilon
.

SUBSET SUM is one of the first NP-hard problems shown to possess FPTASs and there is a long line of research on finding faster FPTASs for SUBSET SUM. The first published FPTAS was designed by Ibarra and Kim
[
22
]
requires
{\mathcal{O}}(n/\epsilon^{2})
-time. Lawler
[
28
]
subsequently proposed an FPTAS
with improved time bound
{\mathcal{O}}(n+1/\epsilon^{4})
. Later, Gens and Levner obtained further improved schemes running in time
{\mathcal{O}}(n/\epsilon)

[
11
,
12
]
and in time
{\mathcal{O}}(\min\{n/\epsilon,n+1/\epsilon^{3}\})

[
10
]
, which was further improved by Kellerer et al.
[
25
,
23
]
to a
{\mathcal{O}}(\min\{n/\epsilon,n+1/\epsilon^{2}\log(1/\epsilon)\})
-time algorithm. Very recently, Bringmann
[
5
]
showed a conditioned lower bound on approximating SUBSET SUM: assuming the
(\min,+)
-convolution conjecture, SUBSET SUM has no approximation scheme in time
{\mathcal{O}}((n+1/\epsilon)^{2-\delta})
for any constant
\delta>0
. This strong lower bound relies on that the constraint of
\Sigma(Y)\leq t
is strict. If the constraint can be slightly violated, then substantially faster approximation schemes exist. Such approximation schemes are called weak approximation schemes. Formally,

Definition 2

(WEAK-APX for SUBSET SUM)
.

Let
X^{*}
be an optimal solution for an instance
(X,t)
of the SUBSET SUM problem. Given
(X,t)
, a weak
(1-\epsilon)
-approximation
algorithm (or a weak approximation scheme) for SUBSET SUM returns
Y\subset X
such that

(1-\epsilon)\Sigma(X^{*})\leq\Sigma(Y)\leq(1+\epsilon)t.

Recently, Mucha et al.
[
32
]
designed a randomized weak approximation scheme for SUBSET SUM with a strongly subquadratic running time of
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(n+1/\epsilon^{{5}/{3}})
, where the
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}
notation hides polylogarithmic factors in
n
and
1/\epsilon
. Bringmann and Nakos
[
5
]
mentioned in their paper that their technique should yield a randomized weak approximation scheme of running time
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(n+1/\epsilon^{{3}/{2}})
for SUBSET SUM, but there is no formal proof. So far all existing subquadratic approximation schemes are randomized. It remains as an important open problem whether there is a deterministic weak subquadratic time approximation scheme for SUBSET SUM.

We also study PARTITION, which is a fundamental special case of SUBSET SUM where
t
is fixed to
\Sigma(X)/2
. Formally,

Definition 3

(PARTITION)
.

Given a (multi-)set
X
of
n
positive integers, find a subset
X^{\prime}\subset X
that achieves the maximum sum among all subsets whose sums do not exceed
\Sigma(X)/2
. Formally, the task is to find
X^{\prime}\subset X
such that

\Sigma(X^{\prime})=\max\{\Sigma(Y)\ |\ Y\subset X,\Sigma(Y)\leq\Sigma(X)/2\}.

PARTITION has many practical applications, including scheduling
[
7
]
, minimization of circuit sizes and cryptography
[
30
]
, as well as game theory
[
16
,
31
]
. As a special case of SUBSET SUM, all SUBSET SUM algorithms also apply to PARTITION. A particularly important observation raised by Mucha et al.
[
32
]
is that any weak approximation scheme for SUBSET SUM is an approximation scheme for PARTITION.
Designing approximation algorithms specifically for PARTITION also has a long history. In 1980, Gens and Levner
[
13
]
proposed an FPTAS with running time
{\mathcal{O}}(\min\{n/\epsilon,n+1/\epsilon^{2}\})
. Recently, Bringmann and Nakos
[
5
]
obtained a deterministic FPTAS with a running time of
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(n+1/\epsilon^{{3}/{2}})
. Regarding the lower bound, PARTITION does not admit any approximation scheme of running time
{\mathcal{O}}(poly(n)/\epsilon^{1-\delta})
for any constant
\delta>0
, assuming the SETH
[
1
]
or the SetCover conjecture
[
8
]
.

An important observation
[
32
]
is that, since the target is
\Sigma(X)/2
in PARTITION, a weak approximation scheme for PARTITION is also an approximation scheme. Hence, from an upper bound perspective, PARTITION and SUBSET SUM differ significantly: PARTITION admits a strongly subquadratic running time approximation scheme while SUBSET SUM does not.

The third problem we study in this paper is the UNBOUNDED SUBSET SUM, which is another special case of SUBSET SUM, where every element has infinitely many copies. Formally,

Definition 4

(UNBOUNDED SUBSET SUM)
.

Given a set
X=\{x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{n}\}
of
n
different positive integers and a target
t>0
, find the set of non-negative integers
(m_{1},m_{2},\cdots,m_{n})
such that

\sum^{n}_{i=1}x_{i}m_{i}=\max\{\sum^{n}_{i=1}x_{i}y_{i}\leq t\ |\ (y_{1},y_{2},\cdots,y_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}_{\geq 0}\}.

UNBOUNDED SUBSET SUM can be reduced to (bounded) SUBSET SUM
[
24
]
, thus algorithms designed for SUBSET SUM also work with UNBOUNDED SUBSET SUM. The best known (deterministic) approximation scheme is due to Jansen and Kraft
[
18
]
, running in time
\tilde{{\mathcal{O}}}(n+1/\epsilon^{2})
. In terms of weak approximation schemes, Bringmann et al.
[
4
]
showed a randomized
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(n+1/\epsilon^{{3}/{2}})
-time algorithm for a more general problem UNBOUNDED KNAPSACK. It is not clear whether UNBOUNDED SUBSET SUM admits a better approximation algorithm.

Related Work.

SUBSET SUM is a special case of KNAPSACK. There is a long line of research on approximation schemes for KNAPSACK, see, e.g.,
[
17
,
28
,
33
,
13
]
. Very recently, Chan
[
6
]
proposed an FPTAS for KNAPSACK with a running time of
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(n+1/\epsilon^{{12}/{5}})

[
6
]
. Later, Jin
[
20
]
obtained an improved algorithm of running time
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(n+1/\epsilon^{{9}/{4}})
, which is the best-known so far. Whether an
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(n+1/\epsilon^{2})
time FPTAS exists is still a crucial open problem. Conditional lower bound has been obtained for KNAPSACK
[
5
]
: an FPTAS with
{\mathcal{O}}(n+1/\epsilon^{2-\delta})\ (\delta>0)
running time would refute the
(\min,+)
-convolution conjecture.

While we focus on approximation algorithms in this paper, it is worth mentioning that exact algorithms for SUBSET SUM have also received extensive studies. Bellman showed in 1957 that KNAPSACK, and hence SUBSET SUM, can be solved in time
{\mathcal{O}}(nt)
by dynamic programming
[
2
]
. Important progress has been achieved in recent years for SUBSET SUM. In 2019, Koiliaris and Xu
[
27
]
obtained an
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{n}t,t^{4/3})
-time algorithm for SUBSET SUM, followed by a randomized
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(n+t)
algorithm
[
3
,
22
]
. For UNBOUNDED SUBSET SUM, Bringmann
[
3
]
gave an exact algorithm with a running time of
\tilde{{\mathcal{O}}}(t)
. Jansen and Rohwedder
[
19
]
showed later that there is an exact algorithm with a running time
\tilde{{\mathcal{O}}}(a_{n})
for the stronger parameter
a_{n}
, where
a_{n}
refers to the largest input number. Klein
[
26
]
showed an exact algorithm with a running time of
\tilde{{\mathcal{O}}}(a_{0}^{2})
where
a_{0}
refers to the smallest input number. In terms of lower bounds, assuming the strong exponential time hypothesis (SETH), Abboud et al.
[
1
]
proved that there is no algorithm for SUBSET SUM or UNBOUNDED SUBSET SUM with a running time of
{\mathcal{O}}(t^{1-\delta})
for any
\delta>0
.

This paper is motivated by the open problem proposed by Bringmann and Nakos
[
5
]
. They mentioned the possibility of extending their approximation scheme for PARTITION to a randomized
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(n+\epsilon^{-\frac{3}{2}})
-time weak approximation for SUBSET SUM. However, they did not provide proof and left an open problem on the existence of a subquadratic deterministic weak approximation algorithm for SUBSET SUM.

Our contributions.

The main contribution of this paper is to obtain substantial improvement on the approximation schemes for three closely related fundamental problems: SUBSET SUM, PARTITION and UNBOUNDED SUBSET SUM. More precisely,

•

We obtain a deterministic
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(n+\epsilon^{-\frac{3}{2}})
-time weak
(1-\epsilon)
-approximation algorithm for SUBSET SUM. This gives the first deterministic subquadratic algorithm for weak approximating SUBSET SUM, and resolves the open problem raised by Bringmann and Nakos
[
5
]
.

•

We obtain an
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(n+\epsilon^{-\frac{5}{4}})
-time deterministic FPTAS for PARTITION, improving upon the existing
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(n+\epsilon^{-\frac{3}{2}})
-time deterministic FPTAS. Furthermore, unlike the prior algorithm that is highly tailored to PARTITION, our algorithm is also an
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(n+\epsilon^{-\frac{5}{4}})
-time weak
(1-\epsilon)
-approximation algorithm for SUBSET SUM when the target
t=\Theta(\Sigma(X))
. This indicates that for SUBSET SUM, the special case PARTITION where
t=\Sigma(X)/2
, is perhaps not too much different from the case, say,
t=\Sigma(X)/3
, despite that a weak approximation algorithm for PARTITION is naturally an approximation algorithm.

•

We obtain a deterministic
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(n+\epsilon^{-1})
-time weak
(1-\epsilon)
-approximation algorithm for UNBOUNDED SUBSET SUM. Note that by taking
\epsilon=\frac{1}{t+1}
, the weak approximation algorithm implies an exact algorithm, therefore the lower bound on the running time of exact algorithms also applies. In particular, for arbitrary small constant
\delta>0
, there does not exist any
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(n+\epsilon^{-1+\delta})
-time weak
(1-\epsilon)
-approximation algorithm for UNBOUNDED SUBSET SUM assuming SETH
[
1
]
, hence our algorithm is essentially the best possible.

Overview of our techniques.

The study of approximation schemes for subset-sum dates back to the 1970s. Recent breakthrough results
[
32
,
5
]
that break the barrier of the quadratic running time,
\tilde{{\mathcal{O}}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}})
, all rely crucially on FFT (Fast Fourier Transform). However, FFT suffers from that the input numbers can be very large. Using the standard rounding technique, we can obtain a rounded instance with
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
integers of value
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
, and a straightforward FFT only yields an
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}})
-time PTAS. Thus, FFT has to be carefully combined with some additive combinatoric result
[
32
]
or some sparsification technique
[
5
]
to achieve a better running time. We improve FFT through a completely new observation. We observe that, if many input numbers share a large common divisor, then we can first scale down these numbers by dividing the common divisor, use FFT to compute their scaled-down subset-sums, and then scale up these subset-sums. Following this natural idea, we observe that FFT will benefit if all the input numbers are very “smooth”. In number theory, a
y
-smooth number is a number whose prime factors do not exceed
y
. If all the input numbers (of value
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
) are
\frac{1}{\epsilon^{\theta}}
-smooth, then we can write them into the form
h_{1}h_{2}\cdots h_{d}
where
h_{i}
’s are
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon^{\theta}})
. Consequently, there are at most
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon^{\theta}})
prime factors for these
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
input numbers to share, which means that many numbers will share a large common divisor. We can then establish a divide-and-conquer based framework that allows FFT to leverage the smoothness.

The question is, if the input numbers are not smooth, can we round them to smooth numbers with a small loss? This is a very challenging problem in number theory. While it is generally believed that for arbitrary small
\theta>0
and any
x\in[N]
(where
[N]:=\{0,1,\cdots,N\}
) there should always exist
x^{\theta}
-smooth numbers that are sufficiently close to
x
, so far it is only known that
x^{\theta}
-smooth numbers are guaranteed to exist within
[x-\Theta({\sqrt{x}}),x+\Theta({\sqrt{x}})]

[
29
]
. Moreover, there do not seem to exist good algorithms for computing such a smooth number except a straightforward bruteforce
[
15
]
. Both the error and running time, which are
\Theta(\sqrt{x})
, do not suffice for our needs. Therefore, we establish a number-theoretic lemma (Lemma
6
) that substantially extends the result of Jin
[
20
]
. We relax the notion of smoothness to “semi-smoothness” in the sense that for any
\lambda>0
and any set
X
of numbers in
\Theta(\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda}})
, we can always find
x_{j}^{\prime}
for each
x_{j}\in X
such that
|x_{j}-x_{j}^{\prime}|\leq\epsilon x_{j}
,
x_{j}^{\prime}=x_{j}^{common}\cdot x_{j}^{smooth}
where
x_{j}^{smooth}
is
\frac{1}{\epsilon^{\theta}}
-smooth for arbitrary small
\theta
, and there are only
\log|X|(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{{\mathcal{O}}(1)}
distinct
x_{j}^{common}
’s. That is, we can always round the input numbers and then divide them into only logarithmically many groups such that by modulo the common divisor of numbers in each group, they all become smooth numbers. More importantly, the overall running time for rounding all the numbers is almost linear in
|X|+\frac{1}{\epsilon}
. We then combine FFT with such a number theoretic construction carefully to obtain improved algorithmic results for (un-)bounded SUBSET SUM and its special case PARTITION. Our method may be of separate interest to other related optimization problems, and also adds to the list of algorithmic applications of smooth numbers surveyed in
[
15
]
.

Organization of the paper.

In Section
1.1
and Section
1.2
, we give the notations and the definitions which are frequently used in this paper. In Section
2.1
, we derive exact and approximation algorithms for computing the sum of multisets (see Definition
11
). In Section
3
, we introduce a number-theoretic rounding lemma (i.e., Lemma
6
), which implies that input numbers can be effectively rounded to semi-smooth numbers. In Section
4
, we consider smooth numbers, and derive algorithms for computing (capped) subset-sums of smooth numbers. Then we consider general input instances. In Section
5
, we establish a lemma (see Lemma
10
) which decomposes an arbitrary SUBSET SUM instance into a logarithmic number of sub-instances with a much simplified structure, and therefore it suffices to develop an (weak) approximation scheme for each sub-instance. The subsequent Section
6
and Section
7
are dedicated to designing improved approximation algorithms for PARTITION and SUBSET SUM, respectively. Finally in Section
8
, we present the weak approximation scheme for UNBOUNDED SUBSET SUM.

1.1
Notations and Definitions

We present notations and definitions that will be used throughout this paper. They mostly follow from prior works
[
32
]
.

Let
\mathbb{Z}
be the set of all integers. Let
\mathbb{N}=\{0,1,2,\cdots\}
be the set of all natural numbers and let
\mathbb{N}_{+}
be the set of all positive integers. Let
\mathbb{R}
be the set of all real numbers and
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be the set of all non-negative real numbers. For a number
x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, we define
pow(x)
as the largest power of 2 not exceeding
x
(i.e.
2^{pow(x)}\leq x<2^{pow(x)+1}
).

A
multiset
is a set-like, unordered collection in which repetition of elements is allowed. Given a finite multiset
X
, we call the number of all elements contained in
X
its cardinality and use
|X|
to represent it. We let
set(X)
denote the set of all
distinct
elements in
X
.

Some
operations
on multisets are defined in the following.

Definition 5
.

Given a finite multiset
X
and an element
x
. If
x\in X
, we define
card_{X}[x]
as the multiplicity of
x
in
X
and we let
card_{X}[x]=0
if
x\notin X
.

We call
Y
a subset of the multiset
X
if
set(Y)
is a subset of
set(X)
and
card_{Y}[x]\leq card_{X}[x]
holds for every
x\in X
. We use
Y\subset X
to represent that
Y
is a subset of
X
.

Definition 6

(Multiset Complement)
.

Given
Y\subset X
, we define a multiset
X\backslash Y
as follows:
for every
x\in X
, if
card_{X}[x]-card_{Y}[x]\neq 0
, then
x\in X\backslash Y
and
card_{X\backslash Y}[x]=card_{X}[x]-card_{Y}[x]
; else if
card_{X}[x]-card_{Y}[x]=0
, then
x\notin X\backslash Y
. We call
X\backslash Y
the multiset-complement of
Y
in
X
.

Definition 7

(Scalar Multiplication)
.

Given a multiset
X:=\{x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{n}\}
and a constant
k
. We define
kX:=\{kx_{1},kx_{2},\cdots,kx_{n}\}
, which is the multiset obtained by multiplying each element in
X
by
k
.

Definition 8

(Multisets Union)
.

Given multisets
X_{1},X_{2},\cdots,X_{\ell}
, where
\ell\geq 2
. We denote
X_{1}\cup X_{2}\cup\cdots\cup X_{\ell}
as a set containing all distinct elements in
X_{1},X_{2},\cdots,X_{\ell}
, that is, each element in
X_{1}\cup X_{2}\cup\cdots\cup X_{\ell}
is distinct and
set(X_{i})\subset X_{1}\cup X_{2}\cup\cdots\cup X_{\ell}
for
i=1,2,\cdots,\ell
. We call

X_{1}\cup X_{2}\cup\cdots\cup X_{\ell}
the union of
X_{i}
’s.

Moreover, we denote
X_{1}\dot{\cup}X_{2}\dot{\cup}\cdots\dot{\cup}X_{\ell}
as a multiset containing all elements in
X_{1},X_{2},\cdots X_{\ell}
. To be specific,
X_{1}\dot{\cup}X_{2}\dot{\cup}\cdots\dot{\cup}X_{\ell}
satisfies the followings: (1).
X_{i}\subset X_{1}\dot{\cup}X_{2}\dot{\cup}\cdots\dot{\cup}X_{\ell}
for
i=1,2,\cdots,\ell
; (2). the multiplicity of an element in
X_{1}\dot{\cup}X_{2}\dot{\cup}\cdots\dot{\cup}X_{\ell}
is the sum of the multiplicity of this element in each
X_{i}
, i.e.,

card_{X_{1}\dot{\cup}X_{2}\dot{\cup}\cdots\dot{\cup}X_{\ell}}[x]=\sum^{\ell}_{i=1}card_{X_{i}}[x]
for any
x\in X_{1}\dot{\cup}X_{2}\dot{\cup}\cdots\dot{\cup}X_{\ell}
. We call
X_{1}\dot{\cup}X_{2}\dot{\cup}\cdots\dot{\cup}X_{\ell}
the multiset-union of
X_{i}
’s.

For simplicity, we sometimes abbreviate
X_{1}{\cup}X_{2}{\cup}\cdots{\cup}X_{\ell}
and
X_{1}\dot{\cup}X_{2}\dot{\cup}\cdots\dot{\cup}X_{\ell}
as
{\cup}^{\ell}_{i=1}X_{i}
and
\dot{\cup}^{\ell}_{i=1}X_{i}
, respectively.

Definition 9
.

For a multiset
X\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, we denote the maximum (resp. minimum) element in
X
as
X^{max}:=\max\{x\ |\ x\in X\}
(resp.
X^{min}:=\min\{x\ |\ x\in X\}
), and the sum of all elements in
X
as
\Sigma(X):=\sum_{x\in X}x
.

Definition 10

(SUBSET-SUMS)
.

For a multiset
X\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, we define
S(X):=set\{\Sigma(Y)\ |\ Y\subset X\}
as the set of all possible subset sums of
X
, and call
S(X)
the subset-sums of
X
. We call two multisets
X_{1},X_{2}\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
equivalent if
S(X_{1})=S(X_{2})
.

Given
[a,b]\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, we use
S(X;[a,b])
to denote
S(X)\cap[a,b]
. Specifically, for
S(X;[0,b])
, we call it the
b
-capped subset-sums of
X
.

Definition 11

(Multisets Sum)
.

Given multisets
X_{1},X_{2},\cdots,X_{\ell}\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, where
\ell\geq 2
. We define
X_{1}\oplus X_{2}\oplus\cdots\oplus X_{\ell}:=set\{\sum^{\ell}_{i=1}x_{i}\ |\ x_{i}\in X_{i}\cup\{0\}\text{ \ for every \ }1\leq i\leq\ell\}
, and call it the sumset of
X_{i}
’s. For simplicity, we sometimes abbreviate
X_{1}\oplus X_{2}\oplus\cdots\oplus X_{\ell}
as
\oplus^{\ell}_{i=1}X_{i}
. Given
b\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, we call
(\oplus^{\ell}_{i=1}X_{i})\cap[0,b]
the
b
-capped sumset of
X_{i}
’s.

Given
F
as a set of functions, by
F\subset\Theta(g)
we mean that
f=\Theta(g)
holds for every
f\in F
. Unless otherwise specified, we use the notation
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(T)
to denote a function
{\mathcal{O}}(T(\log T)^{c})
for any constant
c>0
, i.e.,
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}
suppresses polylogarithmic factors.

Throughout this paper,
\epsilon
refers to an arbitrarily small positive number.

1.2
Definitions of Approximate Set and Backtracking Oracle.

For technical reason, we introduce two concepts in this subsection, the approximate set and the backtracking oracle. Roughly speaking, since the multiset of input numbers may be difficult to deal with directly, we will build an alternative set with a much simplified structure but is sufficient for the purpose of weak approximation. Such a set is the approximate set of the input multiset, further parameterized
r
and
u
as we specify in Definition
12
. Our algorithm will work on the approximate set instead of the input multiset, but then the solution obtained from the approximate set needs to be transformed back to the input multiset. Such a transformation is achieved via the backtracking oracle. We remark that if we only want to “approximately determine” SUBSET SUM or PARTITION, that is, to answer “Yes” or “No” instead of returning a feasible solution when the answer is “Yes”, then the backtracking oracle can be safely ignored.

Definition 12

(
(r,u)
-APX-SET)
.

Given a multiset
A\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, we call
C
an
(r,u)
-approximate set of
A
if and only if
C
satisfies all of the following conditions:

(i).

C\subset[0,(1+r)u]
;

(ii).

for any
c\in C
, there exists
a\in A
such that
|c-a|\leq ru
;

(iii).

for any
a^{\prime}\in A\cap[0,u]
, there exists
c^{\prime}\in C
such that
|c^{\prime}-a^{\prime}|\leq ru
.

In particular, we call
C
an
r
-approximate set of
A
if and only if
C
is an
(r,A^{max})
-approximate set of
A
.

We call
C
an
(r,u)
-approximate set of
A
with an additive error of
ERR
if
C
satisfies all of the following conditions:

(i).

C\subset[0,(1+r)u]
;

(ii).

for any
c\in C
, there exists
a\in A
such that
|c-a|\leq ru+ERR
;

(iii).

for any
a^{\prime}\in A\cap[0,u]
, there exists
c^{\prime}\in C
such that
|c^{\prime}-a^{\prime}|\leq ru+ERR
, where
ERR>0
is an additive error.

Definition 13

(T-time ORACLE for Backtracking)
.

We give the definitions of
T-time oracle
for four cases.

Def-1.

Given multisets
X_{1},X_{2},\cdots,X_{\ell}\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and a set
C\subset\oplus^{\ell}_{i=1}X_{i}
, a
T-time oracle
for backtracking from
C
to
\dot{\cup}X^{\ell}_{i=1}
is an algorithm that given any
c\in C
, in
T
processing time, it will return
(x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{\ell})
satisfying
\sum^{\ell}_{i=1}x_{i}=c
, where
x_{i}\in X_{i}\cup\{0\}
for every
i=1,2,\cdots,\ell
.

Def-2.

Given a multiset
X\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and a set
C^{\prime}\subset S(X)
, a
T-time oracle
for backtracking from
C^{\prime}
to
X
is an algorithm that given any
c^{\prime}\in C^{\prime}
, in
T
processing time, it will return
X^{\prime}\subset X
satisfying
\Sigma(X^{\prime})=c^{\prime}
.

Def-3.

Given multisets
X_{1},X_{2},\cdots,X_{\ell}\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. Let
B
be an
(r,u)
-approximate set of
\oplus^{\ell}_{i=1}X_{i}
with an additive error of
ERR
. A
T-time oracle
for backtracking from
B
to
\dot{\cup}X^{\ell}_{i=1}
is an algorithm that given any
b\in B
, in
T
processing time, it will return
(x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{\ell})
satisfying
|b-\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}x_{i}|\leq ru+ERR
, where
x_{i}\in X_{i}\cup\{0\}
for every
i=1,2,\cdots,\ell
.

Def-4.

Given a multiset
X\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. Let
B^{\prime}
be an
(r,u)
-approximate set of
S(X)
with an additive error of
ERR
, an
(r,u)
-approximate
T-time oracle
for backtracking from
B^{\prime}
to
X
is an algorithm that given any
b^{\prime}\in B^{\prime}
, in
T
processing time, it will return
X^{\prime}\subset X
satisfying
|b^{\prime}-\Sigma(X^{\prime})|\leq ru+ERR
.

Now we give a very high-level description on how we leverage the two concepts introduced above in our algorithms. Towards that, we first present a simple observation.

Lemma 1
.

Given an instance
(X,t)
of SUBSET SUM. Let
OPT
be the optimal objective value of
(X,t)
and let
X_{t}=\{x\in X\ |\ x\leq t\}
. If
\Sigma(X_{t})<t/2
, then
OPT<t/2
and
X_{t}
is the optimal solution of
(X,t)
. Else if
\Sigma(X_{t})<t/2
, we can assert that
OPT\geq t/2
.

Proof.

Given an instance
(X,t)
of SUBSET SUM, let
X^{*}\subset X
and
OPT
be the optimal solution and optimal objective value of
(X,t)
, respectively. Define
X_{t}:=\{x\in X\ |\ x\leq t\}
. It is easy to see that if
\Sigma(X_{t})<t/2
, then apparently
OPT<t/2
and
X_{t}
is the optimal solution of
(X,t)
. We claim that if
\Sigma(X_{t})\geq t/2
, then
OPT\geq t/2
.

Assume that
\Sigma(X_{t})\geq t/2
and
OPT<t/2
. Recall that
X^{*}\subset X
is the optimal solution of
(X,t)
. The following two observations show that
x>t
holds for every
x\in X\backslash X^{*}
.

•

if there exists
x\in X\backslash X^{*}
with
x\leq t/2
, then
\{x\}\dot{\cup}X^{*}
is a better solution;

•

if there exists
x\in X\backslash X^{*}
with
t/2<x\leq t
, then
\{x\}
is a better solution.

Hence
X_{t}=\{x\in X:x\leq t\}\subset X\backslash(X\backslash X^{*})=X^{*}
. Since
OPT<t/2
, we have
\Sigma(X_{t})<t/2
, which contradicts the fact that
\Sigma(X_{t})\geq t/2
. Thus if
\Sigma(X_{t})\geq t/2
, then
OPT\geq t/2
.

Note that
X_{t}:=\{x\in X\ |\ x\leq t\}
and
\Sigma(X_{t})
can be obtained in linear time, so far the proof of Lemma
1
is completed.∎∎

How Definition
12
and Definition
13
are used in our algorithms?
Consider any instance
(X,t)
of SUBSET SUM. Let
X^{*}\subset X
and
OPT
be the optimal solution and optimal objective value of
(X,t)
, respectively. Taking advantage of Lemma
1
, we only need to consider the case that
OPT\geq t/2
. Let
B_{\epsilon}
be an
(\epsilon,t)
-approximate set of
S(X)
and assume that there is a
T-time oracle
for backtracking from
B_{\epsilon}
to
X
. Then according to Definition
12
and Definition
13
, it is straightforward that a weak
(1-{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon))
-approximate solution of
(X,t)
can be determined within
{\mathcal{O}}(|B_{\epsilon}|+T)
processing time. Thus towards designing a weak approximation scheme for SUBSET SUM with a running time of
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(n+1/\epsilon^{1+\delta})
, it suffices to design an
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(n+1/\epsilon^{1+\delta})
-time algorithm such that given any instance
(X,t)
of SUBSET SUM, the algorithm can return: (i). an
(\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon),t)
-approximate set with cardinality of
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(n+1/\epsilon^{1+\delta})
for
S(X)
, where recall that
S(X)
is the set of all subset-sums of
X
; and (ii). an
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(n+1/\epsilon^{1+\delta})
-time oracle for backtracking from this approximate set to
X
. Therefore, when designing the approximation algorithm for SUBSET SUM, we mainly consider two problems: one is to find the approximate set of
S(X)
of a small cardinality, and the other is to build an efficient oracle for backtracking.

2
Computing Sumset.

The algorithms designed in this paper for (un-)bounded SUBSET SUM follow a general divide-and-conquer framework, where we need to frequently compute (approximate) sumset and give the corresponding oracle for backtracking. In this section, we introduce exact and approximation algorithms for computing (capped) sumset, respectively. Both exact and approximation algorithms will be used in our subsequent analysis. In particular, the exact algorithm is (almost) linear in the largest integer in the input, and will thus be used when the input only involves very small numbers (which may be
o(1/\epsilon)
). On the other hand, the approximation algorithm is linear in
1/\epsilon
, which will be used when the input numbers are large.

Note that sumset is always a set. Throughout this section, when we say compute a sumset, we mean to specify every element in this sumset.

2.1
Exact Algorithms for Computing Sumset.

One basic approach to compute the sumset is to use Fast Fourier Transform (FFT). In particular, we can use the following lemma extended from
[
5
]
.

Lemma 2
.

Given sets
X_{1},X_{2},\cdots,X_{\ell}\subset\mathbb{N}
, in
{\mathcal{O}}(\sigma\log\sigma\log\ell)
processing time, where
\sigma:=X^{max}_{1}+X^{max}_{2}+\cdots+X^{max}_{\ell}
, we can compute
X_{1}\oplus X_{2}\oplus\cdots\oplus X_{\ell}
and build an
{\mathcal{O}}(\sigma\log\sigma\log\ell)
-time oracle for backtracking from
\oplus^{\ell}_{i=1}X_{i}
to
\dot{\cup}^{\ell}_{i=1}X_{i}
.

Remark.
Lemma
2
implies an algorithm for computing subset-sums of a multiset. This is because that for any multiset
X\subset\mathbb{N}
, we have
S(X)=\oplus_{x\in set(X)}\{x\}
and
X=\dot{\cup}_{x\in set(X)}\{x\}
. Then by Lemma
2
, in
{\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma(X)\log\Sigma(X)\log|X|)
processing time, we can compute
S(X)
and build an
{\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma(X)\log\Sigma(X)\log|X|)
-time oracle for backtracking from
S(X)
to
X
.

Bringmann et al.
[
5
]
have derived an algorithm for computing
X_{1}\oplus X_{2}\oplus\cdots\oplus X_{\ell}
. In the following, we show that this algorithm can be extended so that it also gives an oracle for backtracking from
\oplus^{\ell}_{i=1}X_{i}
to
\dot{\cup}^{\ell}_{i=1}X_{i}
. We first present the following observation.

Observation 1
.

Given sets
B_{1},B_{2}\subset\mathbb{N}
, in
{\mathcal{O}}((B^{max}_{1}+B^{max}_{2})\log(B^{max}_{1}+B^{max}_{2}))
processing time, we can compute
B_{1}\oplus B_{2}
and meanwhile build an
{\mathcal{O}}((B^{max}_{1}+B^{max}_{2})\log(B^{max}_{1}+B^{max}_{2}))
-time oracle for backtracking from
B_{1}\oplus B_{2}
to
B_{1}\dot{\cup}B_{2}
.

Proof.

We first construct arrays
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
with a length of
B^{max}_{1}+B^{max}_{2}
. Then we store
B_{i}(i=1,2)
in arrays
L_{i}
such that (1).
L_{i}[0]=1
; (2).
L_{i}[j]=1
if
B_{i}
contains
j
; (3).
L_{i}[j]=0
otherwise. Note that the length of
L_{i}(i=1,2)
is
B^{max}_{1}+B^{max}_{2}
. Perform FFT (Fast Fourier Transform) on
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
, we can obtain
B_{1}\oplus B_{2}
in
{\mathcal{O}}((B^{max}_{1}+B^{max}_{2})\log(B^{max}_{1}+B^{max}_{2}))
processing time.

For the second part of the observation, the oracle built for backtracking from
B_{1}\oplus B_{2}
to
B_{1}\dot{\cup}B_{2}
works as follows: given any
c\in B_{1}\oplus B_{2}
, consider every
b
satisfying
L_{1}[b]=1
, i.e.,
b\in B_{1}\cup\{0\}
. If
L_{2}[c-b]=1
, i.e.,
c-b\in B_{2}\cup\{0\}
, let
b_{1}=b
and
b_{2}=c-b
, it follows
c=b_{1}+b_{2}
. It is easy to see that the time to determine
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
is
{\mathcal{O}}((B^{max}_{1}+B^{max}_{2})\log(B^{max}_{1}+B^{max}_{2}))
.∎∎

Now we are ready to prove Lemma
2
.

Proof of Lemma
2
.

We design an iterative approach to compute
\oplus^{\ell}_{i=1}X_{i}
and build an oracle for backtracking.

Define
X_{j}:=\{0\}
for
j=\ell+1,\ell+2,\cdots,2^{pow(\ell)+1}
. It holds that
\oplus^{\ell}_{i=1}X_{i}=\oplus^{2^{pow(\ell)+1}}_{i=1}X_{i}
. We build a tree structure of
{\mathcal{O}}(pow(\ell))
layers as follows:

•

At the beginning, we create
2^{pow(\ell)+1}
leaf nodes and let the
i
-th leaf node contain
X_{i}
.

•

At iteration-1, we use Observation
1
to compute pairwise sumset
X_{2j_{1}-1}\oplus X_{2j_{1}}
and derive an
{\mathcal{O}}((X^{max}_{2j_{1}-1}+X^{max}_{2j_{1}})\log(X^{max}_{2j_{1}-1}+X^{max}_{2j_{1}}))
-time oracle for backtracking from
X_{2j_{1}-1}\oplus X_{2j_{1}}
to
X_{2j_{1}-1}\dot{\cup}X_{2j_{1}}
, where
j_{1}=1,2,\cdots,2^{pow(\ell)}
. For two nodes containing
X_{2j_{1}-1}
and
X_{2j_{1}}
separately, we create a parent node of these two nodes, and let the parent node contain
X_{2j_{1}-1}\oplus X_{2j_{1}}
and the oracle for backtracking from
X_{2j_{1}-1}\oplus X_{2j_{1}}
to
X_{2j_{1}-1}\dot{\cup}X_{2j_{1}}
. The processing time for one pair
X_{2j_{1}-1}\oplus X_{2j_{1}}
is
{\mathcal{O}}((X^{max}_{2j_{1}-1}+X^{max}_{2j_{1}})\log(X^{max}_{2j_{1}-1}+X^{max}_{2j_{1}}))
, thus the total processing time at iteration-1 is
{\mathcal{O}}(\sigma\log\sigma)
. We then obtain a reduced instance with size of
2^{pow(\ell)}
, i.e.,
X_{1}\oplus X_{2},X_{3}\oplus X_{4},\cdots,X_{2^{pow(\ell)+1}-1}\oplus X_{2^{pow(\ell)+1}}
.

•

Using the same approach in iteration-1 recursively, and iteratively create tree nodes. After
pow(\ell)+1
such rounds we have built a tree structure whose root node containing
\oplus^{2^{pow(\ell)+1}}_{i=1}X_{i}
and an oracle for backtracking from
\oplus^{2^{pow(\ell)+1}}_{i=1}X_{i}
to
(\oplus^{2^{pow(\ell)}}_{i=1}X_{i})\dot{\cup}(\oplus^{2^{pow(\ell)+1}}_{i=2^{pow(\ell)}+1}X_{i})
.

Observe that the total processing time at each iteration is
{\mathcal{O}}(\sigma\log\sigma)
, since there are
pow(\ell)+1={\mathcal{O}}(\log\ell)
iterations, thus the overall processing time to build this tree is
{\mathcal{O}}(\sigma\log\sigma\log\ell)
.

Note that we have obtained
\oplus^{2^{pow(\ell)+1}}_{i=1}X_{i}
, which is contained in the root node. It remains to show that we have designed an
{\mathcal{O}}(\sigma\log\sigma\log\ell)
-time oracle for backtracking from
\oplus^{2^{pow(\ell)+1}}_{i=1}X_{i}
to
\dot{\cup}^{\ell}_{i=1}X_{i}
. The oracle works as follows: given any
c\in\oplus^{2^{pow(\ell)+1}}_{i=1}X_{i}
, through backtracking recursively from the root node to leaf nodes, a simple calculation shows that in total
{\mathcal{O}}(\sigma\log\sigma\log\ell)
-processing time, one can determine
(c_{1},c_{2},\cdots,c_{\ell})
such that
c=\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}c_{i}
, where
c_{i}\in X_{i}\cup\{0\}
for every
i=1,2,\cdots,\ell,\cdots,2^{pow(\ell)+1}
. ∎∎

Sometimes we only care about computing
\omega
-capped sumset, i.e.,
(\oplus^{\ell}_{i=1}X_{i})\cap[0,\omega]
, and hope to design a customized algorithm with running time decreases as
\omega
decreases. Towards this, we develop the following Lemma
3
.

Lemma 3
.

Given sets
X_{1},X_{2},\cdots,X_{\ell}\subset\mathbb{N}
. For any
\omega\in\mathbb{N}
, in
{\mathcal{O}}(\ell\omega\log\omega)
processing time, we can compute

(\oplus^{\ell}_{i=1}X_{i})\cap[0,\omega]
and meanwhile build an
{\mathcal{O}}(\ell\omega\log\omega)
-time oracle for backtracking from
(\oplus^{\ell}_{i=1}X_{i})\cap[0,\omega]
to
\dot{\cup}^{\ell}_{i=1}X_{i}
.

Proof.

Given any sets
A,B\subset\mathbb{N}
, note that
(A\oplus B)\cap[0,\omega]=\left((A\cap[0,\omega])\oplus(B\cap[0,\omega])\right)\cap[0,\omega]
. Observation
1
guarantees that in
{\mathcal{O}}(\omega\log\omega)
processing time, we can compute
A\oplus B\cap[0,\omega]
, and meanwhile build an
{\mathcal{O}}(\omega\log\omega)
-time oracle for backtracking from
(A\oplus B)\cap[0,\omega]
to
A\dot{\cup}B
.

We design an iterative approach to compute
(\oplus^{\ell}_{i=1}X_{i})\cap[0,\omega]
and build an oracle for backtracking.

Define
X_{j}:=\{0\}
for
j=\ell+1,\ell+2,\cdots,2^{pow(\ell)+1}
. It holds that
\oplus^{\ell}_{i=1}X_{i}=\oplus^{2^{pow(\ell)+1}}_{i=1}X_{i}
. We build a tree structure of
{\mathcal{O}}(pow(\ell))
layers as follows:

•

At the beginning, we create
2^{pow(\ell)+1}
leaf nodes and let the
i
-th leaf node contain
X_{i}
.

•

At iteration-1, we use Observation
1
to compute
pairwise
\left(X_{2j_{1}-1}\oplus X_{2j_{1}}\right)\cap[0,\omega]
and derive an
{\mathcal{O}}(\omega\log\omega)
-time oracle for backtracking from
\left(X_{2j_{1}-1}\oplus X_{2j_{1}}\right)\cap[0,\omega]
to
X_{2j_{1}-1}\dot{\cup}X_{2j_{1}}
, where
j_{1}=1,2,\cdots,2^{pow(\ell)}
. For two nodes containing
X_{2j_{1}-1}
and
X_{2j_{1}}
separately, we create a parent node of these two nodes, and let the parent node contain
\left(X_{2j_{1}-1}\oplus X_{2j_{1}}\right)\cap[0,\omega]
and the oracle for backtracking from
\left(X_{2j_{1}-1}\oplus X_{2j_{1}}\right)\cap[0,\omega]
to
X_{2j_{1}-1}\dot{\cup}X_{2j_{1}}
. The processing time for one pair
\left(X_{2j_{1}-1}\oplus X_{2j_{1}}\right)\cap[0,\omega]
is
{\mathcal{O}}(\omega\log\omega)
, thus the total
processing time at iteration-1 is
{\mathcal{O}}(2^{pow(\ell)}\omega\log\omega)
. We then obtain a reduced instance with size of
2^{pow(\ell)}
, i.e.,
\left(X_{1}\oplus X_{2}\right)\cap[0,\omega],\left(X_{3}\oplus X_{4}\right)\cap[0,\omega],\cdots,\left(X_{2^{pow(\ell)+1}-1}\oplus X_{2^{pow(\ell)+1}}\right)\cap[0,\omega]
.

•

Using the same approach in iteration-1 recursively, and iteratively create tree nodes. After
pow(\ell)+1
such rounds we have built a tree structure whose root node containing
\oplus^{2^{pow(\ell)+1}}_{i=1}X_{i}\cap[0,\omega]
and an
{\mathcal{O}}(\omega\log\omega)
-time oracle for backtracking from
\oplus^{2^{pow(\ell)+1}}_{i=1}X_{i}\cap[0,\omega]
to
(\oplus^{2^{pow(\ell)}}_{i=1}X_{i})\dot{\cup}(\oplus^{2^{pow(\ell)+1}}_{i=2^{pow(\ell)}+1}X_{i})
.

Observe that the total processing time at iteration-
i
is
{\mathcal{O}}(2^{pow(\ell)+1-i}\omega\log\omega)
, since there are
pow(\ell)+1
iterations, thus the overall processing time to build this tree is
{\mathcal{O}}((\sum^{pow(\ell)+1}_{i=1}2^{pow(\ell)+1-i})\omega\log\omega)={\mathcal{O}}(\ell\omega\log\omega)
.

Note that we have obtained
\oplus^{2^{pow(\ell)+1}}_{i=1}X_{i}\cap[0,\omega]
, which is contained in the root node. It remains to show that we have designed an

{\mathcal{O}}(\ell\omega\log\omega)
-time oracle for backtracking from
\oplus^{2^{pow(\ell)+1}}_{i=1}X_{i}\cap[0,\omega]
to
\dot{\cup}^{\ell}_{i=1}X_{i}
. The oracle works as follows: given any
c\in\oplus^{2^{pow(\ell)+1}}_{i=1}X_{i}\cap[0,\omega]
, through backtracking recursively from the root node to leaf nodes, a simple calculation shows that in total
{\mathcal{O}}(\ell\omega\log\omega)
-processing time, one can determine
(c_{1},c_{2},\cdots,c_{\ell})
such that
c=\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}c_{i}
, where
c_{i}\in X_{i}\cup\{0\}
for every
i=1,2,\cdots,\ell
. ∎∎

2.2
Approximation Algorithms for Computing Sumset.

2.2.1
Approximating Sumset.

In this section, we aim to prove the following lemma, which implies an approxmation algorithm for computing sumset.

Lemma 4
.

Given multisets
C_{1},C_{2},\cdots,C_{\ell}\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, in
{\mathcal{O}}(\sum^{\ell}_{i=1}|C_{i}|+\frac{\ell}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
processing time, we can

(i).

Compute an
{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\log\ell+\epsilon^{2}\ell)
-approximate set with cardinality of
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
for
C_{1}\oplus C_{2}\oplus\cdots\oplus C_{\ell}
;

(ii).

Meanwhile build an
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{\ell}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
-time oracle for backtracking from this approximate set to
\dot{\cup}^{\ell}_{i=1}C_{i}
.

Before proving Lemma
4
, we first present the following observations.

Observation 2
.

Given multisets
B_{1},B_{2}\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, in
{\mathcal{O}}(|B_{1}|+|B_{2}|+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
processing time, we can compute an
{\epsilon}
-approximate set with cardinality of
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
for
B_{1}\oplus B_{2}
, and meanwhile build an
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
-time oracle for backtracking from this approximate set to
B_{1}\dot{\cup}B_{2}
.

Proof.

Let
\sigma:=B^{max}_{1}+B^{max}_{2}
and we define
\tilde{B}_{i}=set\left\{\left\lfloor\frac{x}{{(\epsilon\sigma)}/{2}}\right\rfloor:x\in B_{i}\right\}
for
i=1,2
. We first construct array
L_{i}
with a length of
\left\lfloor\frac{2}{\epsilon}\right\rfloor
for
i=1,2
.
Then we store
\tilde{B}_{i}(i=1,2)
in array
L_{i}
such that (1).
L_{i}[0]=1
; (2).
L_{i}[j]=1
if
\tilde{B}_{i}
contains
j
; (3).
L_{i}[j]=0
otherwise. In the meantime, we build auxiliary array
L^{\prime}_{i}
with a length of
\left\lfloor\frac{2}{\epsilon}\right\rfloor
for
i=1,2
.
Then we store
{B}_{i}(i=1,2)
in array
L^{\prime}_{i}
such that (1).
L^{\prime}_{i}=\{0\}
; (2).
L^{\prime}_{i}[j]=\{x\in B_{i}\cup\{0\}:\left\lfloor\frac{x}{{(\epsilon\sigma)}/{2}}\right\rfloor=j\}
if
\tilde{B}_{i}
contains
j
; (3).
L^{\prime}_{i}[j]=\emptyset
otherwise.

Perform FFT on
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
, we can obtain
\tilde{B}_{1}\oplus\tilde{B}_{2}
. Let
C:=\frac{\epsilon\sigma}{2}(\tilde{B}_{1}\oplus\tilde{B}_{2})
. We first show that
C
is an
\epsilon
-approximate set of
B_{1}\oplus B_{2}
. It suffices to observe the followings:

•

0\leq C^{\min}\leq C^{\max}\leq B^{\max}_{1}+B^{\max}_{2}
, thus
C\subset[0,(1+\epsilon)(B_{1}\oplus B_{2})^{\max}]
.

•

Consider any
c=c_{1}+c_{2}\in C
, where
c_{i}\in\frac{\epsilon\sigma}{2}(\tilde{B}_{i}\cup\{0\})
for
i=1,2
. For each
c_{i}
, there exists
c^{\prime}_{i}\in B_{i}\cup\{0\}
satisfying
\left\lfloor\frac{c^{\prime}_{i}}{{(\epsilon\sigma)}/{2}}\right\rfloor=\frac{c_{i}}{{(\epsilon\sigma)}/{2}}
. Let
c^{\prime}=c^{\prime}_{1}+c^{\prime}_{2}
, it follows that
c^{\prime}\in B_{1}\oplus B_{2}
and

|c-c^{\prime}|=\left|(c_{1}+c_{2})-(c^{\prime}_{1}+c^{\prime}_{2})\right|\leq\frac{\epsilon\sigma}{2}\left(\left|\frac{c_{1}}{{(\epsilon\sigma)}/{2}}-\frac{c^{\prime}_{1}}{{(\epsilon\sigma)}/{2}}\right|+\left|\frac{c_{2}}{{(\epsilon\sigma)}/{2}}-\frac{c^{\prime}_{2}}{{(\epsilon\sigma)}/{2}}\right|\right)\leq\epsilon\sigma.

•

Consider any
b=b_{1}+b_{2}\in B_{1}\oplus B_{2}
, where
b_{i}\in B_{i}\cup\{0\}
for
i=1,2
. For each
b_{i}
, it holds that
\left\lfloor\frac{b_{i}}{{(\epsilon\sigma)}/{2}}\right\rfloor\in\tilde{B}_{i}\cup\{0\}
. Let
b^{\prime}_{i}=\frac{\epsilon\sigma}{2}\left\lfloor\frac{b_{i}}{{(\epsilon\sigma)}/{2}}\right\rfloor(i=1,2)
and let
b^{\prime}=b^{\prime}_{1}+b^{\prime}_{2}
, it follows that
b^{\prime}\in\frac{(\epsilon\sigma)}{2}(\tilde{B}_{1}\oplus\tilde{B}_{2})
and

|b^{\prime}-b|=|(b_{1}+b_{2})-(b^{\prime}_{1}+b^{\prime}_{2})|\leq\frac{\epsilon\sigma}{2}\left(\left|\left\lfloor\frac{b_{1}}{{(\epsilon\sigma)}/{2}}\right\rfloor-\frac{b_{1}}{{(\epsilon\sigma)}/{2}}\right|+\left|\left\lfloor\frac{b_{2}}{{(\epsilon\sigma)}/{2}}\right\rfloor-\frac{b_{2}}{{(\epsilon\sigma)}/{2}}\right|\right)\leq{\epsilon}\sigma.

Now we show that an
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
-time oracle for backtracking from
C
to
B_{1}\dot{\cup}B_{2}
has been built and works as follows: given any
c\in C
, consider every
b
satisfying
L_{1}[b]=1
, i.e.,
b\in\tilde{B}_{1}\cup\{0\}
. If
L_{2}[\frac{2c}{\epsilon\sigma}-b]=1
, i.e.,
\frac{2c}{\epsilon\sigma}-b\in\tilde{B}_{2}\cup\{0\}
, choose any
b_{1}\in L^{\prime}_{1}[b]
and any
b_{2}\in L^{\prime}_{2}[\frac{2c}{\epsilon\sigma}-b]
, it follows that
b_{1}+b_{2}\in B_{1}\oplus B_{2}
and

|c-(b_{1}+b_{2})|\leq\frac{\epsilon\sigma}{2}\left(\left|b-\frac{2b_{1}}{\epsilon\sigma}\right|+\left|(\frac{2c}{\epsilon\sigma}-b)-\frac{2b_{2}}{\epsilon\sigma}\right|\right)\leq\epsilon\sigma
. It is easy to see that the time to determine
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
is
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
.

It remains to prove that the total processing time is
{\mathcal{O}}(|B_{1}|+|B_{2}|+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
. One can easily prove that the time to build
L_{i}(i=1,2)
and
L^{\prime}_{i}(i=1,2)
is
{\mathcal{O}}(|B_{1}|+|B_{2}|)
. Recall that the length of
L_{i}(i=1,2)
is
\left\lfloor\frac{2}{\epsilon}\right\rfloor
, thus the FFT to compute
\tilde{B}_{1}\oplus\tilde{B}_{2}
runs in
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
time. Note that
\tilde{B}_{1}\oplus\tilde{B}_{2}
is a set and
\tilde{B}_{1}\oplus\tilde{B}_{2}\subset[0,\frac{4}{\epsilon}]
, thus we can obtain
C
in
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
time. To summarize, the total processing time is
{\mathcal{O}}(|B_{1}|+|B_{2}|+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
. ∎∎

Observation 3
.

Given multisets
A_{1},A_{2}\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, let
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
be
f(\epsilon)
-approximate sets of
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
, respectively, where
f
is an arbitrary computable function. Then any
\epsilon
-approximate set of
B_{1}\oplus B_{2}
is an
(\epsilon+(1+\epsilon)f(\epsilon))
-approximate set of
A_{1}\oplus A_{2}
.

Proof.

Given multisets
A_{1},A_{2}\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, let
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
be
f(\epsilon)
-approximate sets of
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
, respectively, where
f
is an arbitrary computable function. We have
B^{\max}_{i}\leq(1+f(\epsilon))A^{\max}_{i}
for
i=1,2
. Denote by
C
an
\epsilon
-approximate set of
B_{1}\oplus B_{2}
. It suffices to observe the followings:

•

C^{max}\leq(1+\epsilon)(B^{max}_{1}+B^{max}_{2})\leq(1+\epsilon)(1+f(\epsilon))(A^{max}_{1}+A^{max}_{2})=(1+\epsilon+(1+\epsilon)f(\epsilon))(A^{max}_{1}+A^{max}_{2})
;

•

Consider any
c\in C
, since
C
is an
\epsilon
-approximate set of
B_{1}\oplus B_{2}
, there exists
c^{\prime}=c^{\prime}_{1}+c^{\prime}_{2}\in B_{1}\oplus B_{2}
where
c^{\prime}_{i}\in B_{i}\cup\{0\}
, such that
|c-c^{\prime}|\leq\epsilon(B^{max}_{1}+B^{max}_{2})\leq\epsilon(1+f(\epsilon))(A^{max}_{1}+A^{max}_{2})
. For each
c^{\prime}_{i}
,
since
B_{i}
is an
f(\epsilon)
-approximate set of
A_{i}
, there exists
c^{\prime\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}\cup\{0\}
such that
|c^{\prime\prime}_{i}-c^{\prime}_{i}|\leq f(\epsilon)A^{max}_{i}
. Let
c^{\prime\prime}=c^{\prime\prime}_{1}+c^{\prime\prime}_{2}
, it follows that
c^{\prime\prime}\in A_{1}\oplus A_{2}
and
|c^{\prime\prime}-c^{\prime}|\leq f(\epsilon)(A^{max}_{1}+A^{max}_{2})
. Then we have

|c^{\prime\prime}-c|\leq|c^{\prime\prime}-c^{\prime}|+|c^{\prime}-c|\leq(\epsilon+(1+\epsilon)f(\epsilon))(A^{max}_{1}+A^{max}_{2}).

•

Consider any sum
a=a_{1}+a_{2}\in A_{1}\oplus A_{2}
where
a_{i}\in A_{i}\cup\{0\}
. For each
a_{i}
, since
B_{i}
is an
f(\epsilon)
-approximate set of
A_{i}
, there exists
a^{\prime}_{i}\in B_{i}\cup\{0\}
such that
|a_{i}-a^{\prime}_{i}|\leq f(\epsilon)A^{max}_{i}
, it follows that
|(a^{\prime}_{1}+a^{\prime}_{2})-a|\leq f(\epsilon)(A^{max}_{1}+A^{max}_{2})
. Let
a^{\prime}=a^{\prime}_{1}+a^{\prime}_{2}
, note that
a^{\prime}\in B_{1}\oplus B_{2}
, since
C
is an
\epsilon
-approximate set of
B_{1}\oplus B_{2}
, thus there exists
a^{\prime\prime}\in C
such that
|a^{\prime\prime}-a^{\prime}|\leq\epsilon(B^{max}_{1}+B^{max}_{2})\leq\epsilon(1+f(\epsilon))(A^{max}_{1}+A^{max}_{2})
. Then we have

|a^{\prime\prime}-a|\leq|a^{\prime\prime}-a^{\prime}|+|a^{\prime}-a|\leq(\epsilon+(1+\epsilon)f(\epsilon))(A^{max}_{1}+A^{max}_{2}).

∎∎

Now we are ready to prove Lemma
4
.

Proof of Lemma
4
.

Given multisets
C_{1},C_{2},\cdots,C_{\ell}\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. In the following, we design an iterative approach to compute an approximate set for
\oplus^{\ell}_{i=1}C_{i}
and build an oracle for backtracking.

Define
C_{j}:=\{0\}
for
j=\ell+1,\ell+2,2^{pow(\ell)+1}
. It holds that
\oplus^{\ell}_{i=1}C_{i}=\oplus^{2^{pow(\ell)+1}}_{i=1}C_{i}
. We build a tree structure of
{\mathcal{O}}(pow(\ell))
layers as follows:

•

At the beginning, we create
2^{pow(\ell)+1}
leaf nodes and let the
i
-th leaf node contain
C_{i}
.

•

At iteration-1, for each pair
C_{2j_{1}-1}
and
C_{2j_{1}}
, where
j_{1}=1,2,\cdots,2^{pow(\ell)}
, we use Observation
2
to compute an
\epsilon
-approximate set with cardinality of
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
for
C_{2j_{1}-1}\oplus C_{2j_{1}}
and derive an
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
-time oracle for backtracking from this approximate set to
C_{2j_{1}-1}\dot{\cup}C_{2j_{1}}
. Denote by
U^{1}_{j_{1}}
this approximate set. For two nodes containing
C_{2j_{1}-1}
and
C_{2j_{1}}
separately, we create a parent node of these two nodes, and let the parent node contain
U^{1}_{j_{1}}
and the oracle for backtracking from
U^{1}_{j_{1}}
to
C_{2j_{1}-1}\dot{\cup}C_{2j_{1}}
.
The processing time for one pair
C_{2j_{1}-1}
and
C_{2j_{1}}
is
{\mathcal{O}}(|C_{2j_{1}-1}|+|C_{2j_{1}}|+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
by Observation
2
, thus the total processing time at iteration-1 is
{\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}|C_{i}|+2^{pow(\ell)}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
. A reduced instance with size of
2^{pow(\ell)}
, i.e.,
U^{1}_{1},U^{1}_{2},\cdots,U^{1}_{2^{pow(\ell)}}
, is obtained.

•

Before proceeding to iteration
h
where
h\geq 2
, we assume the following things have been done:

–

Let
U^{0}_{j_{0}}
denote
C_{j_{0}}
, where
j_{0}=1,2,\cdots,2^{pow(\ell)+1}
.

We have obtained
\{U^{k}_{j_{k}}|j_{k}=1,2,\cdots,2^{pow(\ell)+1-k}\text{\ and\ }k=1,2,3,\cdots,h-1\}
, where
U^{k}_{j_{k}}
is an
\epsilon
-approximate set with cardinality of
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
for
U^{k-1}_{2j_{k}-1}\oplus U^{k-1}_{2j_{k}}
.

–

For each
U^{k}_{j_{k}}
, where
j_{k}=1,2,\cdots,2^{pow(\ell)+1-k}\text{\ and\ }k=1,2,3,\cdots,h-1
, we have built an
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
-time oracle for backtracking from
U^{k}_{j_{k}}
to
U^{k-1}_{2j_{k}-1}\dot{\cup}U^{k-1}_{2j_{k}}
. Moreover, a node is created for containing
U^{k}_{j_{k}}
and the oracle for backtracking from
U^{k}_{j_{k}}
to
U^{k-1}_{2j_{k}-1}\dot{\cup}U^{k-1}_{2j_{k}}
.

Now we start iteration-
h
. For each pair
U^{h-1}_{2j_{h}-1}
and
U^{h-1}_{2j_{h}}
, where
j_{h}=1,2,\cdots,2^{pow(\ell)+1-h}
, we use Observation
2
to compute an
\epsilon
-approximate set with cardinality of
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
for
U^{h-1}_{2j_{h}-1}\oplus U^{h-1}_{2j_{h}}
and derive an
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
-time oracle for backtracking from this approximate set to
U^{h-1}_{2j_{h}-1}\dot{\cup}U^{h-1}_{2j_{h}}
. Denote by
U^{h}_{j_{h}}
this approximate set. For two nodes containing
U^{h-1}_{2j_{h}-1}
and
U^{h-1}_{2j_{h}}
separately, we create a parent node of these two nodes, and let the parent node contain
U^{h}_{j_{h}}
and the oracle for backtracking from
U^{h}_{j_{h}}
to
U^{h-1}_{2j_{h}-1}\dot{\cup}U^{h-1}_{2j_{h}}
. Notice that

|U^{h-1}_{2j_{h}-1}|={\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
and
|U^{h-1}_{2j_{h}}|={\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
for every
j_{h}=1,2,\cdots,2^{pow(\ell)+1-h}
, then the processing time for one pair
U^{h-1}_{2j_{h}-1}
and
U^{h-1}_{2j_{h}}
is
{\mathcal{O}}(|U^{h-1}_{2j_{h}-1}|+|U^{h-1}_{2j_{h}}|+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})={\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
by Observation
2
, thus the total processing time at iteration-
h
is
{\mathcal{O}}(2^{pow(\ell)+1-h}\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
. A reduced instance with size of
2^{pow(\ell)+1-h}
, i.e.,
U^{h}_{1},U^{h}_{2},\cdots,U^{h}_{2^{pow(\ell)+1-h}}
, is obtained.

•

Using the same approach in iteration-
h
recursively, and iteratively create tree nodes. After
pow(\ell)+1
such rounds, we stop and have built a tree structure whose root node contains (i).
U^{pow(\ell)+1}_{1}
, which is an
\epsilon
-approximate set with cardinality of
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
for
U^{pow(\ell)}_{1}\oplus U^{pow(\ell)}_{2}
; (ii). an
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
-time oracle for backtracking from
U^{pow(\ell)+1}_{1}
to
U^{pow(\ell)}_{1}\dot{\cup}U^{pow(\ell)}_{2}
.

To summarize, the total processing time is

{\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}|C_{i}|+(2^{1}+2^{2}+\cdots+2^{pow(\ell)})\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})={\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}|C_{i}|+\frac{\ell}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}).

Consider the functions in
\{f^{h}(\epsilon)\ |\ h=1,2,\cdots,pow(\ell)+1\}
, whch are defined by the following recurrence relation:
f^{1}(\epsilon)=\epsilon
and

f^{h}(\epsilon)=\epsilon+(1+\epsilon)f^{h-1}(\epsilon)\text{ \ for \ }2\leq h\leq pow(\ell)+1
. According to Observation
3
, given integer
1\leq k\leq pow(\ell)
,
if
U^{k}_{j_{k}}
is an
f^{k}(\epsilon)
-approximate set of
\oplus^{2^{k}}_{t=1}C_{2^{k}(j_{k}-1)+t}
for every
j_{k}=1,2,\cdots,2^{pow(\ell)+1-k}
, then
U^{k+1}_{j_{k+1}}
is an
(\epsilon+(1+\epsilon)f^{k}(\epsilon))
-approximate set of
\oplus^{2^{k+1}}_{t=1}C_{2^{k+1}(j_{k+1}-1)+t}
, where
j_{k+1}=1,2,\cdots,2^{pow(\ell)-k}
. Recall that
U^{1}_{j_{1}}
is an
f^{1}(\epsilon)
-approximate set of
\oplus^{2}_{t=1}C_{2(j_{1}-1)+t}
for every
j_{1}=1,2,\cdots,2^{pow(\ell)}
, it can be proved by recursion that
U^{h}_{j_{h}}
is an
f^{h}(\epsilon)
-approximate set of
\oplus^{2^{h}}_{t=1}C_{2^{h}(j_{h}-1)+t}
, where
j_{h}=1,2,\cdots,2^{pow(\ell)+1-h}
and
h=1,2,\cdots,pow(\ell)+1
. In particular,
U^{pow(\ell)+1}_{1}
is an
f^{pow(\ell)+1}(\epsilon)
-approximate set of
\oplus^{2^{pow(\ell)+1}}_{i=1}C_{i}
and a simple calculation shows that
f^{pow(\ell)+1}(\epsilon)={\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\log\ell+\epsilon^{2}\ell)
.

With the help of this tree structure, an
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{\ell}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
-time oracle for backtracking from
U^{pow(\ell)+1}_{1}
to
\dot{\cup}^{\ell}_{i=1}C_{i}
is derived and works as follows:

•

For any
c\in U^{pow(\ell)+1}_{1}
, we backtrace from the root of this tree. Note that root node contains an
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
-time oracle for backtracking from
U^{pow(\ell)+1}_{1}
to
U^{pow(\ell)}_{1}\dot{\cup}U^{pow(\ell)}_{2}
.
Thus in
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
processing time, we can determine

y^{(pow(\ell);1)}\in U^{pow(\ell)}_{1}\cup\{0\}
and
y^{(pow(\ell);2)}\in U^{pow(\ell)}_{2}\cup\{0\}
such that

|c-(y^{(pow(\ell);1)}+y^{(pow(\ell);1)})|\leq\epsilon(U^{pow(\ell)}_{1}+U^{pow(\ell)}_{2})^{max}\leq\epsilon(1+f^{pow(\ell)}(\epsilon))\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}C^{max}_{i}.

•

Backtrace recursively.

Given
1\leq h\leq pow(\ell)
, assume that we have determined
y^{(h;j_{h})}\in U^{h}_{j_{h}}\cup\{0\}
for every
j_{h}=1,2,3,\cdots,2^{pow(\ell)+1-h}
, such that

|c-\Sigma^{2^{pow(\ell)+1-h}}_{j_{h}=1}y^{(h;j_{h})}|\leq\epsilon\Sigma^{pow(\ell)}_{k=h}(1+f^{k}(\epsilon))\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}C^{max}_{i}.

For each
y^{(h;j_{h})}\in U^{h}_{j_{h}}\cup\{0\}
, if
y^{(h;j_{h})}=0
, then we let
y^{(h-1;2j_{h}-1)}=0
and
y^{(h-1;2j_{h})}=0
. If
y^{(h;j_{h})}\in U^{h}_{j_{h}}
, note that the node containing
U^{h}_{j_{h}}
also contains an
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
-time oracle for backtracking from
U^{h}_{j_{h}}
to
U^{h-1}_{2j_{h}-1}\dot{\cup}U^{h-1}_{2j_{h}}
. Thus
in
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
processing time, we can determine
y^{(h-1;2j_{h}-1)}\in U^{h-1}_{2j_{h}-1}\cup\{0\}
and
y^{(h-1;2j_{h})}\in U^{h-1}_{2j_{h}}\cup\{0\}
such that

|y^{(h;j_{h})}-(y^{(h-1;2j_{h}-1)}+y^{(h-1;2j_{h})})|\leq\epsilon((U^{h-1}_{2j_{h}-1})^{max}+(U^{h-1}_{2j_{h}})^{max}).

Then in
{\mathcal{O}}(2^{pow(\ell)+1-h}\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
processing time, we can determine
y^{(h-1;j_{h-1})}\in U^{h-1}_{j_{h-1}}\cup\{0\}
for every
j_{h-1}=1,2,3,\cdots,2^{pow(\ell)+2-h}
, such that

|\Sigma^{2^{pow(\ell)}+1-h}_{j_{h}=1}y^{(h;j_{h})}-\Sigma^{2^{pow(\ell)}+2-h}_{j_{h-1}=1}y^{(h-1;j_{h-1})}|\leq\epsilon\Sigma^{2^{pow(\ell)+2-h}}_{j_{h-1}=1}(U^{h-1}_{j_{h-1}})^{max}\leq\epsilon(1+f^{h-1}(\epsilon))\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}C^{max}_{i}.

It follows that

|c-\Sigma^{2^{pow(\ell)+2-h}}_{j_{h-1}=1}(y^{(h-1;j_{h-1})})|\leq\epsilon\Sigma^{pow(\ell)}_{k=h-1}(1+f^{k}(\epsilon))\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}C^{max}_{i}.

•

After
pow(\ell)
such rounds, we stop and have determined
c_{i}\in C_{i}\cup\{0\}
for every

i=1,2,3,\cdots,2^{pow(\ell)+1}
, such that

|c-\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}c_{i}|\leq\epsilon\left(1+\Sigma^{pow(\ell)}_{k=1}(1+f^{k}(\epsilon))\right)\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}C^{max}_{i}={\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\log\ell+\epsilon^{2}\ell)\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}C^{max}_{i}.

To summarize, the total processing time is
{\mathcal{O}}((1+2^{1}+2^{2}+\cdots+2^{pow(\ell)+1})\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})={\mathcal{O}}(\frac{\ell}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
.

In conclude, within
{\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}|C_{i}|+\frac{\ell}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
processing time, we will build a tree structure whose root node contains an
{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\log\ell+\epsilon^{2}\ell)
-approximate set with cardinality of
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
for
\oplus^{2^{pow(\ell)+1}}_{i=1}C_{i}
. Meanwhile, with the help of this tree structure, an
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{\ell}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
-time oracle for backtracking from this approximate set to
\dot{\cup}^{\ell}_{i=1}C_{i}
is derived. ∎∎

Lemma
4
implies the following corollary, which allows us to build the approximate set of
\dot{\cup}^{\ell}_{i=1}X_{i}
from the approximate set of each
X_{i}
.

Corollary 1
.

Given multisets
X_{1},X_{2},\cdots,X_{\ell}\subset\mathbb{R}
. Let
C_{i}
be an
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)
-approximate set of
S(X_{i})
with an additive error of
ERR_{i}
, where
i=1,2,\cdots,\ell
. Assume that for each
C_{i}
, there is a
T_{i}
-time oracle for backtracking from
C_{i}
to
X_{i}
. Then in
{\mathcal{O}}(\sum^{\ell}_{i=1}|C_{i}|+\frac{\ell}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
processing time, within an additive error of
(1+{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\log\ell+\epsilon^{2}\ell))\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}ERR_{i}
, we can

(i).

Compute an
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\log\ell+\epsilon^{2}\ell)
-approximate set with cardinality of
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
for
S(\dot{\cup}^{\ell}_{i=1}X_{i})
;

(ii).

Meanwhile build an
{\mathcal{O}}(\sum^{\ell}_{i=1}T_{i}+\frac{\ell}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
-time oracle for backtracking from this approximate set to
\dot{\cup}^{\ell}_{i=1}X_{i}
.

Proof.

Given multisets
X_{1},X_{2},\cdots,X_{\ell}\subset\mathbb{R}
. Let
C_{i}
be an
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)
-approximate set of
S(X_{i})
with an additive error of
ERR_{i}
, where
i=1,2,\cdots,\ell
. Assume that for each
C_{i}
, there is a
T_{i}
-time oracle for backtracking from
C_{i}
to
X_{i}
. The approach to approximate
S(\dot{\cup}^{\ell}_{i=1}X_{i})
and build an oracle for backtracking is very similar to the one we have designed in the proof of Lemma
4
. The only difference is that when creating leaf nodes, we let the
i
-th node contain not only
C_{i}
but also the
T_{i}
-time oracle for backtracking from
C_{i}
to
X_{i}
. Then we use the above iterative approach to approximate
\oplus^{\ell}_{i=1}C_{i}
, in
{\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}|C_{i}|+\frac{\ell}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
processing time, we will build an augmented tree structure whose root node contains an
{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\log\ell+\epsilon^{2}\ell)
-approximate set with cardinality of
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
for
\oplus^{\ell}_{i=1}C_{i}
. Denote by
U
this approximate set.
Meanwhile, with the help of this tree structure, an
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{\ell}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
-time oracle for backtracking from
U
to
\dot{\cup}^{\ell}_{i=1}C_{i}
is derived.

We claim that within an additive error of
(1+{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\log\ell+\epsilon^{2}\ell))\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}ERR_{i}
,
U
is an
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\log\ell+\epsilon^{2}\ell)
-approximate set of
S(\dot{\cup}^{\ell}_{i=1}X_{i})
. Towards the claim, it is sufficient to observe the followings:

•

Since
U
is an
{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\log\ell+\epsilon^{2}\ell)
-approximate set of
\oplus^{\ell}_{i=1}C_{i}
, then
U^{\max}\leq(1+{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\log\ell+\epsilon^{2}\ell))\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}C^{\max}_{i}
. For each
S(X_{i})
, note that
C_{i}
is an
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)
-approximate set of
S(X_{i})
with an additive error of
ERR_{i}
, we have
C^{max}_{i}\leq(1+\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon))\Sigma(X_{i})+ERR_{i}
and

\displaystyle U^{\max}

\displaystyle\leq(1+{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\log\ell+\epsilon^{2}\ell))\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}C^{\max}_{i}

\displaystyle\leq(1+{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\log\ell+\epsilon^{2}\ell))((1+\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon))\sum^{\ell}_{i=1}\Sigma(X_{i})+\sum^{\ell}_{i=1}ERR_{i})

\displaystyle\leq(1+\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\log\ell+\epsilon^{2}\ell))\sum^{\ell}_{i=1}\Sigma(X_{i})+(1+{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\log\ell+\epsilon^{2}\ell))\sum^{\ell}_{i=1}ERR_{i}

•

Consider any
s\in S(\dot{\cup}^{\ell}_{i=1}X_{i})
. There exists
(s_{1},s_{2},\cdots,s_{\ell})
where
s_{i}\in S(X_{i})
for
i=1,2,\cdots,\ell
, such that
s=\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}s_{i}
. For each
S(X_{i})
, recall that
C_{i}
is an
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)
-approximate set of
S(X_{i})
with an additive error of
ERR_{i}
, then
C^{max}_{i}\leq(1+\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon))\Sigma(X_{i})+ERR_{i}
. Moreover, for each
s_{i}\in S(X_{i})
, there exits
s^{\prime}_{i}\in C_{i}
such that
|s^{\prime}_{i}-s_{i}|\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)\Sigma(X_{i})+ERR_{i}
, it follows that
|s-\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}s^{\prime}_{i}|\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)\sum^{\ell}_{i=1}\Sigma(X_{i})+\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}ERR_{i}.

Let
s^{\prime}=\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}s^{\prime}_{i}
. Note that
s^{\prime}\in\oplus^{\ell}_{i=1}C_{i}
and
U
is an
{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\log\ell+\epsilon^{2}\ell)
-approximate set of
\oplus^{\ell}_{i=1}C_{i}
, thus there exists
s^{\prime\prime}\in U
such that

|s^{\prime\prime}-s^{\prime}|\leq{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\log\ell+\epsilon^{2}\ell)\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}C^{max}_{i}\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\log\ell+\epsilon^{2}\ell)\sum^{\ell}_{i=1}\Sigma(X_{i})+{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\log\ell+\epsilon^{2}\ell)\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}ERR_{i}.

Furthermore, we have

|s^{\prime\prime}-s|\leq|s^{\prime\prime}-s^{\prime}|+|s^{\prime}-s|\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\log\ell+\epsilon^{2}\ell)\sum^{\ell}_{i=1}\Sigma(X_{i})+(1+{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\log\ell+\epsilon^{2}\ell))\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}ERR_{i}.

•

Consider any
y\in U
. Since
U
is an
{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\log\ell+\epsilon^{2}\ell)
-approximate set of
\oplus^{\ell}_{i=1}C_{i}
, there exists
(c_{1},c_{2},\cdots,c_{\ell})
where
c_{i}\in C_{i}\cup\{0\}
for
i=1,2,\cdots
, such that
|y-\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}c_{i}|\leq{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\log\ell+\epsilon^{2}\ell)\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}C^{max}_{i}\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\log\ell+\epsilon^{2}\ell)\sum^{\ell}_{i=1}\Sigma(X_{i})+{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\log\ell+\epsilon^{2}\ell)\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}ERR_{i}
. For each
c_{i}\in C_{i}\cup\{0\}
, if
c_{i}=0
, let
X^{\prime}_{i}=\emptyset
, apparently
X^{\prime}_{i}\subset X_{i}
and
|c_{i}-\Sigma(X^{\prime}_{i})|\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)\Sigma(X_{i})+ERR_{i}
. Else if
c_{i}\in C_{i}
, note that
C_{i}
is an
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)
-approximate set of
S(X_{i})
with an additive error of
ERR_{i}
, there exists
X^{\prime}_{i}\subset X_{i}
such that
|c_{i}-\Sigma(X^{\prime}_{i})|\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)\Sigma(X_{i})+ERR_{i}
. Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle|y-\sum^{\ell}_{i=1}\Sigma(X^{\prime}_{i})|

\displaystyle\leq|y-\sum^{\ell}_{i=1}c_{i}|+|\sum^{\ell}_{i=1}c_{i}-\sum^{\ell}_{i=1}\Sigma(X^{\prime}_{i})|

\displaystyle\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\log\ell+\epsilon^{2}\ell)\sum^{\ell}_{i=1}\Sigma(X_{i})+{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\log\ell+\epsilon^{2}\ell)\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}ERR_{i}+\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}\Sigma(X_{i})+\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}ERR_{i}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\log\ell+\epsilon^{2}\ell)\sum^{\ell}_{i=1}\Sigma(X_{i})+(1+{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\log\ell+\epsilon^{2}\ell))\sum^{\ell}_{i=1}ERR_{i}.

It is easy to see that
\dot{\cup}^{\ell}_{i=1}X^{\prime}_{i}\subset\dot{\cup}^{\ell}_{i=1}X_{i}
and
\Sigma(\dot{\cup}^{\ell}_{i=1}X^{\prime}_{i})=\sum^{\ell}_{i=1}\Sigma(X^{\prime}_{i})\in S(\dot{\cup}^{\ell}_{i=1}X_{i})
.

In the following, we show that with the help of the above augmented tree structure, an
{\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}T_{i}+\frac{\ell}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
-time oracle for backtracking from
U
to
\dot{\cup}^{\ell}_{i=1}X_{i}
can be derived. The oracle works as follows. Given any
y\in U
, note that
U
is an
{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\log\ell+\epsilon^{2}\ell)
-approximate set of
\oplus^{\ell}_{i=1}C_{i}
and with the help of augmented tree structure, an
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{\ell}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
-time oracle for backtracking from
U
to
\dot{\cup}^{\ell}_{i=1}C_{i}
has been derived. Thus in
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{\ell}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
processing time, we can determine
c_{i}\in C_{i}\cup\{0\}
for every
i=1,2,\cdots,\ell
such that
|y-\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}c_{i}|\leq{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\log\ell+\epsilon^{2}\ell)\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}C^{max}_{i}\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\log\ell+\epsilon^{2}\ell)\sum^{\ell}_{i=1}\Sigma(X_{i})+{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\log\ell+\epsilon^{2}\ell)\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}ERR_{i}
. For each
c_{i}\in C_{i}\cup\{0\}
, if
c_{i}=0
, let
X^{\prime}_{i}=\emptyset
, apparently
X^{\prime}_{i}\subset X_{i}
and
|c_{i}-\Sigma(X^{\prime}_{i})|\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)\Sigma(X_{i})+ERR_{i}
. Else if
c_{i}\in C_{i}
, recall that the leaf node containing
C_{i}
also contains a
T_{i}
-time oracle for backtracking from
C_{i}
to
X_{i}
, thus in
T_{i}
processing time, we can determine
X^{\prime}_{i}\subset X_{i}
such that
|c_{i}-\Sigma(X^{\prime}_{i})|\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)\Sigma(X_{i})+ERR_{i}
. To summarize, with the help of augmented tree structure, in total
{\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}T_{i}+\frac{\ell}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
processing time, we can determine
X^{\prime}_{i}\subset X_{i}
for every
i=1,2,\cdots,\ell
such that

|y-\sum^{\ell}_{i=1}\Sigma(X^{\prime}_{i})|\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\log\ell+\epsilon^{2}\ell)\sum^{\ell}_{i=1}\Sigma(X_{i})+(1+{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\log\ell+\epsilon^{2}\ell))\sum^{\ell}_{i=1}ERR_{i}.

Note that
\dot{\cup}^{\ell}_{i=1}X^{\prime}_{i}\subset\dot{\cup}^{\ell}_{i=1}X_{i}
and
\Sigma(\dot{\cup}^{\ell}_{i=1}X^{\prime}_{i})=\sum^{\ell}_{i=1}\Sigma(X^{\prime}_{i})
, hence Corollary
1
is proved. ∎∎

2.2.2
Approximating Capped Sumset.

Sometimes, we only care about approximating
\omega
-capped sumset, e.g.,
(\oplus^{\ell}_{i=1}X_{i})\cap[0,\omega]
. Towards this, we develop the following Lemma
5
.

Lemma 5
.

Given multisets
C_{1},C_{2},\cdots,C_{\ell}\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and a parameter
\omega>0
.
In
{\mathcal{O}}(\sum^{\ell}_{i=1}|C_{i}|+\frac{\ell}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
processing time, we can

(i).

Compute a
({\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\ell+\epsilon^{2}\ell^{2}),\omega)
-approximate set with cardinality of
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
for
C_{1}\oplus C_{2}\oplus\cdots\oplus C_{\ell}
;

(ii).

Meanwhile build an
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{\ell}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
-time oracle for backtracking from this approximate set to
\dot{\cup}^{\ell}_{i=1}C_{i}
.

The proof idea of Lemma
5
is similar to the proof of Lemma
4
. We iteratively construct a tree structure such that the root node contains the desired approximate set of
\oplus^{\ell}_{i=1}C_{i}
and the corresponding backtracking oracle. When building the tree structure, the biggest difference is that in the proof of Lemma
4
, each layer-
h
node is obtained by computing the
\epsilon
-approximate set of the sumset of its two child nodes, while in the proof of Lemma
5
, each layer-
h
node is obtained by computing the
(\epsilon,(1+2f^{h}(\epsilon))\omega)
-approximate set of the sumset of its two child nodes, where
f^{h}(\epsilon)
’s are defined by the following recurrence relation:
f^{0}(\epsilon)=0
and
f^{t}(\epsilon)=\epsilon+2(1+\epsilon)f^{t-1}(\epsilon)\text{ \ for \ }1\leq t\leq h-1
.

Before proving Lemma
5
, we first present the following observations. In particular, Observation
4
provides an efficient algorithm for computing a
(\epsilon,(1+2f^{h}(\epsilon))\omega)
-approximate set of the sumset of given two multisets, and Observation
5
guarantees that the set in root node is actually the
({\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\ell+\epsilon^{2}\ell^{2}),\omega)
-approxiamte set of
\oplus^{\ell}_{i=1}C_{i}
.

Observation 4
.

Given multisets
B_{1},B_{2}\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and a parameter
\omega>0
. In
{\mathcal{O}}(|B_{1}|+|B_{2}|+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
processing time, we can compute an
(\epsilon,\omega)
-approximate set with cardinality of
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
for
B_{1}\oplus B_{2}
, while building an
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
-time oracle for backtracking from this approximate set to
B_{1}\dot{\cup}B_{2}
.

Proof.

Define
B^{\omega}_{i}=\{b\ |\ b\in B_{i}\text{ \ and \ }b\leq\omega\}
for
i=1,2
. Recall Observation
2
, in
{\mathcal{O}}(|B^{\omega}_{1}|+|B^{\omega}_{2}|+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
processing time, we can compute an
\frac{\epsilon}{2}
-approximate set with cardinality of
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
for
B^{\omega}_{1}\oplus B^{\omega}_{2}
, and meanwhile build an
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
-time oracle for backtracking from this approximate set to
B^{\omega}_{1}\dot{\cup}B^{\omega}_{2}
. Denote by
C
this approximate set. Let
C^{\omega}=C\cap[0,(1+\epsilon)\omega]
. We claim that
C^{\omega}
is an
(\epsilon,\omega)
-approximate set of
B_{1}\oplus B_{2}
. It is sufficient to observe the followings:

•

C^{\omega}=C\cap[0,(1+\epsilon)\omega]\subset[0,(1+\epsilon)\omega]
;

•

Consider any
c\in C^{\omega}
. Note that
C^{\omega}\subset C
and
C
is an
\frac{\epsilon}{2}
-approximate set of
B^{\omega}_{1}\oplus B^{\omega}_{2}
, thus there exist
c^{\prime}\in B^{\omega}_{1}\oplus B^{\omega}_{2}
such that
|c-c^{\prime}|\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2}(B^{\omega}_{1}\oplus B^{\omega}_{2})^{\max}\leq\epsilon\omega
.

•

Consider any
b\in(B_{1}\oplus B_{2})\cap[0,\omega]
. It is easy to see that
b\leq\omega
and
b\in B^{\omega}_{1}\oplus B^{\omega}_{2}
. By the fact that
C
is an
\frac{\epsilon}{2}
-approximate set of
B^{\omega}_{1}\oplus B^{\omega}_{2}
, there exists
b^{\prime}\in C
such that
|b-b^{\prime}|\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2}(B^{\omega}_{1}\oplus B^{\omega}_{2})^{\max}\leq\epsilon\omega
. It follows that
b^{\prime}\leq b+\epsilon\omega\leq(1+\epsilon)\omega
, thus
b^{\prime}\in C^{\omega}
.

Moreover, note that
C^{\omega}\subset C
and
B^{\omega}_{1}\dot{\cup}B^{\omega}_{2}\subset B_{1}\dot{\cup}B_{2}
, the
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
-time oracle built for backtracking from
C
to
B^{\omega}_{1}\dot{\cup}B^{\omega}_{2}
diretly yields an
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
-time oracle for backtracking from
C^{\omega}
to
B_{1}\dot{\cup}B_{2}
.

Note that the time to obtain
B^{\omega}_{1}
and
B^{\omega}_{2}
is
{\mathcal{O}}(|B_{1}|+|B_{2}|)
. Since
|C|={\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
, the time to obtain
C^{\omega}
from
C
is
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
. To summarize, within
{\mathcal{O}}(|B_{1}|+|B_{2}|+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
processing time, we can compute an
(\epsilon,\omega)
-approximate set with cardinality of
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
for
B_{1}\oplus B_{2}
, while building an
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
-time oracle for backtracking from this approximate set to
B_{1}\dot{\cup}B_{2}
.
∎∎

Observation 5
.

Given multisets
A_{1},A_{2}\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and a parameter
\omega>0
. Let
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
be
(f(\epsilon),\omega)
-approximate sets of
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
, respectively, where
f
is an arbitrary computable function. Then any
(\epsilon,(1+2f(\epsilon))\omega)
-approximate set of
B_{1}\oplus B_{2}
is an
(\epsilon+2(1+\epsilon)f(\epsilon),\omega)
-approximate set of
A_{1}\oplus A_{2}
.

Proof.

Given multisets
A_{1},A_{2}\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and a parameter
\omega>0
. Let
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
be
(f(\epsilon),\omega)
-approximate sets of
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
, respectively, where
f
is an arbitrary computable function. We have
B^{\max}_{i}\leq(1+f(\epsilon))\omega
for
i=1,2
. Denote by
C
an
(\epsilon,(1+2f(\epsilon))\omega)
-approximate set of
B_{1}\oplus B_{2}
. Towards Observation
5
, it is sufficient to observe the followings:

•

Since
C
is an
(\epsilon,(1+2f(\epsilon))\omega)
-approximate set of
B_{1}\oplus B_{2}
, we have
C\subset[0,(1+\epsilon)(1+2f(\epsilon))\omega]=[0,(1+\epsilon+2(1+\epsilon)f(\epsilon))\omega].

•

Consider any
c\in C
. Since
C
is an
(\epsilon,(1+2f(\epsilon))\omega)
-approximate set of
B_{1}\oplus B_{2}
, there exists
c^{\prime}=c^{\prime}_{1}+c^{\prime}_{2}\in B_{1}\oplus B_{2}
where
c^{\prime}_{i}\in B_{i}\cup\{0\}
, such that
|c-c^{\prime}|\leq\epsilon(1+2f(\epsilon))\omega
. Recall that
B_{i}(i=1,2)
is an
(f(\epsilon),\omega)
-approximate set of
A_{i}
, then for each
c^{\prime}_{i}
, there exists
c^{\prime\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}\cup\{0\}
such that
|c^{\prime\prime}_{i}-c^{\prime}_{i}|\leq f(\epsilon)\omega
. Let
c^{\prime\prime}=c^{\prime\prime}_{1}+c^{\prime\prime}_{2}
, it follows that
c^{\prime\prime}\in A_{1}\oplus A_{2}
and
|c^{\prime\prime}-c^{\prime}|=|(c^{\prime\prime}_{1}+c^{\prime\prime}_{2})-(c^{\prime}_{1}+c^{\prime}_{2})|\leq 2f(\epsilon)\omega
. Furthermore, we have
|c^{\prime\prime}-c|\leq|c^{\prime\prime}-c^{\prime}|+|c^{\prime}-c|\leq 2f(\epsilon)\omega+\epsilon(1+2f(\epsilon))\omega=(\epsilon+2(1+\epsilon)f(\epsilon))\omega.

•

Consider any
a\in(A_{1}\oplus A_{2})\cup[0,\omega]
. There exist
a_{1}\in(A_{1}\cup\{0\})\cap[0,\omega]
and
a_{2}\in(A_{2}\cup\{0\})\cap[0,\omega]
such that
a=a_{1}+a_{2}
. For each
a_{i}
, recall that
B_{i}
is an
(f(\epsilon),\omega)
-approximate set of
A_{i}
, there exists
a^{\prime}_{i}\in B_{i}\cup\{0\}
such that
|a_{i}-a^{\prime}_{i}|\leq f(\epsilon)\omega
, it follows that
|(a^{\prime}_{1}+a^{\prime}_{2})-a|\leq|a^{\prime}_{1}-a_{1}|+|a^{\prime}_{2}-a_{2}|\leq 2f(\epsilon)\omega.

Let
a^{\prime}=a^{\prime}_{1}+a^{\prime}_{2}
, it holds that

a^{\prime}=a^{\prime}_{1}+a^{\prime}_{2}\leq a+2f(\epsilon)\omega\leq(1+2f(\epsilon))\omega
, thus
a^{\prime}\in(B_{1}\oplus B_{2})\cap[0,(1+2f(\epsilon))\omega]
. Recall that
C
is an
(\epsilon,(1+2f(\epsilon))\omega)
-approximate set of
B_{1}\oplus B_{2}
, thus for
a^{\prime}
, there exists
a^{\prime\prime}\in C
such that
|a^{\prime\prime}-a^{\prime}|\leq\epsilon(1+2f(\epsilon))\omega
. To summarize, we have
|a^{\prime\prime}-a|\leq|a^{\prime\prime}-a^{\prime}|+|a^{\prime}-a|\leq\epsilon(1+2f(\epsilon))\omega+2f(\epsilon)\omega=(\epsilon+2(1+\epsilon)f(\epsilon))\omega.

∎∎

Now we are ready to prove Lemma
5
.

Proof of Lemma
5
.

Given multisets
C_{1},C_{2},\cdots,C_{\ell}\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and a parameter
\omega\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. In the following, we design an iterative approach to compute an approximate set for
\oplus^{\ell}_{i=1}C_{i}
and build an oracle for backtracking.

Define
C_{j}:=\{0\}
for
j=\ell+1,\ell+2,2^{pow(\ell)+1}
. It holds that
\oplus^{\ell}_{i=1}C_{i}=\oplus^{2^{pow(\ell)+1}}_{i=1}C_{i}
. We build a tree structure of
{\mathcal{O}}(pow(\ell))
layers as follows:

•

At the beginning, we create
2^{pow(\ell)+1}
leaf nodes and let the
i
-th node contain
C_{i}
. Let
f^{0}(\epsilon):=0
.

•

At iteration-1, for each pair
C_{2j_{1}-1}
and
C_{2j_{1}}
, where
j_{1}=1,2,\cdots,2^{pow(\ell)}
, we use Observation
4
to compute an
(\epsilon,\omega)
-approximate set with cardinality of
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
for
C_{2j_{1}-1}\oplus C_{2j_{1}}
and derive an
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
-time oracle for backtracking from this approximate set to
C_{2j_{1}-1}\dot{\cup}C_{2j_{1}}
. Denote by
U^{1}_{j_{1}}
this approximate set.
For two nodes containing
C_{2j_{1}-1}
and
C_{2j_{1}}
separately, we create a parent node of these two nodes, and let the parent node contain
U^{1}_{j_{1}}
and the oracle for backtracking from
U^{1}_{j_{1}}
to
C_{2j_{1}-1}\dot{\cup}C_{2j_{1}}
. The processing time for one pair
C_{2j_{1}-1}
and
C_{2j_{1}}
is
{\mathcal{O}}(|C_{2j_{1}-1}|+|C_{2j_{1}}|+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
by Observation
4
, thus the total processing time at iteration-1 is
{\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}|C_{i}|+2^{pow(\ell)}\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
.
A reduce instance with size of
2^{pow({\ell})}
, i.e.,
U^{1}_{1},U^{1}_{2},\cdots,U^{1}_{2^{pow(\ell)}}
, is obtained. Let
f^{1}(\epsilon):=\epsilon
.

•

Before proceeding to iteration
h
where
h\geq 2
, we assume the following things have been done:

–

Let
U^{0}_{j_{0}}
denote
C_{j_{0}}
, where
j_{0}=1,2,\cdots,2^{pow(\ell)+1}
.

We have obtained
\{U^{k}_{j_{k}}\ |\ j_{k}=1,2,\cdots,2^{pow(\ell)+1-k}\text{\ and\ }k=1,2,3,\cdots,h-1\}
, where
U^{k}_{j_{k}}
is an
(\epsilon,(1+2f^{(k-1)}(\epsilon))\omega)
-approximate set with cardinality of
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
for
U^{k-1}_{2j_{k}-1}\oplus U^{k-1}_{2j_{k}}
.

Functions in
\{f^{t}(\epsilon)\ |\ t=0,1,\cdots,h-1\}
are defined by the following recurrence relation:
f^{0}(\epsilon)=0
,
f^{1}(\epsilon)=\epsilon
and

f^{t}(\epsilon)=\epsilon+2(1+\epsilon)f^{t-1}(\epsilon)\text{ \ for \ }2\leq t\leq h-1.

–

For each
U^{k}_{j_{k}}
, where
j_{k}=1,2,\cdots,2^{pow(\ell)+1-k}\text{\ and \ }k=1,2,3\cdots,h-1
, we have built an
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
-time oracle for backtracking from
U^{k}_{j_{k}}
to
U^{k-1}_{2j_{k}-1}\dot{\cup}U^{k-1}_{2j_{k}}
. Moreover, a node is created for containing
U^{k}_{j_{k}}
and the oracle for backtracking from
U^{k}_{j_{k}}
to
U^{k-1}_{2j_{k}-1}\dot{\cup}U^{k-1}_{2j_{k}}
.

Now we start iteration-
h
. For each pair
U^{h-1}_{2j_{h}-1}
and
U^{h-1}_{2j_{h}}
, where
j_{h}=1,2,\cdots,2^{pow(\ell)+1-h}
, we use Observation
4
to compute an
(\epsilon,(1+2f^{h-1}(\epsilon))\omega)
-approximate set with cardinality of
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
for
U^{h-1}_{2j_{h}-1}\oplus U^{h-1}_{2j_{h}}
and build an
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
-time oracle for backtracking from
U^{h}_{j_{h}}
to
U^{h-1}_{2j_{h}-1}\dot{\cup}U^{h-1}_{2j_{h}}
. Denote by
U^{h}_{j_{h}}
this approximate set. For two nodes containing
U^{h-1}_{2j_{h}-1}
and
U^{h-1}_{2j_{h}}
separately, we create a parent node of these two nodes, and let the parent node contain
U^{h}_{j_{h}}
and the oracle for backtracking from
U^{h}_{j_{h}}
to
U^{h-1}_{2j_{h}-1}\dot{\cup}U^{h-1}_{2j_{h}}
. Notice that
|U^{h-1}_{2j_{h}-1}|={\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
and
|U^{h-1}_{2j_{h}}|={\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
for every
j_{h}=1,2,\cdots,2^{pow(\ell)+1-h}
, then
the processing time for one pair
U^{h-1}_{2j_{h}-1}
and
U^{h-1}_{2j_{h}}
is
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
by Observation
4
, thus the total processing time at iteration-
h
is
