{\mathcal{O}}(2^{pow(\ell)+1-h}\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
. A reduced instance with size of
2^{pow(\ell)+1-h}
, i.e.,
U^{h}_{1},U^{h}_{2},\cdots,U^{h}_{2^{pow(\ell)+1-h}}
, is obtained. Let
f^{h}(\epsilon):=\epsilon+2(1+\epsilon)f^{h-1}(\epsilon)
.

•

Using the same approach in iteration-
h
recursively, and iteratively create tree nodes. After
pow(\ell)+1
such rounds we stop and have built a tree structure whose root node contains (i).
U^{pow(\ell)+1}_{1}
, which is an
(\epsilon,(1+2f^{pow(\ell)}(\epsilon))\omega)
-approximate set with cardinality of
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
for
U^{pow(\ell)}_{1}\oplus U^{pow(\ell)}_{2}
; (ii). an
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
-time oracle for backtracking from
U^{pow(\ell)+1}_{1}
to
U^{pow(\ell)}_{1}\dot{\cup}U^{pow(\ell)}_{2}
.

To summarize, the total processing time is

{\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}|C_{i}|+(2^{1}+2^{2}+\cdots+2^{pow(\ell)})\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})={\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}|C_{i}|+\frac{\ell}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}).

Consider the functions in
\{f^{h}(\epsilon)\ |\ h=0,1,2,\cdots,pow(\ell)+1\}
, which are defined by the following recurrence relation:
f^{0}(\epsilon)=0
,
f^{1}(\epsilon)=\epsilon
and

f^{h}(\epsilon)=\epsilon+2(1+\epsilon)f^{h-1}(\epsilon)\text{ \ for \ }2\leq h\leq pow(\ell)+1
. According to Observation
5
, given integer
1\leq k\leq pow(\ell)
, if
U^{k}_{j_{k}}
is an
(f^{k}(\epsilon),\omega)
-approximate set of
\oplus^{2^{k}}_{t=1}C_{2^{k}(j_{k}-1)+t}
, where
j_{k}=1,2,\cdots,2^{pow(\ell)+1-k}
and
k=1,2,\cdots,h-1
, then
U^{k+1}_{j_{k+1}}
is an
(\epsilon+2(1+\epsilon)f^{k}(\epsilon),\omega)
-approximate set of

\oplus^{2^{k+1}}_{t=1}C_{2^{h}(j_{k+1}-1)+t}=\left(\oplus^{2^{k}}_{t=1}C_{2^{k}(2j_{k+1}-2)+t}\right)\oplus\left(\oplus^{2^{k}}_{t=1}C_{2^{k}(2j_{k+1}-1)+t}\right)
, where
j_{k+1}=1,2,\cdots,2^{pow(\ell)-k}
. Recall that
U^{1}_{j_{1}}
is
an
(f^{1}(\epsilon),\omega)
-approximate set of
C_{2j_{1}-1}\oplus C_{2j_{1}}
for every
j_{1}=1,2,\cdots,2^{pow(\ell)}
, it can be proved by recursion that
U^{h}_{j_{h}}
is an
(f^{h}(\epsilon),\omega)
-approximate set of
\oplus^{2^{h}}_{t=1}C_{2^{h}(j_{h}-1)+t}
, where
j_{h}=1,2,\cdots,2^{pow(\ell)+1-h}
and
h=1,2,\cdots,pow(\ell)+1
. In particular,
U^{pow(\ell)+1}_{1}
is an
(f^{pow(\ell)+1}(\epsilon),\omega)
-approximate set of
\oplus^{2^{pow(\ell)+1}}_{i=1}C_{i}
and a simple calculation shows that
f^{pow(\ell)+1}(\epsilon)={\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\ell+\epsilon^{2}\ell^{2})
.

With the help of this tree structure, an
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{\ell}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
-time oracle for backtracking from
U^{pow(\ell)+1}_{1}
to
\dot{\cup}^{\ell}_{i=1}C_{i}
is derived and works as follows:

•

For any
c\in U^{pow(\ell)+1}_{1}
, we back trace from the root of the tree. Note that the root node contains an
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
-time oracle for backtracking from
U^{pow(\ell)+1}_{1}
to
U^{pow(\ell)}_{1}\dot{\cup}U^{pow(\ell)}_{2}
. Thus in
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
processing time, we can find numbers

y^{(pow(\ell);1)}\in U^{pow(\ell)}_{1}\cup\{0\}
and
y^{(pow(\ell);2)}\in U^{pow(\ell)}_{2}\cup\{0\}
such that

|c-(y^{(pow(\ell);1)}+y^{(pow(\ell);1)})|\leq\epsilon(1+2f^{pow(\ell)}(\epsilon))\omega.

•

Back track recursively.

Given
1\leq h\leq pow(\ell)
, assume that we have determined
y^{(h;j_{h})}\in U^{h}_{j_{h}}\cup\{0\}
for every
j_{h}=1,2,3,\cdots,2^{pow(\ell)+1-h}
such that

|c-\Sigma^{2^{pow(\ell)+1-h}}_{j_{h}=1}y^{(h;j_{h})}|\leq\epsilon\Sigma^{pow(\ell)}_{k=h}2^{pow(\ell)-k}(1+2f^{k}(\epsilon))\omega.

For each
y^{(h;j_{h})}\in U^{h}_{j_{h}}\cup\{0\}
, if
y^{(h;j_{h})}=0
, then we let
y^{(h-1;2j_{h}-1)}=0
and
y^{(h-1;2j_{h})}=0
. If
y^{(h;j_{h})}\in U^{h}_{j_{h}}
, note that the node containing
U^{h}_{j_{h}}
also contains an
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
-time oracle for backtracking from
U^{h}_{j_{h}}
to
U^{h-1}_{2j_{h}-1}\dot{\cup}U^{h-1}_{2j_{h}}
, thus in
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
processing time, we can determine
y^{(h-1;2j_{h}-1)}\in U^{h-1}_{2j_{h}-1}\cup\{0\}
and
y^{(h-1;2j_{h})}\in U^{h-1}_{2j_{h}}\cup\{0\}
such that

|y^{(h;j_{h})}-(y^{(h-1;2j_{h}-1)}+y^{(h-1;2j_{h})})|\leq\epsilon(1+2f^{h-1}(\epsilon))\omega.

Then in
{\mathcal{O}}(2^{pow(\ell)+1-h}\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
processing time, we can determine
y^{(h-1;j_{h-1})}\in U^{h-1}_{j_{h-1}}\cup\{0\}
for every
j_{h-1}=1,2,3,\cdots,2^{pow(\ell)+2-h}
such that

|\Sigma^{2^{pow(\ell)}+1-h}_{j_{h}=1}y^{(h;j_{h})}-\Sigma^{2^{pow(\ell)}+2-h}_{j_{h-1}=1}y^{(h-1;j_{h-1})}|\leq 2^{pow(\ell)+1-h}\epsilon(1+2f^{h-1}(\epsilon))\omega.

It follows that

|c-\Sigma^{2^{pow(\ell)+2-h}}_{j_{h-1}=1}y^{(h-1;j_{h-1})}|\leq\epsilon\Sigma^{pow(\ell)}_{k=h-1}2^{pow(\ell)-k}(1+2f^{k}(\epsilon))\omega.

•

After
pow(\ell)
such rounds, we stop and have determined
c_{i}\in C_{i}\cup\{0\}
for every
i=1,2,3,\cdots,{\ell}
such that

|c-\sum^{\ell}_{i=1}c_{i}|\leq\epsilon\Sigma^{pow(\ell)}_{k=0}2^{pow(\ell)-k}(1+2f^{k}(\epsilon))\omega={\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\ell+\epsilon^{2}\ell^{2})\omega.

To summarize, the total processing time is
{\mathcal{O}}((1+2^{1}+2^{2}+\cdots+2^{pow(\ell)})\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})={\mathcal{O}}(\frac{\ell}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
.

In conclude, within
{\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}|C_{i}|+\frac{\ell}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
processing time, we will build a tree structure whose root node contains an
({\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\ell+\epsilon^{2}\ell^{2}),\omega)
-approximate set with cardinality of
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
for
\oplus^{\ell}_{i=1}C_{i}
. Meanwhile, with the help of this tree structure, an
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{\ell}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
-time oracle for backtracking from this approximate set to
\dot{\cup}^{\ell}_{i=1}C_{i}
is derived. ∎∎

Lemma
5
implies the following corollary, which allows us to build the approximate set of
S(\dot{\cup}^{\ell}_{i=1}X_{i})
from the approximate set of each
S(X_{i})
.

Corollary 2
.

Given multisets
X_{1},X_{2},\cdots,X_{\ell}\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. Let
C_{i}
be an
(\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon),\omega)
-approximate set of
S(X_{i})
, where
i=1,2,\cdots,\ell
. Assume that for each
C_{i}
, there is a
T_{i}
-time oracle for backtracking from
C_{i}
to
X_{i}
. Then in
{\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}|C_{i}|+\frac{\ell}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
processing time, we can

(i).

Compute an
(\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\ell+\epsilon^{3}\ell^{3}),\omega)
-approximate set with cardinality of
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
for
S(\dot{\cup}^{\ell}_{i=1}X_{i})
;

(ii).

Meanwhile build an
{\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}T_{i}+\frac{\ell}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
-time oracle for backtracking from this approximate set to
\dot{\cup}^{\ell}_{i=1}X_{i}
.

Proof.

Given multisets
X_{1},X_{2},\cdots,X_{\ell}\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. Let
C_{1},C_{2},\cdots,C_{\ell}
be
(\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon),\omega)
-approximate sets of
S(X_{1}),S(X_{2}),\cdots,S(X_{\ell})
, respectively. Assume that for each
C_{i}
, there is a
T_{i}
-time oracle for backtracking from
C_{i}
to
X_{i}
. The approach to approximate
S(\dot{\cup}^{\ell}_{i=1}X_{i})
and build oracle for backtracking is very similar to the one we have designed in the proof of Lemma
5
. The only difference is that when creating leaf nodes, we let the
i
-th node contain not only
C_{i}
but also the
T_{i}
-time oracle for backtracking from
C_{i}
to
X_{i}
. Let
\tilde{\omega}=(1+\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\ell))\omega
. Then we use the above iterative approach to approximate
\oplus^{\ell}_{i=1}C_{i}
, in
{\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}|C_{i}|+\frac{\ell}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
processing
time, we will build an augmented tree structure whose root node contains a
({\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\ell+\epsilon^{2}\ell^{2}),\tilde{\omega})
-approximate set with cardinality of
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
for
\oplus^{\ell}_{i=1}C_{i}
. Denote by
U
this approximate set. Meanwhile, with the help of this tree structure, an
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{\ell}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
-time oracle for backtracking from
U
to
\dot{\cup}^{\ell}_{i=1}C_{i}
is derived.

We claim that
U
is an
(\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\ell+\epsilon^{3}\ell^{3}),\omega)
-approximate set of
S(\dot{\cup}^{\ell}_{i=1}X_{i})
. Towards the claim, it is sufficient to observe the followings:

•

Since
U
is an
({\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\ell+\epsilon^{2}\ell^{2}),\tilde{\omega})
-approximate set of
\oplus^{\ell}_{i=1}C_{i}
, we have
U\subset[0,(1+{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\ell+\epsilon^{2}\ell^{2}))\tilde{\omega}]=[0,(1+\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\ell+\epsilon^{3}\ell^{3})))\omega]
.

•

Consider any
s\in S(\dot{\cup}^{\ell}_{i=1}X_{i})\cap[0,\omega]
. There exists
(s_{1},s_{2},\cdots,s_{\ell})
where
s_{i}\in S(X_{i})\cap[0,\omega]
for
i=1,2,\cdots,\ell
such that
s=\sum^{\ell}_{i=1}s_{i}
. For each
S(X_{i})
, recall that
C_{i}
is an
(\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon),\omega)
-approximate set of
S(X_{i})
, thus for
s_{i}\in S(X_{i})\cap[0,\omega]
, there exists
s^{\prime}_{i}\in C_{i}
such that
|s_{i}-s^{\prime}_{i}|\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)\omega
. It follows that
|s-\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}s^{\prime}_{i}|\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\ell)\omega
. Let
s^{\prime}=\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}s^{\prime}_{i}
, we have
s^{\prime}\leq s+\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\ell)\omega\leq(1+\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\ell))\omega=\tilde{\omega}
. Thus
s^{\prime}\in(\oplus^{{\ell}}_{i=1}C_{i})\cap[0,\tilde{\omega}]
. Recall that
U
is an
({\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\ell+\epsilon^{2}\ell^{2}),\tilde{\omega})
-approximate set for
\oplus^{\ell}_{i=1}C_{i}
, thus there exists
s^{\prime\prime}\in U
such that
|s^{\prime\prime}-s^{\prime}|\leq{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\ell+\epsilon^{2}\ell^{2})\tilde{\omega}=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\ell+\epsilon^{3}\ell^{3})\omega
. Furthermore, we have

|s-s^{\prime\prime}|\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\ell+\epsilon^{3}\ell^{3})\omega.

•

Consider any
c\in U
. Since
U
is an
({\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\ell+\epsilon^{2}\ell^{2}),\tilde{\omega})
-approximate set of
\oplus^{\ell}_{i=1}C_{i}
, there exists
(c^{\prime}_{1},c^{\prime}_{2},\cdots,c^{\prime}_{\ell})
where
c^{\prime}_{i}\in C_{i}\cup\{0\}
for
i=1,2,\cdots,\ell
such that
|c-\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}c^{\prime}_{i}|\leq{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\ell+\epsilon^{2}\ell^{2})\tilde{\omega}=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\ell+\epsilon^{3}\ell^{3})\omega
. For each
c^{\prime}_{i}\in C_{i}\cup\{0\}
, if
c^{\prime}_{i}=0
, let
X^{\prime}_{i}=\emptyset
, apparently
X^{\prime}_{i}\subset X_{i}
and
|c^{\prime}_{i}-\Sigma(X^{\prime}_{i})|\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)\omega
. Else if
c^{\prime}_{i}\in C_{i}
, note that
C_{i}
is an
(\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon),\omega)
-approximate set of
S(X_{i})
, then there exists
X^{\prime}_{i}\subset X_{i}
such that
|c^{\prime}_{i}-\Sigma(X^{\prime}_{i})|\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)\omega
. Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle|c-\sum^{\ell}_{i=1}\Sigma(X^{\prime}_{i})|

\displaystyle\leq|c-\sum^{\ell}_{i=1}c^{\prime}_{i}|+|\sum^{\ell}_{i=1}\Sigma(X^{\prime}_{i})-\sum^{\ell}_{i=1}c^{\prime}_{i}|

\displaystyle\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\ell+\epsilon^{3}\ell^{3})\omega+\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\ell){\omega}=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\ell+\epsilon^{3}\ell^{3})\omega

It is easy to see that
\dot{\cup}^{\ell}_{i=1}X^{\prime}_{i}\subset\dot{\cup}^{\ell}_{i=1}X_{i}
and
\Sigma(\dot{\cup}^{\ell}_{i=1}X^{\prime}_{i})=\sum^{\ell}_{i=1}\Sigma(X^{\prime}_{i})\in S(\dot{\cup}^{\ell}_{i=1}X_{i})
.

In the following, we show that with the help of the above augmented tree structure, an
{\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}T_{i}+\frac{\ell}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
-time oracle for backtracking from
U
to
\dot{\cup}^{\ell}_{i=1}X_{i}
can be derived. The oracle works as follows. Given any
c\in U
, note that
U
is a
({\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\ell+\epsilon^{2}\ell^{2}),\tilde{\omega})
-approximate set of
\oplus^{\ell}_{i=1}C_{i}
and with the help of augmented structure, an
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{\ell}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
-time oracle for backtracking from
U
to
\dot{\cup}^{\ell}_{i=1}C_{i}
has been derived. Thus in
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{\ell}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
-processing time, we can determine
c_{i}\in C_{i}\cup\{0\}
for every
i=1,2,\cdots,\ell
such that
|c-\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}c_{i}|\leq{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\ell+\epsilon^{2}\ell^{2})\tilde{\omega}=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\ell+\epsilon^{3}\ell^{3})\omega
. For each
c_{i}
, if
c_{i}=0
, let
X^{\prime}_{i}=\emptyset
, apparently
X^{\prime}_{i}\subset X_{i}
and
|c_{i}-\Sigma(X^{\prime}_{i})|\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)\omega
. Else if
c_{i}\in C_{i}
, recall that
C_{i}
is an
(\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon),\omega)
-approximate set of
S(X_{i})
and the leaf node containing
C_{i}
also contains a
T_{i}
-time oracle for backtracking from
C_{i}
to
X_{i}
, thus in
T_{i}
time, we can determine
X^{\prime}_{i}\subset X_{i}
such that
|c_{i}-\Sigma(X^{\prime}_{i})|\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)\omega
. To summarize, with the help of augmented tree structure, in total
{\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}T_{i}+\frac{\ell}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
processing time, we can determine
X^{\prime}_{i}\subset X_{i}
for every
i=1,2,\cdots,\ell
such that

|c-\sum^{\ell}_{i=1}\Sigma(X^{\prime}_{i})|\leq|c-\Sigma^{\ell}_{i=1}c_{i}|+|\sum^{\ell}_{i=1}\Sigma(X^{\prime}_{i})-\sum^{\ell}_{i=1}c_{i}|\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\ell+\epsilon^{3}\ell^{3})\omega.

Note that
\dot{\cup}^{\ell}_{i=1}X^{\prime}_{i}\subset\dot{\cup}^{\ell}_{i=1}X_{i}
and
\Sigma(\dot{\cup}^{\ell}_{i=1}X^{\prime}_{i})=\sum^{\ell}_{i=1}\Sigma(X^{\prime}_{i})
, hence Corollary
2
is proved.
∎∎

3
Number-theoretic construction

In this section, we introduce one of our main technical contributions, namely, the following number-theoretic rounding lemma.

Lemma 6

(Number-Theoretic Rounding Lemma)
.

Given a multiset
X\subset[\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda}},\frac{2}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda}}]
, where
\lambda\in[-1,+\infty)
is a parameter.For any
\alpha\in[1+\lambda,2+\lambda)
and any
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
let
\bar{d}
be the integer such that
2+\lambda-\alpha\in(\frac{\bar{d}}{d},\frac{\bar{d}+1}{d}]
. Then there exists a set
\Delta\subset\mathbb{R}
with
\Delta\subset\Theta(\epsilon^{-\alpha})

and
|\Delta|={\mathcal{O}}(\log(|X|)\cdot(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{{\mathcal{O}}(\bar{d}+1)})
, such that each
x\in X
can be rounded to the form
\rho h_{1}h_{2}\cdots h_{\bar{d}+1}
, where
\rho\in\Delta
and
h_{i}
’s satisfying the following conditions:

\displaystyle h_{1}h_{2}\cdots h_{\bar{d}+1}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\cap[\frac{1}{4\cdot\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}},\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}}].

(1a)

\displaystyle h_{\bar{d}+1}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\cap[\frac{1}{2\cdot\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha-\frac{\bar{d}}{d}}},\frac{2}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha-\frac{\bar{d}}{d}}}]\ \text{and if \ }\bar{d}>0\text{ \ then \ }h_{i}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\cap[\frac{1}{2\cdot\epsilon^{\frac{1}{d}}},\frac{2}{\epsilon^{\frac{1}{d}}}]\text{ \ for \ }i=1,2,\cdots,\bar{d}.

(1b)

\displaystyle|x-\rho h_{1}h_{2}\cdots h_{\bar{d}+1}|\leq\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}x.

(1c)

Moreover, there exists a deterministic algorithm, which can return
\Delta
and round every
x\in X
to the form
\rho h_{1}h_{2}\cdots h_{\bar{d}+1}
in
{\mathcal{O}}((|X|+\frac{\alpha(\alpha+1)}{\epsilon})\cdot\log(|X|)\cdot(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{{\mathcal{O}}(\bar{d}+1)})
time.

Remark 1.
Since
\epsilon
is a sufficiently small positive number, we can assume that
\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{d}}\geq 2
(by setting
\epsilon\leq 2^{-{d}}
). It follows that
\mathbb{N}_{+}\cap[\frac{1}{2\cdot\epsilon^{\frac{1}{d}}},\frac{2}{\epsilon^{\frac{1}{d}}}]\neq\emptyset
holds for every
i=1,2,\cdots,\bar{d}
. Moreover, note that
2+\lambda-\alpha\in(0,1]
, then
2+\lambda-\alpha\in(\frac{\bar{d}}{d},\frac{\bar{d}+1}{d}]
implies (i).
0\leq\bar{d}\leq d-1
; (ii).
\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha-\frac{\bar{d}}{d}}}\geq 1
, hence
\mathbb{N}_{+}\cap[\frac{1}{2\cdot\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha-\frac{\bar{d}}{d}}},\frac{2}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha-\frac{\bar{d}}{d}}}]\neq\emptyset
.

Remark 2.
The rounding procedure we derived in the proof of Lemma
6
guarantees that numbers with the same value in multiset
X
will be rounded to the same number.

Roughly speaking, Lemma
6
states that any multiset of given numbers within an interval of
[b,2b]
for some
b>0
can be rounded into “semi-smooth” numbers (relative error within
{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})
), which is a multiplication of two components – a common divisor component (i.e.,
\rho
) and a smooth number component (i.e.,
h_{1}h_{2}\cdots h_{\bar{d}+1}
). Moreover, there are only a few distinct common divisors (i.e.,
|\Delta|
is small). Here
\alpha
is a parameter that can be adjusted depending on the application. In particular, we will take two specific values for
\alpha
when we apply Lemma
6
in the future, namely
1+\lambda
and
3/2
.

It is worth mentioning that a similar number-theoretic result has been obtained by Jin
[
20
]
for solving the knapsack problem. However, Jin’s result is not sufficient for SUBSET SUM related problems when our target is a running time below
{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-2})
. We briefly explain the bottleneck. Using Jin’s number-theoretic construction, one can also compute a subset
\Delta^{\prime}
and round every
x\in X
to the form
\rho^{\prime}h^{\prime}_{1}h^{\prime}_{2}\cdots h^{\prime}_{\bar{d}+1}
, where
\delta^{\prime}\in\Delta^{\prime}
and
h^{\prime}_{i}
’s satisfying above conditions (
1b
) and (
1c
). However, there is a trade-off between the size of
\Delta^{\prime}
and the time for constructing it. That is, for any
\gamma\in(0,1)
, if
|\Delta^{\prime}|=\tilde{{\mathcal{O}}}(\epsilon^{\gamma-1})
, then the time to compute
\Delta^{\prime}
is
\tilde{{\mathcal{O}}}(\epsilon^{-\gamma-1})
. For the purpose of SUBSET SUM, we want
\gamma
to approach to
1
(so that
\Delta^{\prime}
has logarithmic cardinality), then Jin’s method requires
\tilde{{\mathcal{O}}}(\epsilon^{-2})
time, which is too large.

The rest of this section is dedicated to proving Lemma
6
. The proof is divided into two steps: we first prove the existence of
\Delta
, and then derive an algorithm which computes
\Delta
and rounds every
x\in X
to the form
\rho h_{1}h_{2}\cdots h_{d}
.
Step 1, the existence proof, utilizes the idea of Chan
[
6
]
and Jin
[
20
]
, which constructs the set
\Delta
by iteratively determining the existence an integer that can be a common divisor to a subset of numbers that are close to sufficiently many input numbers. We generalize their method in a parameterized way that allows us to control how large the common divisor and the smooth component are, which will also facilitate Step 2, the computation of
\Delta
.

Proof of Lemma
6
.

Step 1 - Existence of
\Delta
.

We proceed with a constructive proof that finds the elements in
\Delta
in an iterative way. In each iteration, we show that the current numbers can be rounded such that a significant fraction (i.e., a fraction of
1/(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}})^{{\mathcal{O}}(\bar{d}+1)}
) of them share one large common divisor. Hence,
{\mathcal{O}}(\log|X|(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{{\mathcal{O}}(\bar{d}+1)})
iterations suffice, which is the size of
\Delta
.

Let
X=\{x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{m}\}\subset[\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda}},\frac{2}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda}}]
, where
m=|X|
and
\lambda\in[-1,+\infty)
is a parameter.
Given
\alpha\in[1+\lambda,2+\lambda)
, we have
2+\lambda-\alpha\in(0,1]
. Then for each
x_{i}\in X
, there exists
k_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
x_{i}\in[(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})^{k_{i}},(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})^{k_{i}+1})
, we round
x_{i}
down to
\bar{x}_{i}=(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})^{k_{i}}
. Let
\overline{X}=\{\bar{x}_{1},\bar{x}_{2},\cdots,\bar{x}_{m}\}
.
It follows that

\displaystyle\bar{x}_{i}\leq x_{i}<(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})\bar{x}_{i}\text{\ \ for \ \ }i=1,2,\cdots,m.

(2a)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda}(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})}\leq\bar{x}_{i}\leq\frac{2}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda}}\text{\ \ for \ \ }i=1,2,\cdots,m.

(2b)

Given
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, let
0\leq\bar{d}<d
be the integer such that
2+\lambda-\alpha\in(\frac{\bar{d}}{d},\frac{\bar{d}+1}{d}]
, then
2+\lambda-\alpha-\frac{\bar{d}}{d}\in(0,\frac{1}{d}]
.

Consider positive integer
H
that can be written as
H=h_{1}h_{2}\cdots h_{\bar{d}+1}
, where
h_{\bar{d}+1}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\cap[\frac{1}{2\cdot\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha-\frac{\bar{d}}{d}}},\frac{2}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha-\frac{\bar{d}}{d}}}]
and if
\bar{d}>0
then
h_{i}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\cap[\frac{1}{2\cdot\epsilon^{\frac{1}{d}}},\frac{2}{\epsilon^{\frac{1}{d}}}]
for
i=1,2,\cdots,\bar{d}
. Denote by
\mathscr{H}
the set of all different integers with this property within
[\frac{1}{4\cdot\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}},\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}}]
. It is easy to see that
|\mathscr{H}|\leq\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}}
. Next we show that
|\mathscr{H}|
is relatively big, more precisely, we claim the following.

Claim 1
.

|\mathscr{H}|\geq\frac{1}{4\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}}/\left(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}}\right)^{{\mathcal{O}}(\bar{d}+1)}
.

Before proving Claim
1
, we first import the following lemma from
[
20
]
.

Lemma 7

(CF. Lemma 12 from
[
20
]
)
.

Let
T_{1},T_{2},\ldots,T_{\ell}
be positive real numbers satisfying
T_{1}\geq 2
and
T_{i+1}\geq 2T_{i}
. There exist at least
T_{\ell}/\left(\log T_{\ell}\right)^{{\mathcal{O}}(\ell)}
integers
t
satisfying the following condition:
t
can be written as a product of integers
t=n_{1}n_{2}\cdots n_{\ell}
, such that
n_{1}n_{2}\cdots n_{i}\in\left(T_{i}/2,T_{i}\right]
for every
1\leq i\leq\ell
.

Now we are ready to prove Claim
1
.

Proof of Claim
1
.

If
\bar{d}=0
, which implies that
\mathscr{H}=\mathbb{N}_{+}\cap[\frac{1}{2\cdot\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}},\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}}]
, then we have
|\mathscr{H}|\geq\frac{1}{4\cdot\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}}
. Otherwise
\bar{d}\geq 1
. Recall Lemma
7
, let
T_{\bar{d}+1}=\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}}={\epsilon^{-\frac{\bar{d}}{d}}}\cdot\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha-\frac{\bar{d}}{d}}}
and

T_{i}=\epsilon^{-\frac{i}{d}}
for
1\leq i\leq\bar{d}
. Since
\epsilon
is a sufficiently small positive number, we can assume that
\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{d}}\geq 2
, then
T_{1}=\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{d}}\geq 2
. Next, we will deal with
\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha-\frac{\bar{d}}{d}}}\geq 2
and
\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha-\frac{\bar{d}}{d}}}<2
separately.

First we consider the case that
\bar{d}\geq 1
and
\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha-\frac{\bar{d}}{d}}}\geq 2
.
It is easy to see that
T_{\bar{d}+1}={\epsilon^{-\frac{\bar{d}}{d}}}\cdot\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha-\frac{\bar{d}}{d}}}\geq 2T_{\bar{d}}
. Moreover, the following two observations guarantee that
T_{1}\geq 2
and
T_{i+1}\geq 2T_{i}
holds for every
1\leq i\leq\bar{d}
.

•

If
\bar{d}=1
, then apparently
T_{1}\geq 2
and
T_{i+1}\geq 2T_{i}
holds for every
1\leq i\leq\bar{d}
.

•

If
\bar{d}\geq 2
, we have
T_{1}\geq 2
,
T_{\bar{d}+1}\geq 2T_{\bar{d}}
and
T_{i+1}=\epsilon^{-\frac{i+1}{d}}=\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{d}}\cdot\epsilon^{-\frac{i}{d}}\geq 2T_{i}
holds for every
1\leq i\leq\bar{d}-1
.

According to Lemma
7
, there are at least
T_{\bar{d}+1}/(\log T_{\bar{d}+1})^{{\mathcal{O}}(\bar{d}+1)}
different integers
t
satisfying the following condition:
t
can be written as a product of integers
t=n_{1}n_{2}\cdots n_{\bar{d}+1}
, such that
n_{1}n_{2}\cdots n_{i}\in\left(T_{i}/2,T_{i}\right]
for every
1\leq i\leq\bar{d}+1
. These imply that
(\frac{1}{2\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}},\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}}]
contains at least
\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}}/\left(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}}\right)^{{\mathcal{O}}(\bar{d}+1)}
different integers
t
satisfying the following condition:
t
can be written as a product of integers
t=n_{1}n_{2}\cdots n_{\bar{d}+1}
, where
n_{\bar{d}+1}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\cap[\frac{1}{2\cdot\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha-\frac{\bar{d}}{d}}},\frac{2}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha-\frac{\bar{d}}{d}}}]
and
n_{i}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\cap[\frac{1}{2\cdot\epsilon^{\frac{1}{d}}},\frac{2}{\epsilon^{\frac{1}{d}}}]\ \text{for}\ 1\leq i\leq\bar{d}
.
By the definition of
\mathscr{H}
,
\mathscr{H}
contains at least
\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}}/\left(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}}\right)^{{\mathcal{O}}(\bar{d}+1)}
elements.

Now we consider the case that
\bar{d}\geq 1
and
\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha-\frac{\bar{d}}{d}}}<2
(i.e.,
\frac{1}{4\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}}\leq\frac{\epsilon^{-\frac{\bar{d}}{d}}}{2}
). Recall that
\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha-\frac{\bar{d}}{d}}}\geq 1
, we have
[\frac{\epsilon^{-\frac{\bar{d}}{d}}}{2},{\epsilon^{-\frac{\bar{d}}{d}}}]\subset[\frac{1}{4\cdot\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}},\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}}]
and
1\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\cap[\frac{1}{2\cdot\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha-\frac{\bar{d}}{d}}},\frac{2}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha-\frac{\bar{d}}{d}}}]
. Thus to prove that
\mathscr{H}
contains at least
\frac{1}{4\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}}/\left(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}}\right)^{{\mathcal{O}}(\bar{d}+1)}
elements, we only need to prove that

[\frac{\epsilon^{-\frac{\bar{d}}{d}}}{2},{\epsilon^{-\frac{\bar{d}}{d}}}]
contains at least
\frac{\epsilon^{-\frac{\bar{d}}{d}}}{2}/\left(\log\epsilon^{-\frac{\bar{d}}{d}}\right)^{{\mathcal{O}}(\bar{d}+1)}
different integers
t
satisfying the following condition:
t
can be written as a product of integers
t=n_{1}\cdots n_{\bar{d}}n_{\bar{d}+1}
, where
n_{\bar{d}+1}=1
and
n_{i}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\cap[\frac{1}{2\cdot\epsilon^{\frac{1}{d}}},\frac{2}{\epsilon^{\frac{1}{d}}}]
for
1\leq i\leq\bar{d}
. Towards this, it is sufficient to observe the followings:

•

If
\bar{d}=1
, then
\frac{\epsilon^{-\frac{\bar{d}}{d}}}{2}=\frac{\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{d}}}{2}
. Note that
[\frac{\epsilon^{-\frac{\bar{d}}{d}}}{2},{\epsilon^{-\frac{\bar{d}}{d}}}]
contains at least
\frac{\epsilon^{-\frac{\bar{d}}{d}}}{2}/\left(\log\epsilon^{-\frac{\bar{d}}{d}}\right)^{{\mathcal{O}}(\bar{d}+1)}
different integers, and any integer
t
in
[\frac{\epsilon^{-\frac{\bar{d}}{d}}}{2},\epsilon^{-\frac{\bar{d}}{d}}]
, i.e., in
[\frac{\epsilon^{-\frac{{1}}{d}}}{2},\epsilon^{-\frac{{1}}{d}}]
, satisfies the condition:
t
can be written as a product of integers
t=n_{1}n_{2}\cdots n_{\bar{d}+1}=n_{1}n_{2}
, where
n_{\bar{d}+1}=n_{2}=1
and
n_{i}=t\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\cap[\frac{1}{2\cdot\epsilon^{\frac{1}{d}}},\frac{2}{\epsilon^{\frac{1}{d}}}]
for
1\leq i\leq\bar{d}
.

•

If
\bar{d}\geq 2
, recall that
T_{1}\geq 2
and
T_{i+1}=\epsilon^{-\frac{i+1}{d}}=\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{d}}\cdot\epsilon^{-\frac{i}{d}}\geq 2T_{i}
holds for every
1\leq i\leq\bar{d}-1
. According to Lemma
7
, there are at least
T_{\bar{d}}/(\log T_{\bar{d}})^{{\mathcal{O}}(\bar{d})}=\epsilon^{-\frac{\bar{d}}{d}}/\left(\log\epsilon^{-\frac{\bar{d}}{d}}\right)^{{\mathcal{O}}(\bar{d})}
different integers
t
satisfying the following condition:
t
can be written as a product of integers
t=n_{1}n_{2}\cdots n_{\bar{d}}
, such that
n_{1}n_{2}\cdots n_{i}\in\left(T_{i}/2,T_{i}\right]
for every
1\leq i\leq\bar{d}
. These imply that
[\frac{\epsilon^{-\frac{\bar{d}}{d}}}{2},{\epsilon^{-\frac{\bar{d}}{d}}}]
contains at least
\epsilon^{-\frac{\bar{d}}{d}}/\left(\log\epsilon^{-\frac{\bar{d}}{d}}\right)^{{\mathcal{O}}(\bar{d})}\geq\frac{\epsilon^{-\frac{\bar{d}}{d}}}{2}/\left(\log\epsilon^{-\frac{\bar{d}}{d}}\right)^{{\mathcal{O}}(\bar{d}+1)}
different integers
t
satisfying the following condition:
t
can be written as a product of integers
t=n_{1}n_{2}\cdots n_{\bar{d}}n_{\bar{d}+1}
, where
n_{\bar{d}+1}=1
and
n_{i}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\cap[\frac{1}{2\cdot\epsilon^{\frac{1}{d}}},\frac{2}{\epsilon^{\frac{1}{d}}}]
for
1\leq i\leq\bar{d}
.

To summarize,
\mathscr{H}
contains at least
\frac{1}{4\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}}/\left(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}}\right)^{{\mathcal{O}}(\bar{d}+1)}
elements. ∎∎

Let
\mathscr{H}=\{H_{1},H_{2},\cdots,H_{\tau}\}
, where
\frac{1}{4\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}}/(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}})^{{\mathcal{O}}(\bar{d}+1)}\leq\tau\leq\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}}
. For each
H_{v}\in\mathscr{H}
, there exists
s_{v}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
H_{v}\in[(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})^{s_{v}},(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})^{s_{v}+1})
, we round
H_{v}
down to
\overline{H}_{v}=(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})^{s_{v}}
. Let
\overline{\mathscr{H}}=\{\overline{H}_{1},\overline{H}_{2},\cdots,\overline{H}_{\tau}\}
. It follows that

\displaystyle\overline{H}_{v}\leq H_{v}<(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})\overline{H}_{v}\text{\ \ for \ \ }v=1,2,\cdots,\tau.

(3a)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})\cdot 4\cdot\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}}\leq\overline{H}_{v}\leq\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}}\text{\ \ for \ \ }v=1,2,\cdots,\tau.

(3b)

Notice that elements in
\overline{\mathscr{H}}
are different from each other. This is because that if there exist two different integers
H_{v_{1}},H_{v_{2}}\in\mathscr{H}
satisfying
H_{v_{1}},H_{v_{2}}\in[(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})^{s},(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})^{s+1})
, then we have
1\leq|H_{v_{1}}-H_{v_{2}}|<\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}\cdot(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})^{s}\leq\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}\cdot\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}}=1,
which is impossible.

Consider the following
\tau\times m
table, denote it by
Table^{(1)}
.

\bar{x}_{1}=(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})^{k_{1}}

\bar{x}_{2}=(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})^{k_{2}}

\cdots

\bar{x}_{m}=(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})^{k_{m}}

\overline{{H}}_{1}=(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})^{s_{1}}

{k_{1}-s_{1}}

{k_{2}-s_{1}}

\cdots

{k_{m}-s_{1}}

\overline{{H}}_{2}=(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})^{s_{2}}

{k_{1}-s_{2}}

{k_{2}-s_{2}}

\cdots

{k_{m}-s_{2}}

\vdots

\vdots

\vdots

\vdots

\vdots

\overline{{H}}_{\tau}=(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})^{s_{\tau}}

{k_{1}-s_{\tau}}

{k_{2}-s_{\tau}}

\cdots

{k_{m}-s_{\tau}}

Since elements in
\overline{\mathscr{H}}
are different from each other, the elements of the same column in
Table^{(1)}
are different from each other, i.e., integers in
\{k_{i}-s_{v}\ |\ v=1,2,\cdots,\tau\}
are different from each other for every
i=1,2,\cdots,m
. Let
\mathscr{C}=set\{k_{i}-s_{v}\ |\ i=1,2,\cdots,m\text{ \ and \ }v=1,2,\cdots,\tau\}
, which is the set of all distinct entries in
Table^{(1)}
. Recall (
2b
) and (
3b
), for every
i=1,2,\cdots,m
and every
v=1,2,\cdots,\tau
, we have
(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})^{k_{i}-s_{v}}=\frac{\bar{x}_{i}}{\overline{H}_{v}}\in[\frac{1}{(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})\epsilon^{\alpha}},\frac{8\cdot(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})}{\epsilon^{\alpha}}]
, thus
|\mathscr{C}|={\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}})
.
Let
\mathscr{C}=\{c_{1},c_{2},\cdots,c_{\mu}\}
, where
\mu={\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}}).

We first find in
\mathscr{C}
the element with the most occurrences in
Table^{(1)}
(we break tie arbitrarily), let’s say
c_{1}
. Then we find the columns containing
c_{1}
in
Table^{(1)}
and build
Table^{(2)}
by dropping these columns from
Table^{(1)}
. Let
n^{(1)}_{i}
be the number of columns containing
c_{i}
in
Table^{(1)}
for every
i=1,2,\cdots,\mu
. Note that elements of the same column in
Table^{(1)}
are different from each other, thus we have
\sum_{i=1}^{\mu}n^{(1)}_{i}=\tau m.
Since
c_{1}
is the element with the most occurrences in
Table^{(1)}
, it follows that
n^{(1)}_{1}\geq\frac{\tau m}{\mu}
and the number of columns in
Table^{(2)}
is

m-n^{(1)}_{1}\leq(1-\frac{\tau}{\mu})m.

Repeat the above operations iteratively. Assume that we have constructed
Table^{(1)},\cdots,Table^{(j)}
. Besides, assume that we have found
c_{1},\cdots,c_{j}
, which are the elements with the most occurrences in
Table^{(1)},\cdots,Table^{(j)}
, respectively. Let
n^{(t)}_{i}
denote the number of columns containing
c_{i}
in
Table^{(t)}
, where
i=t,t+1,\cdots,\mu
and
t=1,2,\cdots,j
. It can be inductively proved that the number of columns in
Table^{(t)}
is

m-\sum^{t-1}_{i=1}n^{(i)}_{i}\leq(1-\frac{\tau}{\mu})^{t-1}m,\text{\ where \ }t=1,2,\cdots,j.

We stop building
Table^{(j+1)}
as soon as
m-\sum^{j}_{i=1}n^{(i)}_{i}=0
. Then
(1-\frac{\tau}{\mu})^{j-1}m\geq m-\sum^{j-1}_{i=1}n^{(i)}_{i}\geq 1
. Recall that
\mu={\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}})
and
\frac{1}{4\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}}/(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}})^{{\mathcal{O}}(\bar{d}+1)}\leq\tau\leq\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}}
, a careful calculation shows that
j={\mathcal{O}}(\log(m)\cdot(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{{\mathcal{O}}(\bar{d}+1)})={\mathcal{O}}(\log(|X|)\cdot(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{{\mathcal{O}}(\bar{d}+1)})
. In the meantime, we have obtained
c_{1},\cdots,c_{j}
, which are the elements with the most occurrences in
Table^{(1)},\cdots,Table^{(j)}
, respectively. We claim that

\{(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})^{c_{i}}\ |\ i=1,2,\cdots,j\}
is a required
\Delta
in the Lemma
6
. The claim is guaranteed by the followings:

•

(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})^{c_{i}}\in[\frac{1}{(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})\epsilon^{\alpha}},\frac{8\cdot(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})}{\epsilon^{\alpha}}],
i.e.,
(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})^{c_{i}}=\Theta(\epsilon^{-\alpha})
, holds for every
1\leq i\leq j
.

•

Consider any
x_{i}\in X
, whose rounded value is
\bar{x}_{i}=(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})^{k_{i}}
. According to (
2a
), we have

(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})^{k_{i}}\leq x_{i}<(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})^{k_{i}+1}.

Since
m-\sum^{j}_{i=1}n^{(i)}_{i}=0
, the column corresponding to
\bar{x}_{i}
in
Table^{(1)}
must be dropped from some
Table^{(t)}\ (t\leq j)
. Recall that
c_{t}
is the element with the most occurrences in
Table^{(t)}
and all columns dropped from
Table^{(t)}
contain
c_{t}
, thus
c_{t}
is in the column corresponding to
\bar{x}_{i}
. Hence
(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})^{k_{i}-c_{t}}=\frac{\bar{x}_{i}}{(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})^{c_{t}}}\in\overline{\mathscr{H}}
. Note that
(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})^{k_{i}-c_{t}}
is a rounded value of some
h_{1}h_{2}\cdots h_{\bar{d}+1}\in\mathscr{H}
, it thus follows that
h_{1}h_{2}\cdots h_{\bar{d}+1}\in[(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})^{k_{i}-c_{t}},(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})^{k_{i}-c_{t}+1})
, then we have
\frac{x_{i}}{1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}}\leq(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})^{k_{i}}\leq(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})^{c_{t}}\cdot h_{1}h_{2}\cdots h_{\bar{d}+1}\leq(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})^{k_{i}+1}\leq(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})x_{i}.

Thus
x_{i}
can be rounded to
(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})^{c_{t}}\cdot h_{1}h_{2},\cdots h_{\bar{d}+1}
and it holds that
|x_{i}-(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})^{c_{t}}\cdot h_{1}h_{2},\cdots h_{\bar{d}+1}|\leq\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}x_{i}.

Step 2 -
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(|X|+\frac{\alpha(\alpha+1)}{\epsilon})
-time algorithm to compute
\Delta
and round every
x\in X
to the form
\rho h_{1}h_{2}\cdots h_{\bar{d}+1}
.

According to the discussion above, we can obtain
\Delta
and round every
x\in X
to the form
\rho h_{1}h_{2}\cdots h_{\bar{d}+1}
by iteratively solving the following two problems for every
j={\mathcal{O}}(\log(|X|)\cdot(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{{\mathcal{O}}(\bar{d}+1)})
:

P_{1}^{(j)}:

Find the element with the most occurrences in
Table^{(j)}
, say,
c_{j}
.

P_{2}^{(j)}:

Determine the columns containing
c_{j}
in
Table^{(j)}
, and construct
Table^{(j+1)}
if the iteration continues.

A brute-force method that enumerates all the entries in each
Table^{(j)}
can work, but it is too expensive. In the following, we aim to derive a more efficient algorithm.

We need to build
Table^{(1)}
before iteration. Towards this, we first compute
\overline{X}
and sort the elements in
\overline{X}
in ascending order. Without loss of generality, we assume that
\bar{x}_{1}\leq\bar{x}_{2}\leq\cdots\leq\bar{x}_{m}
, i.e.,
k_{1}\leq k_{2}\leq\cdots\leq k_{m}
. Then we compute
\mathscr{H}
and
\overline{\mathscr{H}}
, and sort the elements in
\overline{\mathscr{H}}
in descending order. Without loss of generality, we assume that
\overline{H}_{1}\geq\overline{H}_{2}\geq\cdots\geq\overline{H}_{\tau}
, i.e.,
s_{1}\geq s_{2}\geq\cdots\geq s_{\tau}
. Finally,
Table^{(1)}
will be built in an implicit way without specifying each entry, in particular, only
\overline{X}
and
\overline{\mathscr{H}}
are stored (from
\overline{X}
and
\overline{\mathscr{H}}
it is sufficient to recover the whole table as each entry can be uniquely determined). The total time of building
Table^{(1)}
is
{\mathcal{O}}(|X|\log|X|+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
.

Now we consider an arbitrary iteration
j
, and solve
P_{1}^{(j)}
and
P_{2}^{(j)}
. At the beginning of iteration
j
, we have obtained
Table^{(j)}
, whose columns are exactly columns
l^{j}_{1},l^{j}_{2},\cdots,l^{j}_{m_{j}}
of
Table^{(1)}
, where
l^{j}_{1}\leq l^{j}_{2}\leq\cdots\leq l^{j}_{m_{j}}
. Let
\overline{X}^{j}=\{\bar{x}_{l^{j}_{1}},\bar{x}_{l^{j}_{2}},\cdots,\bar{x}_{l^{j}_{m_{j}}}\}
, then
Table^{(j)}
is as follows.

\bar{x}_{l^{j}_{1}}=(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})^{k_{l^{j}_{1}}}

\bar{x}_{l^{j}_{2}}=(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})^{k_{l^{j}_{2}}}

\cdots

\bar{x}_{l^{j}_{m_{j}}}=(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})^{k_{l^{j}_{m_{j}}}}

\overline{{H}}_{1}=(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})^{s_{1}}

{k_{l^{j}_{1}}-s_{1}}

{k_{l^{j}_{2}}-s_{1}}

\cdots

{k_{l^{j}_{m_{j}}}-s_{1}}

\overline{{H}}_{2}=(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})^{s_{2}}

{k_{l^{j}_{1}}-s_{2}}

{k_{l^{j}_{2}}-s_{2}}

\cdots

{k_{l^{j}_{m_{j}}}-s_{2}}

\vdots

\vdots

\vdots

\vdots

\vdots

\overline{{H}}_{\tau}=(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})^{s_{\tau}}

{k_{l^{j}_{1}}-s_{\tau}}

{k_{l^{j}_{2}}-s_{\tau}}

\cdots

{k_{l^{j}_{m_{j}}}-s_{\tau}}

Towards
P_{1}^{(j)}
, we define the following two polynomials:

f^{j}(x)=x^{k_{l^{j}_{1}}-s_{1}}+x^{k_{l^{j}_{1}}-s_{2}}+x^{k_{l^{j}_{1}}-s_{3}}+\cdots+x^{k_{l^{j}_{1}}-s_{\tau}}

g^{j}(x)=1+x^{k_{l^{j}_{2}}-k_{l^{j}_{1}}}+x^{k_{l^{j}_{3}}-k_{l^{j}_{1}}}+x^{k_{l^{j}_{4}}-k_{l^{j}_{1}}}+\cdots+x^{k_{l^{j}_{m_{j}}}-k_{l^{j}_{1}}}

Consider the product
f^{j}(x)\cdot g^{j}(x)
and the coefficient of an arbitrary term
x^{c}
in the product. By the definition of polynomial multiplication, the coefficient of
x^{c}
counts all the pairs
(a,b)
such that the
a
-th term of
f^{j}
and the
b
-th term of
g^{j}
multiply to
x^{c}
. Equivalently, the coefficient of
x^{c}
counts all the pairs
(a,b)
such that the exponent of
a
-th term of
f^{j}
, which is
k_{l^{j}_{1}}-s_{a}
, and the exponent of
b
-th term of
g^{j}
, which is
k_{l_{b}^{j}}-k_{l^{j}_{1}}
, add up to
c
. Observe that the two exponents,
k_{l^{j}_{1}}-s_{a}
and
k_{l_{b}^{j}}-k_{l^{j}_{1}}
, add up to exactly
k_{l_{b}^{j}}-s_{a}
, which is the element in the
b
-th column of Table
(j)
. Hence, the coefficient of
x^{c}
counts the number of the occurrences of element
c
in
Table^{(j)}
.

Recall that
\mathscr{C}=set\{k_{i}-s_{v}\ |\ i=1,2,\cdots,m\text{ \ and \ }v=1,2,\cdots,\tau\}
denotes the set of all distinct entries in
Table^{(1)}
, we claim that
0\leq\mathscr{C}^{\min}\leq\mathscr{C}^{\max}={\mathcal{O}}(\frac{\alpha+1}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
. The claim is guaranteed by the followings:

•

By the facts that
\alpha\in[1+\lambda,2+\lambda)
and
\lambda\in[-1,+\infty)
, we have
\alpha\geq 0
. Recall that
X\subset[\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda}},\frac{2}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda}}]
and
\mathscr{H}\subset[\frac{1}{4\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}},\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}})
, we have
{\overline{\mathscr{H}}}^{\max}\leq\overline{X}^{\min}
.

•

Notice that
\min_{c\in\mathscr{C}}(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})^{c}={\overline{X}^{\min}}/{{\overline{\mathscr{H}}}^{\max}}\geq 1
, we have
\mathscr{C}^{\min}\geq 0
.

•

Recall (
2b
) and (
3b
), we have
\max_{c\in\mathscr{C}}(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})^{c}={\overline{X}^{\max}}/{{\overline{\mathscr{H}}}^{\min}}\in[\frac{1}{(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})\epsilon^{\alpha}},\frac{8\cdot(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})}{\epsilon^{\alpha}}]
, which imlpies that
\mathscr{C}^{\max}={\mathcal{O}}(\frac{\alpha+1}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
.

Since
{\overline{\mathscr{H}}}^{\max}\leq\overline{X}^{\min}
, we have
k_{l^{j}_{1}}\geq s_{\tau}
, hence
k_{l^{j}_{m_{j}}}-k_{l^{j}_{1}}\leq k_{l^{j}_{m_{j}}}-s_{\tau}
. Notice that
\mathscr{C}^{\min}\leq k_{l^{j}_{1}}-s_{1}\leq k_{l^{j}_{1}}-s_{2}\leq\cdots\leq k_{l^{j}_{1}}-s_{\tau}\leq\mathscr{C}^{\max}
and
0\leq k_{l^{j}_{2}}-k_{l^{j}_{1}}\leq k_{l^{j}_{3}}-k_{l^{j}_{1}}\leq\cdots\leq k_{l^{j}_{m_{j}}}-k_{l^{j}_{1}}\leq k_{l^{j}_{m_{j}}}-s_{\tau}\leq\mathscr{C}^{\max}
. Then in
{\mathcal{O}}(\mathscr{C}^{\max}\log(\mathscr{C}^{\max}))={\mathcal{O}}(\frac{\alpha(\alpha+1)}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}}(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{2})

processing time, Fast Fourier Transform can return the product
f^{j}(x)\cdot g^{j}(x)
. We pick the term
x^{c_{j}}
whose coefficient is the largest in
f^{j}(x)\cdot g^{j}(x)
(we break tie arbitrarily), then
c_{j}
is the element with the most occurrences in
Table^{(j)}
. Note that the time to construct
f^{j}(x)
and
g^{j}(x)
is
{\mathcal{O}}(|\overline{X}^{j}|+\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}})
, and the time to pick
x^{c_{j}}
from
f^{j}(x)\cdot g^{j}(x)
is
{\mathcal{O}}(\mathscr{C}^{\max})={\mathcal{O}}(\frac{\alpha+1}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
. To summarize, the total time to find
c_{j}
, i.e., fine the element with the most occurrences in
Table^{(j)}
, is
{\mathcal{O}}(|\overline{X}^{j}|+\frac{\alpha(\alpha+1)}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha}}(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{2})
.

Now we consider
P_{2}^{(j)}
. Given
c_{j}
as the element with the most occurrences in
Table^{(j)}
, we want to identify all columns that contain
c_{j}
. For every
\bar{x}_{l^{j}_{u}}\in\overline{X}_{j}
, we use binary search to check whether
\frac{\bar{x}_{l^{j}_{u}}}{(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})^{c_{j}}}
is in
\overline{\mathscr{H}}
. If the answer is “yes”, i.e., there exists
\overline{H}_{v}\in\overline{\mathscr{H}}
such that
\overline{H}_{v}=\frac{\bar{x}_{l^{j}_{u}}}{(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})^{c_{j}}}
, then column
l^{j}_{u}
contains
c_{j}
and we can round
x_{l^{j}_{u}}
to
(1+\epsilon^{2+\lambda-\alpha})^{c_{j}}\cdot H_{v}
. Binary search takes logarithmic time, therefore in
{\mathcal{O}}(|\overline{X}^{j}|\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
time, we can find all columns containing
c_{j}
in
Table^{(j)}
, and meanwhile round the elements in
X
corresponding to these columns accordingly. Moreover, by dropping these columns from
Table^{(j)}
, we can obtain
Table^{(j+1)}
and go to the next iteration. In general, for
Table^{j+1}
we only store
\overline{X}^{j+1}
and
\overline{\mathscr{H}}
. From
\overline{X}^{j+1}
and
\overline{\mathscr{H}}
it is sufficient to recover the whole table as each entry can be uniquely determined.

To summarize, there are at most
{\mathcal{O}}(\log(|X|)\cdot(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{{\mathcal{O}}(\bar{d}+1)})
iterations, a simple calculation shows that the total processing time is
{\mathcal{O}}((|X|+\frac{\alpha(\alpha+1)}{\epsilon})\cdot\log(|X|)\cdot(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{{\mathcal{O}}(\bar{d}+1)})
. This completes the proof of Lemma
6
.∎∎

4
Algorithms for computing subset-sums of smooth numbers

Given any multiset
X
, recall that the subset-sums of
X
is
S(X)=set\{\Sigma(Y)\ |\ Y\subset X\}
, representing the set of all possible subset sums of
X
. We show that, if all the input numbers have a nice number-theoretic property, then their subset-sums can be computed (approximately) in a more efficient way. More precisely, given
\epsilon>0
,
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
\bar{d}\in\mathbb{N}\cap[0,d-1]
, we define
(\epsilon,d,\bar{d})
-
smooth numbers
as the integers that can and have been factorized as
h_{1}h_{2}\cdots h_{\bar{d}+1}
, where
h_{\bar{d}+1}\in\mathbb{N}\cap[1,{2\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{d}}}]
and

h_{i}\in\mathbb{N}\cap[\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{d}},2\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{d}}]
for
i=1,2,\cdots,\bar{d}
. For ease of presentation, we refer to
(\epsilon,d,\bar{d})
-smooth numbers as smooth numbers when
d,\bar{d}
are clear from the context. The goal of this section is to prove the following Lemma on algorithms for approximating subset-sums of smooth numbers.

Lemma 8
.

Given
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
\bar{d}\in\mathbb{N}\cap[0,d-1]
. Let
A
be a multiset of
(\epsilon,d,\bar{d})
-smooth numbers, that is, every element in
A
can and have been factorized as
h_{1}h_{2}\cdots h_{\bar{d}+1}
, where
h_{\bar{d}+1}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\cap[1,2\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{d}}]
and if
\bar{d}\geq 1
then
h_{i}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\cap[\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{d}},{2\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{d}}}]
for
i=1,2,\cdots,\bar{d}
.

Then for any
k\in\mathbb{N}\cap[0,\bar{b}]
, in
{\mathcal{O}}(d\cdot|A|+\Sigma(A){\epsilon^{\frac{k}{d}}}\cdot\log(|A|)\cdot\log(\Sigma(A){\epsilon^{\frac{k}{d}}})+{\epsilon^{-(1+\frac{k}{d})}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
processing time, we can

(i).

Compute an
{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{k})
-approximate set with cardinality of
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
for
S(A)
;

(ii).

Meanwhile build a
T^{(1)}
-time oracle for backtracking from this approximates set to
A
, where
T^{(1)}={\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma(A){\epsilon^{\frac{k}{d}}}\cdot\log(|A|)\cdot\log(\Sigma(A){\epsilon^{\frac{k}{d}}})+{\epsilon^{-(1+\frac{k}{d})}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
.

Remark.
We will take a specific value for
k
when we apply Lemma
8
in the future, namely
k={d}/{4}
, where
d\geq 12
is divisible by
4
.

Note that Lemma
8
consists of two parts: one is computing (approximately) subset-sums of
A
, and the other is building an oracle for backtracking. Thus Lemma
8
implies a deterministic weak
(1-\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon))
-approximation algorithm for SUBSET SUM instance
(A,t)
, where
A
is a multiset of
(\epsilon,d,\bar{d})
-smooth numbers and
t=\Theta(\Sigma(A))
.

Proof of Lemma
8
.

Denote the set of all different integers in
\mathbb{N}_{+}\cap[\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{d}},2\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{d}}]
by
\mathcal{P}=\{p_{1},p_{2},\cdots,p_{m}\}
. Then every element of
A
is a multiplication of
\bar{d}+1
integers (which are called factors of the element), with
\bar{d}
integers belonging to
\mathcal{P}
and one extra integer
h_{\bar{d}+1}
that may or may not belong to
\mathcal{P}
. For simplicity, we fix the order of factors of each element arbitrarily (except that the
(\bar{d}+1)
-th factor must be
h_{\bar{d}+1}
if
h_{\bar{d}+1}\not\in\mathcal{P}
) and refer to
h_{i}
as its
i
-th factor.

Intuitively, we want to divide integers of
A
into subsets such that integers that share the same factors are in the same subset. However, since every integer is a multiplication of
\bar{d}+1
factors, the division will have a layered structure. We give a simple example for when
\bar{d}=2
. Consider 3 integers
p_{1}p_{2}p_{3}
,
p_{2}p_{3}p_{4}
and
p_{1}p_{2}p_{4}
. We first divide them based on the first factor, that is, integers whose first factor is
p_{i}
are put into group
i
. Hence we derive two groups: group 1 containing
p_{1}p_{2}p_{3}
,
p_{1}p_{2}p_{4}
, and group 2 containing
p_{2}p_{3}p_{4}
. We further subdivide group 1 based on the second factor, that is, integers whose second factor is
p_{i}
are put into subgroup
i
. We can see that
p_{1}p_{2}p_{3}
and
p_{1}p_{2}p_{4}
are still in the same subgroup as their second factors are also the same. We keep the subdivision procedure for
\bar{d}
times. It is easy to see that if two integers are in the same subgroup after
\zeta
rounds of subdivisions, then they share the first
\zeta
factors.

Now we formally present the division procedure. We build a tree structure of
\bar{d}+1
layers for all elements in
A
as follows:

•

Let the root node contain all elements in
A
. Root node has layer-
1
.

•

If
\bar{d}=0
, we stop building and obtain a single node tree structure. Else if
\bar{d}\geq 1
, we proceed to create
m
child nodes of root node by subdividing
A
into
m
groups such that elements in the
i
-th group share
p_{i}
as their first factor (If
A
does not contain an element with
p_{i}
as its first factor, then let the
i
-th group be an empty set).

•

Suppose we have obtained nodes of layer-
\zeta
, where
2\leq\zeta\leq\bar{d}+1
, such that elements in each node share the same
j
-th factor for
1\leq j\leq\zeta-1
. If
\zeta=\bar{d}+1
, we stop building and obtain a tree structure of
\bar{d}+1
layers. Else if
\zeta\leq\bar{d}
, we create nodes of layer-
(\zeta+1)
as follows. Consider an arbitrary node of layer-
\zeta
, say,
u
. Let
A_{u}\subset A
be the (multi-)set of all the elements contained in node
u
. We create
m
child nodes of
u
by subdividing
A_{u}
into
m
groups such that elements in the
i
-th group share
p_{i}
as their
\zeta
-th factor (If
A_{u}
does not contain an element with
p_{i}
as its
\zeta
-th factor, then let the
i
-th group be an empty set).

For each layer-
\zeta
node
u
, let
A_{u}\subset A
be the (multi-)set of all the elements contained in node
u
. We define
\pi_{u}
as follows: if
\zeta=1
, then
\pi_{u}=1
; else if

2\leq\zeta\leq\bar{d}+1
, then elements in
A_{u}
share the same
j
-th factor for
1\leq j\leq\zeta-1
, we let
\pi_{u}
be the product of these
\zeta-1
factors.

Notice that each node in this tree except the leaf node has a degree of
m={\mathcal{O}}({\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{d}}})
, so there are in total
m^{\zeta-1}={\mathcal{O}}({\epsilon^{-\frac{(\zeta-1)}{d}}})
nodes at the
\zeta
-th layer (called layer-
\zeta
nodes). The time of building this tree is
{\mathcal{O}}({\epsilon^{-\frac{(\bar{d}+1)}{d}}}+\bar{d}\cdot|A|)
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}\cap[0,\bar{d}]
. Next, with the help of this tree structure, we will compute an
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)
-approximate set of
S(A)
and build an oracle for backtracking from this approximate set to
A
. Note that we do not start from the leaves of the tree, but start from layer-
(k+1)
nodes for some parameter
k
that can be optimized when we apply Lemma
8
.

1.

Start from layer-
(k+1)
. Consider any node in layer-
(k+1)
, namely node
u
, let
A_{u}
be the (multi-)set of elements it contains. Let
\tilde{A}_{u}=A_{u}/\pi_{u}
. Note that
\tilde{A}_{u}
is also an integral (multi-)set. Recall Lemma
2
, in
{\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma(\tilde{A}_{u})\log\Sigma(\tilde{A}_{u})\log|\tilde{A}_{u}|)
processing time, we can compute
S(\tilde{A}_{u})
and build an
{\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma(\tilde{A}_{u})\log\Sigma(\tilde{A}_{u})\log|\tilde{A}_{u}|)
-time oracle for backtracking from
S(\tilde{A}_{u})
to
\tilde{A}_{u}
. Define
B_{\tau}=S(\tilde{A}_{u})\cap[(\tau-1)\epsilon\Sigma(\tilde{A}_{u}),\tau\epsilon\Sigma(\tilde{A}_{u}))
, where
\tau=1,2,\cdots,2+\lfloor{1}/{\epsilon}\rfloor
. Let
C_{\tilde{A}_{u}}=set\{(B_{\tau})^{max},(B_{\tau})^{min}\ |\ \tau=1,2,\cdots,2+\lfloor{1}/{\epsilon}\rfloor\}
.

C_{\tilde{A}_{u}}
essentially contains the two “extreme” points (i.e., largest and smallest) of
S({\tilde{A}_{u}})
within each subinterval
[(\tau-1)\epsilon\Sigma({\tilde{A}_{u}}),\tau\epsilon\Sigma({\tilde{A}_{u}}))
, and it is thus easy to see that
C_{\tilde{A}_{u}}
is an
\epsilon
-approximate set with cardinality of
{\mathcal{O}}(1/\epsilon)
for
S({\tilde{A}_{u}})
and the above oracle is an
{\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma({\tilde{A}_{u}})\log\Sigma({\tilde{A}_{u}})\log|{\tilde{A}_{u}}|)
-time oracle for backtracking from
C_{\tilde{A}_{u}}
to
{\tilde{A}_{u}}
. Note that the time of computing
C_{\tilde{A}_{u}}
is bounded by the cardinality of
S({\tilde{A}_{u}})
, which is
{\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma({\tilde{A}_{u}}))
. Observe that
\pi_{u}C_{{\tilde{A}_{u}}}
is an
\epsilon
-approximate set of
S(\pi_{u}{\tilde{A}_{u}})=S(A_{u})
, and the oracle for backtracking from
C_{{\tilde{A}_{u}}}
to
{\tilde{A}_{u}}
directly yields an
{\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma({\tilde{A}_{u}})\log\Sigma({\tilde{A}_{u}})\log|{\tilde{A}_{u}}|)
-time oracle for backtracking from
\pi_{u}C_{{\tilde{A}_{u}}}
to
A_{u}=\pi_{u}{\tilde{A}_{u}}
. We associate
\pi_{u}C_{\tilde{A}_{u}}
and the oracle for backtracking from
\pi_{u}C_{{\tilde{A}_{u}}}
to
A_{u}
with node
u
.

Note that the processing time of handling one layer-
(k+1)
node, say
u
, is
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma({\tilde{A}_{u}})\log\Sigma({\tilde{A}_{u}})\log|{\tilde{A}_{u}}|)
. Then the total processing time of handling all layer-
(k+1)
nodes is
\sum_{u}{\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma({\tilde{A}_{u}})\log\Sigma({\tilde{A}_{u}})\log|{\tilde{A}_{u}}|)
. Observe that
\pi_{u}=\Theta(\epsilon^{-k/d})
and
\sum_{u}\Sigma(A_{u})=\Sigma(A)
, we have
\sum_{u}\Sigma(\tilde{A}_{u})={\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma(A){\epsilon^{\frac{k}{d}}})
and
\sum_{u}{\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma({\tilde{A}_{u}})\log\Sigma({\tilde{A}_{u}})\log|{\tilde{A}_{u}}|)={\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma(A){\epsilon^{\frac{k}{d}}}\log(|A|)\log(\Sigma(A){\epsilon^{\frac{k}{d}}}))
.

2.

If
k=0
, note that we have computed an
\epsilon
-approximate set for
S(A)
and built an oracle for backtracking from this approximate set to
A
. In the following, we consider the case that
1\leq k\leq\bar{d}
. From layer-
k
to layer-1, we iteratively compute an
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)
-approximate set for each node by using the
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)
-approximate sets of its children: Consider any layer-
\zeta
node
u
, where
1\leq\zeta\leq k
. Let
A_{u}
be the (multi-)set of elements
u
contains, and
{\tilde{A}_{u}}=A_{u}/\pi_{u}
. Let
u_{i}
denote the
i
-th child node of
u
,
A_{u_{i}}
be the set of elements contained in
u_{i}
for
i=1,2,\cdots,m
, and
{\tilde{A}_{u_{i}}}=A_{u_{i}}/\pi_{u_{i}}
.
Suppose we have obtained an
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)
-approximate set with cardinality of
{\mathcal{O}}({1}/{\epsilon})
for each
S({\tilde{A}_{u_{i}}})
, say
C_{{\tilde{A}_{u_{i}}}}
, and meanwhile we have built a
T_{i}
-time oracle for backtracking from
C_{{\tilde{A}_{u_{i}}}}
to
{\tilde{A}_{u_{i}}}
. Then
\pi_{u_{i}}C_{{\tilde{A}_{u_{i}}}}
is an
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)
-approximate set of
S(A_{u_{i}})
, and the oracle for backtracking from
C_{{\tilde{A}_{u_{i}}}}
to
{\tilde{A}_{u_{i}}}
directly yields an
{\mathcal{O}}(T_{i})
-time oracle for backtracking from
\pi_{u_{i}}C_{{\tilde{A}_{u_{i}}}}
to
A_{u_{i}}
. Note that
A_{u}=A_{u_{1}}\dot{\cup}A_{u_{2}}\dot{\cup}\cdots\dot{\cup}A_{u_{m}}
. Recall Corollary
1
and
m={\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{d}})
, in
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon^{1+\frac{1}{d}}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
processing time, we can compute an
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
-approximate set with cardinality of
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
for
S(A_{u})
, denote by
C_{A_{u}}
this approximate set. At the same time, we have built an
{\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma^{m}_{i=1}T_{i}+\frac{1}{\epsilon^{1+\frac{1}{d}}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
-time oracle for backtracking from
C_{A_{u}}
to
A_{u}
. Then we associate
C_{A_{u}}
and the oracle for backtracking from
C_{A_{u}}
to
A_{u}
with node
u
.

Note that there are a total of
{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{(\zeta-1)}{d}})
nodes in layer-
\zeta
, thus the overall processing time in layer-
\zeta
is
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon^{1+\frac{\zeta}{d}}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
. Furthermore, the total processing time from layer-
k
to root is
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon^{1+\frac{k}{d}}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
.

We estimate the overall processing time now. The total processing time is

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle={\mathcal{O}}({\epsilon^{-\frac{(\bar{d}+1)}{d}}}+\bar{d}\cdot|A|)+{\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma(A){\epsilon^{\frac{k}{d}}}\cdot\log(|A|)\cdot\log(\Sigma(A){\epsilon^{\frac{k}{d}}})+{\epsilon^{-(1+\frac{k}{d})}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})

\displaystyle={\mathcal{O}}(d\cdot|A|+\Sigma(A){\epsilon^{\frac{k}{d}}}\cdot\log(|A|)\cdot\log(\Sigma(A){\epsilon^{\frac{k}{d}}})+{\epsilon^{-(1+\frac{k}{d})}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}).

At the root node, we will obtain an
{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{k})
-approximate set with cardinality of
{\mathcal{O}}({1}/{\epsilon})
for
S(A)
. Denote by
C_{A}
this approximate set.

With the help of this tree structure, we derive an oracle for backtracking from
C_{A}
to
A
as follows: given any
c\in C_{A}
, use the oracle associated with the root node to return two numbers from its two child nodes. Backtrace recursively from the root node to leaf nodes, for any number in layer-
h
node, use the oracle associated with this node to return two numbers from its two child nodes. Let
A^{\prime}
be the collection of numbers returned from all leaf nodes. One can easily prove that
A^{\prime}\subset A
satisfies
|\Sigma(A^{\prime})-c|\leq{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{k})\Sigma(A)
. The total processing time for backtracking is
T^{(1)}={\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma(A){\epsilon^{\frac{k}{d}}}\cdot\log(|A|)\cdot\log(\Sigma(A){\epsilon^{\frac{k}{d}}})+{\epsilon^{-(1+\frac{k}{d})}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
, which follows from the following recurrent calculation:
T^{(k+1)}={\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma(A){\epsilon^{\frac{k}{d}}}\log(|A|)\log(\Sigma(A){\epsilon^{\frac{k}{d}}}))
and
T^{(\zeta)}={\mathcal{O}}(T^{(\zeta+1)}+\frac{m^{\zeta-1}}{\epsilon^{1+\frac{1}{d}}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})={\mathcal{O}}(T^{(\zeta+1)}+\frac{1}{\epsilon^{1+\frac{\zeta}{d}}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
for
\zeta=k,k-1,k-2,\cdots,1
. ∎

∎

Sometimes we only care about computing
\omega
-capped subset-sums of smooth numbers and hope to design a customized algorithm whose running time will decrease with the decrease of
\omega
. Towards this, we develop the following Lemma
9
via a similar proof as Lemma
8
.

Lemma 9
.

Given
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
\bar{d}\in\mathbb{N}\cap[0,d-1]
. Let
A
be a multiset of
(\epsilon,d,\bar{d})
-smooth numbers, that is, every element in
A
have been factorized as
h_{1}h_{2}\cdots h_{\bar{d}}h_{\bar{d}+1}
, where
h_{\bar{d}+1}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\cap[1,2\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{d}}]
and if
\bar{d}\geq 1
then
h_{i}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\cap[\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{d}},2\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{d}}]
for
i=1,2,\cdots,\bar{d}
.

Fix
\omega\in[0,\Sigma(A)]
. Then for any
k\in\mathbb{N}\cap[0,\bar{d}]
, in
{\mathcal{O}}\left(\epsilon^{-\frac{(\bar{d}+1)}{d}}+\bar{d}\cdot|A|+\Sigma(A){\epsilon^{\frac{k}{d}}}\log(|A|)\log(\Sigma(A){\epsilon^{\frac{k}{d}}})+2^{2k+1}\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{d}}\omega\log\omega\right)
processing time, we can

(i).

Compute
S(A;[0,\omega])
;

(ii).

Meanwhile build a
T^{1}
-time oracle for backtracking from
S(A;[0,\omega])
to
A
, where
T^{1}={\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma(A){\epsilon^{\frac{k}{d}}}\log(|A|)\log(\Sigma(A){\epsilon^{\frac{k}{d}}})+2^{2k+1}\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{d}}\omega\log\omega)
.

Remark.
Notice that to achieve an optimal time bound in Lemma
9
, one need to choose
k
such that
\max\{\Sigma(A){\epsilon^{\frac{k}{d}}},2^{2k+1}{\epsilon^{\frac{-1}{d}}}{\omega}\}
attains its minimum. We will take a specific value for
k
when we apply Lemma
9
in the future, namely
k={d}/{2}
, where
d
is a positive even number.

Note that Lemma
9
consists of two parts: one is computing capped subset-sums of
A
, and the other is building an oracle for backtracking. Thus Lemma
9
implies an exact algorithm for SUBSET SUM instance
(A,\omega)
, where
A
is a multiset of
(\epsilon,d,\bar{d})
-smooth numbers and
\omega\in[0,\Sigma(A)]
.

Proof of Lemma
9
.

Denote the set of all different integers in
\mathbb{N}\cap[\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{d}},2\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{d}}]
by
\mathcal{P}=\{p_{1},p_{2},\cdots,p_{m}\}
. Then every element of
A
is a multiplication of
\bar{d}+1
integers (which are called factors of the element), with
\bar{d}
integers belonging to
\mathcal{P}
and one extra integer
h_{\bar{d}+1}
that may or may not belong to
\mathcal{P}
. For simplicity, we fix the order of factors of each element arbitrarily (except that the
(\bar{d}+1)
-th factor must be
h_{\bar{d}+1}
if
h_{\bar{d}+1}\not\in\mathcal{P}
) and refers to
h_{i}
as the
i
-th factor.

Now we apply the same method used in the proof of Lemma
4
to build a tree structure of
\bar{d}+1
layers for all elements in
A
as follows:

1.

Let the root node contains all elements in
A
. Root node has layer-
1
.

2.

If
\bar{d}=0
, we stop building and obtain a single node tree structure. Else if
\bar{d}\geq 1
, we proceed to create
m
child nodes of root node by subdividing
A
into
m
groups such that elements in the
i
-th group share
p_{i}
as their first factor (If
A
does not contain an element with
p_{i}
as its first factor, then let the
i
-th group be an empty set).

3.

Suppose we have obtained nodes of layer-
\zeta
, where
2\leq\zeta\leq\bar{d}+1
, such that elements in each node share the same
j
-th factor for
1\leq j\leq\zeta-1
. If
\zeta=\bar{d}+1
, we stop building and obtain a tree structure of
\bar{d}+1
layers. Else if
\zeta\leq\bar{d}
, we create nodes of layer-
(\zeta+1)
as follows. Consider an arbitrary node of layer-
\zeta
, say,
u
. Let
A_{u}\subset A
be the (multi-)set of all the elements contained in node
u
. We create
m
child nodes of
u
by subdividing
A_{u}
into
m
groups such that elements in the
i
-th group share
p_{i}
as their
\zeta
-th factor (If
A_{u}
does not contain an element with
p_{i}
as its
\zeta
-th factor, then let the
i
-th group be an empty set).

For each layer-
\zeta
node
u
, let
A_{u}\subset A
be the (multi-)set of all the elements contained in node
u
. We define
\pi_{u}
as follows: if
\zeta=1
, then
\pi_{u}=1
; else if

2\leq\zeta\leq\bar{d}+1
, then elements in
A_{u}
share the same
j
-th factor for
1\leq j\leq\zeta-1
, we let
\pi_{u}
be the product of these
\zeta-1
factors.

Notice that each node in this tree except the leaf node has a degree of
m={\mathcal{O}}({\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{d}}})
, so there are in total
m^{\zeta-1}={\mathcal{O}}({\epsilon^{-\frac{(\zeta-1)}{d}}})
nodes at the
\zeta
-th layer (called layer-
\zeta
nodes). The time of building this tree is
{\mathcal{O}}({\epsilon^{-\frac{(\bar{d}+1)}{d}}}+\bar{d}\cdot|A|)
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}\cap[0,\bar{d}]
. Next, with the help of this tree structure, we will compute
S(A;[0,\omega])
and build an oracle for backtracking from
S(A;[0,\omega])
to
A
. Note that we
do not start from the leaves of the tree but rather start from layer-
(k+1)
nodes for some parameter
k
that can be optimized when we apply Lemma
9
.

1.

Start from layer-
(k+1)
. Consider any layer-
(k+1)
node
u
. Let
A_{u}
be the (multi-)set of elements it contains and
\tilde{A}_{u}=A_{u}/\pi_{u}
. Recall Lemma
2
, in
{\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma(\tilde{A}_{u})\log\Sigma(\tilde{A}_{u})\log|\tilde{A}_{u}|)
processing time, we can compute
S(\tilde{A}_{u})
and build an
{\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma(\tilde{A}_{u})\log\Sigma(\tilde{A}_{u})\log|\tilde{A}_{u}|)
-time oracle for backtracking from
S(\tilde{A}_{u})
to
\tilde{A}_{u}
. Observe that
A_{u}=\pi_{u}\tilde{A}_{u}
,
S({A}_{u};[0,\omega])=\pi_{u}S(\tilde{A}_{u})\cap[0,\omega]
, and the oracle for backtracking from
S(\tilde{A}_{u})
to
\tilde{A}_{u}
directly yields an
{\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma(\tilde{A}_{u})\log\Sigma(\tilde{A}_{u})\log|\tilde{A}_{u}|)
-time oracle for backtracking from
S(A_{u};[0,\omega])
to
A_{u}
. We associate
S(A_{u};[0,\omega])
and the oracle for backtracking from
S(A_{u};[0,\omega])
to
A_{u}
with node
u
.

Note that the processing time of handling one layer-
(k+1)
node, say
u
, is
{\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma(\tilde{A}_{u})\log\Sigma(\tilde{A}_{u})\log|\tilde{A}_{u}|)
. Then the total processing time of handling all layer-
(k+1)
nodes is
{\mathcal{O}}(\sum_{u}\Sigma(\tilde{A}_{u})\log\Sigma(\tilde{A}_{u})\log|\tilde{A}_{u}|)
.
Observe that
\pi_{u}=\Theta(\epsilon^{-k/d})
and
\sum_{u}\Sigma(A_{u})=\Sigma(A)
, we have
\sum_{u}\Sigma(\tilde{A}_{u})={\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma(A){\epsilon^{\frac{k}{d}}})
and
{\mathcal{O}}(\sum_{u}\Sigma(\tilde{A}_{u})\log\Sigma(\tilde{A}_{u})\log|\tilde{A}_{u}|)={\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma(A){\epsilon^{\frac{k}{d}}}\log(|A|)\log(\Sigma(A){\epsilon^{\frac{k}{d}}}))
.

2.

If
k=0
, note that we have computed
S(A;[0,\omega])
and build an oracle for backtracking from
S(A;[0,\omega])
to
A
. In the following, we consider the case that
1\leq k\leq\bar{d}
. From layer-
k
to layer-1, we iteratively compute the capped subset-sums for each node by using the capped subset-sums of its children: Consider any layer-
\zeta\leq k
node
u
, where
1\leq\zeta\leq k
. Let
A_{u}
be the (multi-)set of elements
u
contains, and
{\tilde{A}_{u}}=A_{u}/\pi_{u}
. For
i=1,2,\cdots,m
, let
u_{i}
denote the
i
-th child node of
u
,
A_{u_{i}}
be the set of elements contained in
u_{i}
and
\tilde{A}_{u_{i}}=A_{u_{i}}/\pi_{u_{i}}
. We have
\pi_{u_{i}}=\pi_{u}\cdot p_{i}
for every
i=1,2,\cdots,m
and
\dot{\cup}^{m}_{i=1}p_{i}\tilde{A}_{u_{i}}=\tilde{A}_{u}
. Note that to compute
S(A_{u};[0,\omega])
, we only need to compute
S(\tilde{A}_{u};[0,\frac{\omega}{\pi_{u}}])
. Observe that
S(\tilde{A}_{u};[0,\frac{\omega}{\pi_{u}}])=\left(\oplus^{m}_{i=1}S(p_{i}\tilde{A}_{u_{i}};[0,\frac{\omega}{\pi_{u}}])\right)\cap[0,\frac{\omega}{\pi_{u}}]
. Recall Lemma
3
, in
{\mathcal{O}}(m\frac{\omega}{\pi_{u}}\log\frac{\omega}{\pi_{u}})
processing time, we can compute
S(\tilde{A}_{u};[0,\frac{\omega}{\pi_{u}}])
and meanwhile build an
{\mathcal{O}}(m\frac{\omega}{\pi_{u}}\log\frac{\omega}{\pi_{u}})
-time oracle for backtracking from
S(\tilde{A}_{u};[0,\frac{\omega}{\pi_{u}}])
to
\dot{\cup}^{m}_{i=1}S(p_{i}\tilde{A}_{u_{i}};[0,\frac{\omega}{\pi_{u}}])
. Suppose for each
\tilde{A}_{u_{i}}
, we have obtained
S(\tilde{A}_{u_{i}};[0,\frac{\omega}{\pi_{u_{i}}}])
and built a
T_{i}
-time oracle for backtracking from
S(\tilde{A}_{u_{i}};[0,\frac{\omega}{\pi_{u_{i}}}])
to
\tilde{A}_{u_{i}}
. Notice that
\left(p_{i}S(\tilde{A}_{u_{i}};[0,\frac{\omega}{\pi_{u_{i}}}])\right)\cap[0,\frac{\omega}{\pi_{u}}]=S(p_{i}\tilde{A}_{u_{i}};[0,\frac{\omega}{\pi_{u}}])
, moreover, the oracle for backtracking from
S(\tilde{A}_{u_{i}};[0,\frac{\omega}{\pi_{u_{i}}}])
to
\tilde{A}_{u_{i}}
directly yields an
{\mathcal{O}}(T_{i})
-time oracle for backtracking from
S(p_{i}\tilde{A}_{u_{i}};[0,\frac{\omega}{\pi_{u}}])
to
p_{i}\tilde{A}_{u_{i}}
. Combine with the oracle for backtracking from
S(\tilde{A}_{u};[0,\frac{\omega}{\pi_{u}}])
to
\dot{\cup}^{m}_{i=1}S(p_{i}\tilde{A}_{u_{i}};[0,\frac{\omega}{\pi_{u}}])
, we will obtain an
{\mathcal{O}}(\sum^{m}_{i=1}T_{i}+m\frac{\omega}{\pi_{u}}\log\frac{\omega}{\pi_{u}})
-time oracle for backtracking from
S(\tilde{A}_{u};[0,\frac{\omega}{\pi_{u}}])
to
\tilde{A}_{u}
. Then we associate
S(\tilde{A}_{u};[0,\frac{\omega}{\pi_{u}}])
and the oracle for backtracking from
S(\tilde{A}_{u};[0,\frac{\omega}{\pi_{u}}])
to
\tilde{A}_{u}
with node
u
.

Recall that
\pi_{u}\in\mathbb{N}\cap[\frac{\epsilon^{-\frac{\zeta-1}{d}}}{2^{\zeta-1}},2^{\zeta-1}\epsilon^{-\frac{\zeta-1}{d}}]
and
m\in\mathbb{N}\cap[\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{d}},2\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{d}}]
. Note that the processing time of handling one layer-
\zeta
node, say
u
, is
{\mathcal{O}}(m\frac{\omega}{\pi_{u}}\log\frac{\omega}{\pi_{u}})
. Since there are a total of
m^{\zeta-1}
nodes in layer-
\zeta
, the total processing time of handling all layer-
\zeta
nodes is
{\mathcal{O}}(\sum_{u}m\frac{\omega}{\pi_{u}}\log\frac{\omega}{\pi_{u}})={\mathcal{O}}(m^{\zeta}\sum_{u}\frac{\omega}{\pi_{u}}\log\frac{\omega}{\pi_{u}})={\mathcal{O}}(m^{\zeta}2^{\zeta-1}\epsilon^{\frac{\zeta-1}{d}}\omega\log\omega)
, furthermore, the overall processing time of handling nodes from layer-
k
to root is
{\mathcal{O}}(2^{2k+1}\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{d}}\omega\log\omega)
.

We estimate the overall processing time now. The total processing time is

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle={\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{(\bar{d}+1)}{d}}+\bar{d}\cdot|A|)+{\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma(A){\epsilon^{\frac{k}{d}}}\log(|A|)\log(\Sigma(A){\epsilon^{\frac{k}{d}}}))+{\mathcal{O}}(2^{2k+1}\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{d}}\omega\log\omega)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{(\bar{d}+1)}{d}}+\bar{d}\cdot|A|+\Sigma(A){\epsilon^{\frac{k}{d}}}\log(|A|)\log(\Sigma(A){\epsilon^{\frac{k}{d}}})+2^{2k+1}\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{d}}\omega\log\omega).

At root node, we will obtain
S(A;[0,\omega])
.

With the help of this tree structure, we derive an oracle for backtracking from
S(A;[0,\omega])
to
A
as follows: given any
c\in S(A;[0,\omega])
, use the oracle associated with the root node to return two numbers from its two child nodes. Backtrace recursively from the root node to leaf nodes, for any number in layer-
h
node, use the oracle associated with this node to return two numbers from its two child nodes. Let
A^{\prime}
be the collection of numbers returned from all leaf nodes. One can easily prove that
A^{\prime}\subset A
satisfies
\Sigma(A^{\prime})=c
. The total processing time for backtracking is
T^{1}={\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma(A){\epsilon^{\frac{k}{d}}}\log(|A|)\log(\Sigma(A){\epsilon^{\frac{k}{d}}})+2^{2k+1}\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{d}}\omega\log\omega)
, which follows from the following recurrent calculation:
T^{k+1}={\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma(A){\epsilon^{\frac{k}{d}}}\log(|A|)\log(\Sigma(A){\epsilon^{\frac{k}{d}}}))
and
T^{\zeta}={\mathcal{O}}(T^{\zeta+1}+m^{\zeta}2^{\zeta-1}\epsilon^{\frac{\zeta-1}{d}}\omega\log\omega)\text{ \ for \ }\zeta=k,k-1,k-2,\cdots,1
. ∎

∎

5
Preprocessing.

Let
\epsilon>0
be a sufficiently small number. Given any SUBSET SUM instance
(X,t)
and let
OPT
be the optimal objective value of
(X,t)
. In this section, we focus on simplifying
(X,t)
where
OPT\geq t/2
and show that we can construct a reduced instance
(F,\hat{t})
. Formally, we have the following lemma.

Lemma 10
.

Given any SUBSET SUM instance
(X,t)
, let
OPT
be the optimal objective value of
(X,t)
. Assume that
OPT\geq t/2
, then in
{\mathcal{O}}((|X|+\frac{1}{\epsilon})(\log|X|)^{2}(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{{\mathcal{O}}(d)})
processing time, we can

(i).

Obtain a modified SUBSET SUM instance
(F,\hat{t})
satisfying the following conditions:

(I)

\Sigma(F)\leq\frac{4(1+\epsilon)\Sigma(X)}{\epsilon^{3}t}
and
\hat{t}=\frac{4(1+\epsilon)}{\epsilon^{3}}
.

(II)

The optimal objective value of
(F,\hat{t})
is at least
\frac{4(1-\epsilon)}{\epsilon^{3}\cdot t}\cdot(OPT-\frac{\epsilon t}{2})
.

(III)

|F|\leq|X|
and
F
has been divided into
{\mathcal{O}}((\log|X|)^{2}(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{{\mathcal{O}}(d)})
subgroups:
F^{(j;i;k)}_{p}
’s.

(IV)

Each subgroup
F^{(j;i;k)}_{p}
satisfies the followings:

(a)

Elements in
F^{(j;i;k)}_{p}
are different from each other;

(b)

F^{(j;i;k)}_{p}\subset[\frac{(1-\epsilon)2^{p+j-1}}{\epsilon^{2}},\frac{(1+\epsilon)2^{p+j}}{\epsilon^{2}})
;

(c)

F^{(j;i;k)}_{p}=2^{p}\rho^{j}_{k}\overline{F}^{(j;i;k)}_{p}
, where
\overline{F}^{(j;i;k)}_{p}\subset\mathbb{N}_{+}\cap[\frac{1}{4\epsilon},\frac{1}{\epsilon}]

and
\overline{F}^{(j;i;k)}_{p}
is a set of
(\epsilon,d,d-1)
-smooth numbers, that is, every element in
\overline{F}^{(j;i;k)}_{p}
has been factorized as
h_{1}h_{2}\cdots h_{d}
, where
h_{d}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\cap[1,2\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{d}}]
and if
d\geq 2
then
h_{i}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\cap[\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{d}},2{\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{d}}}]
for
i=1,2,\cdots,d-1
.

(ii).

Meanwhile build an oracle for backtracking from
F
to
X
. Precisely, given subset
U^{(j;i;k)}_{p}\subset F^{(j;i;k)}_{p}
for every
F^{(j;i;k)}_{p}
and let
F^{\prime}
denote the multiset-union of all
U^{(j;i;k)}_{p}
’s. Then
F^{\prime}\subset F
and in linear time, the oracle will return a subset
X^{\prime}\subset X
satisfying
|\frac{\epsilon^{3}t}{4}\Sigma(F^{\prime})-\Sigma(X^{\prime})|\leq\epsilon\Sigma(X^{\prime})
.

Remark.
Note that optimal objective value of
(F,\hat{t})
is at least
\frac{4(1-\epsilon)}{\epsilon^{3}\cdot t}\cdot(OPT-\frac{\epsilon t}{2})
and
OPT\geq\frac{t}{2}
. The second part of Lemma
10
guarantees that towards finding a weak
(1-\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon))
-approximation of SUBSET SUM instance
(X,t)
, it is sufficient to find a weak
(1-\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon))
-approximation of modified instance
(F,\hat{t})
.

The rest of this section is dedicated to proving Lemma
10
. We will step-by-step modify the given SUBSET SUM instance
(X,t)
, and Lemma
10
follows directly after all the modification operations.

Step 1: Handling small elements.

We call an element
x\in X
a
small
element if and only
x\in[0,\frac{\epsilon t}{4})
, otherwise we call
x
a
large
element. Let
X^{s}
denote all small elements in
X
, then
X\backslash X^{s}
is the set of all large elements in
X
.

We first greedily divide
X^{s}
into the subgroups
X^{s}_{1},X^{s}_{2},X^{s}_{3},\cdots,X^{s}_{\xi}
, such that
\Sigma(X^{s}_{i})\in[\frac{\epsilon t}{4},\frac{\epsilon t}{2})(\forall i)
. Let
\mathcal{L}:=\{X^{s}_{1},X^{s}_{2},X^{s}_{3},\cdots,X^{s}_{\xi}\}\dot{\cup}\left\{\{x\}:x\in X\backslash X^{s}\right\}
, which gives a division of
X
. Then we define multiset
Z:=\{z_{1},z_{2},\cdots,z_{\xi},z_{\xi+1},\cdots,z_{n^{\prime}}\},
where
z_{i}=\Sigma(X^{s}_{i})
for
1\leq i\leq\xi
and
\{z_{\xi+1},\cdots,z_{n^{\prime}}\}=X\backslash X^{s}
. Note that
|Z|\leq|X|
,
\Sigma(Z)=\Sigma(X)
and
Z^{\min}\geq\frac{\epsilon t}{4}
.

The total processing time of Step 1 is
{\mathcal{O}}(|X|)
. We have the following observation.

Observation 6
.

Let
Z
be defined above. Given any
A\subset X
, there exists
B\subset Z
such that

\Sigma(A)-\frac{\epsilon t}{2}\leq\Sigma(B)\leq\Sigma(A)
. Moreover, given any
B^{\prime}\subset Z
, there exists
A^{\prime}\subset X
such that
\Sigma(B^{\prime})=\Sigma(A^{\prime})
.

Proof.

For any
A\subset X
, it holds that
\Sigma(A\cap X^{s})\leq\Sigma(X^{s})=\sum^{\xi}_{i=1}(z_{i})
. Since
z_{i}\in[\frac{\epsilon t}{4},\frac{\epsilon t}{2})
for
1\leq i\leq\xi
, we can greedily pick elements in
\{z_{1},z_{2},\cdots,z_{\xi}\}
such that the summation of all picked elements is within
[\Sigma(A\cap X^{s})-\frac{\epsilon t}{2},\Sigma(A\cap X^{s}))
. Denote by
\hat{Z}
the set of all picked items. It follows that
\hat{Z}\dot{\cup}(A\backslash X^{s})\subset Z
and
\Sigma(\hat{Z})\in[\Sigma(A\cap X^{s})-\frac{\epsilon t}{2},\Sigma(A\cap X^{s}))
, moreover, we have

\displaystyle\Sigma(A)-\frac{\epsilon t}{2}

\displaystyle=\Sigma(A\cap X^{s})-\frac{\epsilon t}{2}+\Sigma(A\backslash X^{s})

\displaystyle\leq\Sigma(\hat{Z})+\Sigma(A\backslash X^{s})=\Sigma(\hat{Z}\dot{\cup}(A\backslash X^{s}))

\displaystyle\leq\Sigma(A\cap X^{s})+\Sigma(A\backslash X^{s})=\Sigma(A)

Now consider the second part of the observation. Given any
B^{\prime}\subset Z
, let
B^{\prime\prime}=B^{\prime}\cap\{z_{1},z_{2},\cdots,z_{\xi}\}
. For each
z\in B^{\prime\prime}
, there exists a corresponding
X_{z}\subset X
such that
\Sigma(X_{z})=z
. Then we have
(\dot{\cup}_{z\in B^{\prime\prime}}X_{z})\dot{\cup}(B^{\prime}\backslash B^{\prime\prime})\subset X
and
\Sigma((\dot{\cup}_{z\in B^{\prime\prime}}X_{z})\dot{\cup}(B^{\prime}\backslash B^{\prime\prime}))=\Sigma(\dot{\cup}_{z\in B^{\prime\prime}}X_{z})+\Sigma(B^{\prime}\backslash B^{\prime\prime})=\sum_{z\in B^{\prime\prime}}(z)+\Sigma(B^{\prime}\backslash B^{\prime\prime})=\Sigma(B^{\prime}).
∎∎

Recall that the optimal objective value of
(X,t)
is
OPT
. Observation
6
guarantees that there exists subset
Z^{\prime}\subset Z
such that
OPT-\frac{\epsilon t}{2}\leq\Sigma(Z^{\prime})\leq t
, then the optimal objective value of SUBSET SUM instance
(Z,t)
is at least
OPT-\frac{\epsilon t}{2}
.

Step 2: Scaling and Grouping.

We scale
t
and
each element in
Z
by
\frac{\epsilon^{3}t}{4}
. To be specific, we scale

t
to
\bar{t}=t\cdot\frac{4}{\epsilon^{3}\cdot t}
and scale each
z_{i}\in Z
to
\bar{z}_{i}=z_{i}\cdot\frac{4}{\epsilon^{3}\cdot t}
. Let
\bar{Z}=\{\bar{z}_{1},\bar{z}_{2},\cdots,\bar{z}_{n^{\prime}}\}\cap[0,\bar{t}]
. Observe that there exists
\bar{Z}^{\prime}\subset\bar{Z}
such that
\frac{4}{\epsilon^{3}\cdot t}(OPT-\frac{\epsilon t}{2})\leq\Sigma(\bar{Z}^{\prime})\leq\bar{t}
, hence the optimal objective value of SUBSET SUM instance
(\bar{Z},\bar{t})
is at least
\frac{4}{\epsilon^{3}\cdot t}(OPT-\frac{\epsilon t}{2})
.

Recall that
\mathcal{L}=\{X^{s}_{1},X^{s}_{2},X^{s}_{3},\cdots,X^{s}_{\xi}\}\dot{\cup}\left\{\{x\}:x\in X\backslash X^{s}\right\}
and each element in
\mathcal{L}
generates one element of
\bar{Z}
, thus there is a one-to-one correspondence between
\mathcal{L}
and
\bar{Z}
:
ele\leftrightarrow\frac{4}{\epsilon^{3}\cdot t}\Sigma(ele)
for any
ele\in\mathcal{L}
. For each
y\in set(\bar{Z})
, let
\mathcal{L}_{y}:=\{ele\in\mathcal{L}\ :\ \frac{4}{\epsilon^{3}\cdot t}\Sigma(ele)=y\}
. We define the mapping
\Phi_{1}
from
\bar{Z}
to
\mathcal{L}
as follows: given any
\bar{Z}^{\prime}\subset\bar{Z}
, for every
y\in set(\bar{Z}^{\prime})
, recall that
card_{\bar{Z}^{\prime}}[y]
refers the multiplicity of
y
in
\bar{Z}^{\prime}
, mapping
\Phi_{1}
returns any
card_{\bar{Z}^{\prime}}[y]
elements in
\mathcal{L}_{y}
. Let
\Phi_{1}(\bar{Z}^{\prime})
be the collection of all elements returned by
\Phi_{1}
given
\bar{Z}^{\prime}
.

Notice that
\Sigma(\bar{Z})\leq\frac{4\Sigma(X)}{\epsilon^{3}t}
and
|\bar{Z}|\leq|Z|\leq|X|
. Moreover, note that multiset
\bar{Z}\subset[\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}},\frac{4}{\epsilon^{3}}]
, we can divide
\bar{Z}
into
\eta={\mathcal{O}}(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
groups, denoted by
Y_{1},Y_{2},\cdots,Y_{\eta}
, such that
y\in Y_{j}
if and only if
y\in[\frac{2^{j-1}}{\epsilon^{2}},\frac{2^{j}}{\epsilon^{2}})\cap\bar{Z}
.

The total processing time of Step 2 is
{\mathcal{O}}(|X|)
.

Step 3: Rounding and Further Grouping.

For each multiset
Y_{j}
, it holds
Y_{j}\subset[\frac{2^{j-1}}{\epsilon^{2}},\frac{2^{j}}{\epsilon^{2}})
. We can rewrite
[\frac{2^{j-1}}{\epsilon^{2}},\frac{2^{j}}{\epsilon^{2}})
as
[\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda_{j}}},\frac{2}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda_{j}}})
, where
0\leq\lambda_{j}<2
. Recall Lemma
6
, given
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
\alpha_{j}=1+\lambda_{j}
, in
{\mathcal{O}}((|Y_{j}|+\frac{1}{\epsilon})\cdot\log(|Y_{j}|)\cdot(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{{\mathcal{O}}(d)})
time, we can obtain a set
\Delta_{j}\subset\mathbb{R}
with
\Delta_{j}\subset\Theta(\frac{1}{\epsilon^{1+\lambda_{j}}})
and
|\Delta_{j}|={\mathcal{O}}(\log(|Y_{j}|)\cdot(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{{\mathcal{O}}(d)})
, moreover, we can round every
y\in Y_{j}
to the form
\rho h_{1}h_{2}\cdots h_{d}
, where
\rho\in\Delta_{j}
and
h_{i}
’s satisfy the following conditions:

\displaystyle h_{1}h_{2}\cdots h_{d}\in\mathbb{N}\cap[\frac{1}{4\epsilon},\frac{1}{\epsilon}]\text{ \ and \ }h_{i}\in\mathbb{N}\cap[\frac{1}{2\epsilon^{\frac{1}{d}}},\frac{2}{\epsilon^{\frac{1}{d}}}]\ \text{ for}\ 1\leq i\leq d;

(4a)

\displaystyle|y-\rho h_{1}h_{2}\cdots h_{d}|\leq\epsilon y.

(4b)

Let
\bar{Y}_{j}
denote the set of all such rounded elements obtained from
Y_{j}
. Consider
\dot{\cup}^{\eta}_{j=1}\bar{Y}_{j}
, we have
|\dot{\cup}^{\eta}_{j=1}\bar{Y}_{j}|\leq\sum^{\eta}_{j=1}|{Y}_{j}|=|\bar{Z}|\leq|X|
. Recall that the optimal objective value of
(\bar{Z},\bar{t})
is at least
\frac{4}{\epsilon^{3}\cdot t}(OPT-\frac{\epsilon t}{2})
.
Let
\hat{t}=(1+\epsilon)\bar{t}=\frac{4(1+\epsilon)}{\epsilon^{3}}
. Condition (
4b
) guarantees that
\Sigma(\dot{\cup}^{\eta}_{j=1}\bar{Y}_{j})\leq\frac{4(1+\epsilon)\Sigma(X)}{\epsilon^{3}t}
and
the optimal objective value of SUBSET SUM instance
(\dot{\cup}^{\eta}_{j=1}\bar{Y}_{j},\hat{t})
is at least
\frac{4(1-\epsilon)}{\epsilon^{3}\cdot t}(OPT-\frac{\epsilon t}{2})
.

Notice that for each
\bar{Y}^{j}
, there is a one-to-one correspondence between
{Y}^{j}
and
\bar{Y}^{j}
:
y\leftrightarrow\bar{y}
for any
y\in Y_{j}
, where
\bar{y}
is the factorized form of
y
obtained after the above rounding procedure. For each
\bar{y}\in\bar{Y}^{j}
, let
\mathcal{C}^{j}_{\bar{y}}
denote the set of all numbers in
Y_{j}
factorized to the form
\bar{y}
. We define the mapping
\Phi^{j}_{2}
from
\bar{Y}_{j}
to
{Y}_{j}
as follows: given any
\bar{Y}^{\prime}_{j}\subset\bar{Y}_{j}
, for every
\bar{y}\in set(\bar{Y}^{\prime}_{j})
, mapping
\Phi^{j}_{2}
returns any
card_{\bar{Y}^{\prime}_{j}}[\bar{y}]
elements in
\mathcal{C}^{j}_{\bar{y}}
. Let
\Phi^{j}_{2}(\bar{Y}^{\prime}_{j})
be the collection of all elements returned by
\Phi^{j}_{2}
given
\bar{Y}^{\prime}_{j}
.

For each
\bar{Y}_{j}
, let
\Delta_{j}=\{\rho^{j}_{1},\rho^{j}_{2},\cdots,\rho^{j}_{|\Delta_{j}|}\}
, where
|\Delta_{j}|={\mathcal{O}}(\log(|Y_{j}|)\cdot(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{{\mathcal{O}}(d)})
. We further divide
\bar{Y}_{j}
into
|\Delta_{j}|
