groups, denoted by
\bar{Y}^{j}_{1},\bar{Y}^{j}_{2},\cdots,\bar{Y}^{j}_{|\Delta_{j}|}
, such that

y\in\bar{Y}^{j}_{k}
if and only if
y
is of the form
\rho^{j}_{k}h_{1}h_{2}\cdots h_{d}
.

The total processing time of Step 3 is
{\mathcal{O}}((|X|+\frac{1}{\epsilon})(\log(|X|))^{2}(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{{\mathcal{O}}(d+1)})
.

Step 4: From Multiset to (almost) Set.

Here, we show that a multiset can be reduced to an alternative multiset with multiplicity at most 2. Towards this, we need the following lemma, which was introduced in
[
27
]
and also used in
[
32
]
. We copy it here with a slight extension.

Lemma 11
.

Given a multiset
A
of positive integers, where
|A|=n
and
|set(A)|=n^{\prime}
. In
{\mathcal{O}}(n(\log n)^{2})
processing time, one can
divide
A
into
m
subgroups
A_{1},A_{2},\cdots,A_{m}
such that
\Sigma(A_{\iota})=2^{p_{\iota}}A^{\max}_{\iota}
, where
p_{\iota}\in\mathbb{N}\cap[0,\log n]
and
\iota=1,2\cdots,m
. Moreover,
B=\{\Sigma(A_{\iota}):\iota=1,2,\cdots,m\}
is a multiset satisfying: (i)
S(A)=S(B)
; (ii)
|B|\leq|A|
and
|B|={\mathcal{O}}(n^{\prime}\log n)
; (iii) no element in
B
has multiplicity exceeding two.

Proof of Lemma
11
.

The proof idea essentially comes from [
[
32
]
, Lemma 4.1], in which the method for computing
B
was provided. For the completeness of this paper, we present it here. Given a multiset
A
of positive integers, where
|A|=n
and
|set(A)|=n^{\prime}
. Copy the elements of
A
into a working multiset
T
and let
B
initially be the empty set. For any
x\in T
with at most
2
copies, i.e.,
card_{T}[x]\leq 2
, delete all of
x
from
T
, then add all of them into
B
. For any
x\in T
with at least 3 copies, i.e.,
card_{T}[x]=2k+num^{x}_{T}
where
k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
num^{x}_{T}=1\ \text{or}\ 2
. Delete all of
x
from
T
, then add
num^{x}_{T}
copies of
x
into
B
and add
k
copies of
2x
into
T
. Iterate over numbers in
set(T)
from the smallest one and perform the procedure as described above. We remark that the method derived in
[
32
]
only returns the set
B
, it does not specify the
A_{\iota}
’s. In particular, it requires some extra effort to get the corresponding division of
A
without exploding the running time. Below we present the details.

We introduce a special data structure, which is “dictionary”(see, e.g.
[
14
]
), to store the division of
A
. Dictionary data structure is used to store data in the key-value pair format. When presented with a key, the dictionary will simply return the associated value. The biggest advantage of this data structure is that the time complexity of inserting, deleting or searching element is
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
. We first build two working dictionaries,
\mathscr{D}^{*}
and
\mathscr{D}
, where
\mathscr{D}^{*}
is the empty dictionary and
\mathscr{D}
is initialized as follows: each element in
\mathscr{D}
is a key-value pair
(x,\mathscr{D}[x])
, where
x\in set(A)
and
\mathscr{D}[x]
is the multiset satisfying (1).
set(\mathscr{D}[x])=\left\{\{x\}\right\}
; (2).
card_{\mathscr{D}[x]}[\{x\}]=card_{A}[x]
; (3). the collection of all keys in
\mathscr{D}
is
set(A)
. Note that the multiset-union of all values in
\mathscr{D}
, i.e,
\mathop{\dot{\cup}}\limits_{x\in set(A)}(\mathscr{D}[x])
, gives a division of
A
.

Let
B
initially be the empty set. Copy the elements of
A
into a working multiset
T
.
We introduce the min heap data structure (see, e.g.
[
14
]
) to maintain
T
. “Min heap” is a specialized tree-based data structure satisfying the following properties: (1). each node is a key-value pair; (2). for any given node
C
, if
P
is a parent node of
P
, the key of
P
is less than or equal to the key of
C
. Thus the key of the root node is the smallest among all nodes. It takes linear time to build a min heap from a given array. For a min heap with
m
nodes, the time complexity of deleting the root node (while keeping the min heap properties) and updating any node is
{\mathcal{O}}(\log m)
. We maintain the elements of
T
in a min heap
D
, where
D
is initialized as follows: each node in
D
is a key-value pair
(x,card_{T}[x])
where
x\in set(T)
, moreover, the collection of all keys in
D
is
set(T)
.

In each iteration, extract the root node
(x,card_{T}[x])
from the heap
D
.

•

If
card_{T}[x]\leq 2
, we first delete all
x
from
T
and insert the key-value pair
(x,\mathscr{D}^{*}[x])
into dictionary
\mathscr{D}^{*}
, where
\mathscr{D}^{*}[x]=\mathscr{D}[x]
. Meanwhile, we add
card_{T}[x]
copies of
x
into
B
. Then we delect key-value pair
(x,\mathscr{D}[x])
from
\mathscr{D}
.

The algorithm continues to the next iteration.

•

Else if
card_{T}[x]>2
, then
card_{T}[x]=2k+num_{T}^{x}
, where
k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
num^{T}_{x}=1\ \text{or}\ 2
. We first delete all
x
from
T
, then add
k
copies of
2x
into
T
. Meanwhile, we add
num_{T}^{x}
copies of
x
into
B
. We then update Dictionary
\mathscr{D}
, Dictionary
\mathscr{D}^{*}
and heap
D
as follows.

–

Consider the key-value pair
(x,\mathscr{D}[x])
in Dictionary
\mathscr{D}
. Select any
num^{T}_{x}
elements from
\mathscr{D}[x]
, denote by
ELE
the set of these selected elements. Define
\mathcal{L}_{2x}
as follows: divide
\mathscr{D}[x]\backslash ELE
into
k
subgroups
Gr_{1},Gr_{2},\cdots,Gr_{k}
such that each subgroup contains exact 2 elements, then
\mathcal{L}_{2x}:=\{\mathop{\dot{\cup}}\limits_{ele\in Gr_{i}}ele:i=1,2,\cdots,k\}
.

–

Insert the key-value pair
(x,\mathscr{D}^{*}[x])
into dictionary
\mathscr{D}^{*}
, where
\mathscr{D}^{*}[x]=ELE
. If key
2x
is already contained in heap
D
, increase the value corresponding to key
2x
in heap
D
by
k
, meanwhile update key-value pair
(2x,\mathscr{D}[2x])
in
\mathscr{D}
by inserting all elements in
\mathcal{L}_{2x}
into
\mathscr{D}[2x]
. Else if
2x
is not already in heap
D
, add
(2x,k)
into heap
D
and add key-value pair
(2x,\mathscr{D}[2x])
into
\mathscr{D}
, where
\mathscr{D}[2x]=\mathcal{L}_{2x}
. Delect key-value pair
(x,\mathscr{D}[x])
from
\mathscr{D}
.

The algorithm now continues to the next iteration.

Let
K_{\mathscr{D}}
and
K_{\mathscr{D}^{*}}
denote the collections of keys in
\mathscr{D}
and
\mathscr{D}^{*}
, respectively. At the end of each iteration, we have the following observations:

1.

S(B\dot{\cup}T)=S(A)
.

2.

Consider any key-value pair in
\mathscr{D}
, say
(x,\mathscr{D}[x])
. Each
ele\in\mathscr{D}[x]
is a subset of
A
satisfying
\Sigma(ele)=x
.

3.

Consider any key-value pair in
\mathscr{D}^{*}
, say
(y,\mathscr{D}^{*}[y])
. Each
ele\in\mathscr{D}^{*}[y]
is a subset of
A
satisfying
\Sigma(ele)=2^{p}\cdot(ele)^{\max}=y
, where
p\in\mathbb{N}\cap[0,\log_{2}|A|]
. Moreover,
\mathscr{D}^{*}[y]
contains at most
2
elements.

4.

Elements in
(\mathop{\dot{\cup}}\limits_{x\in K_{\mathscr{D}}}\mathscr{D}[x])\dot{\cup}(\mathop{\dot{\cup}}\limits_{y\in K_{\mathscr{D}^{*}}}\mathscr{D}^{*}[y])
form a division of
A
. That is, elements in
(\mathop{\dot{\cup}}\limits_{x\in K_{\mathscr{D}}}\mathscr{D}[x])\dot{\cup}(\mathop{\dot{\cup}}\limits_{y\in K_{\mathscr{D}^{*}}}\mathscr{D}^{*}[y])
are subsets of
A
and
\Sigma(A)=\sum\limits_{ele\in(\mathop{\dot{\cup}}\limits_{x\in K_{\mathscr{D}}}\mathscr{D}[x])\dot{\cup}(\mathop{\dot{\cup}}\limits_{y\in K_{\mathscr{D}^{*}}}\mathscr{D}^{*}[y])}\Sigma(ele)
.

By the time the iteration procedure stops, heap
D
, multiset
T
and dictionary
\mathscr{D}
are empty, we will obtain the final dictionary
\mathscr{D}^{*}
. Then elements in
\mathop{\dot{\cup}}\limits_{key\in K_{\mathscr{D}^{*}}}\mathscr{D}^{*}[key]
form a division of
A
. Notice that
B=\{\Sigma(ele):ele\in\mathop{\dot{\cup}}\limits_{key\in K_{\mathscr{D}}}\mathscr{D}[key]\}
. One can easily prove that
B
satisfies (i).
S(A)=S(B)
; (ii).
|B|\leq|A|
and
|B|={\mathcal{O}}(|set(A)|\log_{2}|A|)
.

We estimate the overall processing time now. Initialization takes
{\mathcal{O}}(|A|)
time. Notice that in each iteration, we will add one key-value pair into
\mathscr{D}^{*}
, thus the number of iterations is
{\mathcal{O}}(|K_{\mathscr{D}^{*}}|)={\mathcal{O}}(|set(A)|\log|A|)
. In each iteration, it takes
O(\log|A|)
time for updating
D
,
B
,
T
and
\mathscr{D}^{*}
, then the total time for handling
D
,
B
,
T
and
\mathscr{D}^{*}
is
{\mathcal{O}}(|set(A)|(\log|A|)^{2})
. Moreover, a careful analysis shows that the total time for updating
\mathscr{D}
through all iterations is
{\mathcal{O}}(\sum_{x\in A}card_{A}[x]\cdot\log(card_{A}[x]))={\mathcal{O}}(|A|\log|A|)
. To summarize, the overall processing time is
{\mathcal{O}}(|A|(\log|A|)^{2})
.∎∎

Back to our preprocessing procedure. For each
\bar{Y}^{j}_{k}
, according to Lemma
11
, in
{\mathcal{O}}(|\bar{Y}^{j}_{k}|(\log|\bar{Y}^{j}_{k}|)^{2})
time, we can divide
\bar{Y}^{j}_{k}
into
m^{k}_{j}={\mathcal{O}}(|set(\bar{Y}^{j}_{k})|\log|\bar{Y}^{j}_{k}|)
groups
\bar{Y}^{(j;1)}_{k},\bar{Y}^{(j;2)}_{k},\cdots,\bar{Y}^{(j;m^{k}_{j})}_{k}
such that

\bar{F}^{j}_{k}=\{\Sigma(\bar{Y}^{(j;1)}_{k}),\Sigma(\bar{Y}^{(j;2)}_{k}),\cdots,\Sigma(\bar{Y}^{(j;m^{k}_{j})}_{k})\}
is a multiset satisfying the followings:

\displaystyle\Sigma(\bar{Y}^{(j;\iota)}_{k})=2^{p_{\iota}}\cdot(\bar{Y}^{(j;\iota)}_{k})^{\max},\ \text{where}\ {p_{\iota}}\in\mathbb{N}\cap[0,\log_{2}|\bar{Y}^{j}_{k}|]\ \text{and}\ \iota=1,2,\cdots,m^{k}_{j}.

(5a)

\displaystyle S(\bar{F}^{j}_{k})=S(\bar{Y}^{j}_{k})\text{ \ and \ }|\bar{F}^{j}_{k}|\leq|\bar{Y}^{j}_{k}|;

(5b)

\displaystyle\text{no element in ${\bar{F}^{j}_{k}}$ has multiplicity exceeding two};

(5c)

According to (
5a
), for each number in
\bar{F}^{j}_{k}
, we can factorize it to the form
2^{p}y
, where
p\in\mathbb{N}\cap[0,\log_{2}|\bar{Y}^{j}_{k}|]
and
y\in\bar{Y}^{j}_{k}
. Let
F^{j}_{k}
denote the set of all such factorized elements obtained from
\bar{F}^{j}_{k}
.

Let
\mathcal{H}^{(j;k)}:=\{\bar{Y}^{(j;1)}_{k},\bar{Y}^{(j;2)}_{k},\cdots,\bar{Y}^{(j;m^{k}_{j})}_{k}\}
. Notice that for each
F^{j}_{k}
, there is a one-to-one correspondence between
\mathcal{H}^{(j;k)}
and
F^{j}_{k}
:
ele\leftrightarrow\Sigma(ele)=2^{pow(\frac{\Sigma(ele)}{ele^{\max}})}(ele)^{\max}
for any
ele\in\mathcal{H}^{(j;k)}
. For each
f\in F^{j}_{k}
, let
\mathcal{H}^{(j;k)}_{f}:=\{ele\in\mathcal{H}^{(j;k)}\ :\ \Sigma(ele)=f\}
. We define the mapping
\Phi^{(j;k)}_{3}
from
F^{j}_{k}
to
\mathcal{H}^{(j;k)}
as follows: given any
U^{j}_{k}\subset F^{j}_{k}
, for every
f\in set(U^{j}_{k})
, mapping
\Phi^{(j;k)}_{3}
returns any
card_{U^{j}_{k}}[f]
elements in
\mathcal{H}^{(j;k)}_{f}
. Let
\Phi^{(j;k)}_{3}
be the collection of all elements returned by
\Phi^{(j;k)}_{3}
given
U^{j}_{k}
.

For each
F^{j}_{k}
, we first divide it into two groups
F^{(j;1)}_{k}
and
F^{(j;2)}_{k}
, such that the values of elements in
F^{(j;i)}_{k}\ (i=1,2)
are different from each other. Then for each
F^{(j;i)}_{k}
, we divide it into
\log_{2}|\bar{Y}^{j}_{k}|
groups, denoted by
F^{(j;i;k)}_{1},F^{(j;i;k)}_{2},\cdots,F^{(j;i;k)}_{\log_{2}|\bar{Y}^{j}_{k}|}
, such that
x\in F^{(j;i;k)}_{p}
if and only if
x
has been factorized to the form
2^{p}y
where
y\in\bar{Y}^{j}_{k}
. It is easy to observe that
F^{(j;i;k)}_{p}\subset[\frac{(1-\epsilon)2^{p+j-1}}{\epsilon^{2}},\frac{(1+\epsilon)2^{p+j}}{\epsilon^{2}})
. Let
F:=\mathop{\dot{\cup}}\limits_{1\leq j\leq\eta;1\leq k\leq|\Delta_{j}|}F^{j}_{k}=\mathop{\dot{\cup}}\limits_{0\leq p\leq\log_{2}|\bar{Y}^{j}_{k}|\atop i=1,2;1\leq j\leq\eta;1\leq k\leq|\Delta_{j}|}F^{(j;i;k)}_{p}
. Notice that

|F|\leq|\dot{\cup}^{\eta}_{j=1}\bar{Y}_{j}|\leq|X|
. Moreover, note that
F
is an equivalent multiset of
\dot{\cup}^{\eta}_{j=1}\bar{Y}_{j}
, i.e.,
S(F)=S(\dot{\cup}^{\eta}_{j=1}\bar{Y}_{j})
. Thus
\Sigma(F)=\Sigma(\dot{\cup}^{\eta}_{j=1}\bar{Y}_{j})\leq\frac{4(1+\epsilon)\Sigma(X)}{\epsilon^{3}t}

and the optimal objective value of SUBSET SUM instance
(F,\hat{t})
is at least
\frac{4(1-\epsilon)}{\epsilon^{3}\cdot t}(OPT-\frac{\epsilon t}{2})
.

Observe that the total time to obtain
F^{(j;i;k)}_{p}
’s from
\bar{Y}^{j}_{k}
is
{\mathcal{O}}(|\bar{Y}^{j}_{k}|(\log|\bar{Y}^{j}_{k}|)^{2})
. Thus the total processing time of Step 4 is
{\mathcal{O}}(\mathop{\Sigma}\limits_{1\leq j\leq\eta;1\leq k\leq|\Delta_{j}|}(|\bar{Y}^{j}_{k}|(\log|\bar{Y}^{j}_{k}|)^{2}))={\mathcal{O}}(|X|(\log|X|)^{2})
.

Modified instance after preprocessing.

To summarize, we have reduced the instance
(X,t)
to a modified instance
(Y,\hat{t})
, where
Y
and
\hat{t}
satisfying the following conditions:

(I)

\Sigma(F)\leq\frac{4(1+\epsilon)\Sigma(X)}{\epsilon^{3}t}
and
\hat{t}=\frac{4(1+\epsilon)}{\epsilon^{3}}
.

(II)

The optimal objective value of
(F,\hat{t})
is at least
\frac{4(1-\epsilon)}{\epsilon^{3}\cdot t}\cdot(OPT-\frac{\epsilon t}{2})
.

(III)

|F|\leq|X|
and
F
has been divided into
{\mathcal{O}}((\log|X|)^{2}(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{{\mathcal{O}}(d)})
subgroups:
F^{(j;i;k)}_{p}
’s.

(IV)

Each subgroup
F^{(j;i;k)}_{p}
satisfies the followings:

(a)

Elements in
F^{(j;i;k)}_{p}
are different from each other;

(b)

F^{(j;i;k)}_{p}\subset[\frac{(1-\epsilon)2^{p+j-1}}{\epsilon^{2}},\frac{(1+\epsilon)2^{p+j}}{\epsilon^{2}})
;

(c)

F^{(j;i;k)}_{p}=2^{p}\rho^{j}_{k}\overline{F}^{(j;i;k)}_{p}
, where
\overline{F}^{(j;i;k)}_{p}\subset\mathbb{N}_{+}\cap[\frac{1}{4\epsilon},\frac{1}{\epsilon}]

and
\overline{F}^{(j;i;k)}_{p}
is a set of
(\epsilon,d,d-1)
-smooth numbers, that is, every element in
\overline{F}^{(j;i;k)}_{p}
has been factorized as
h_{1}h_{2}\cdots h_{d}
, where
h_{d}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\cap[1,2\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{d}}]
and if
d\geq 2
then
h_{i}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\cap[\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{d}},2{\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{d}}}]
for
i=1,2,\cdots,d-1
.

Till now, we have completed the preprocessing procedure for modifying SUBSET SUM instance
(X,t)
. The total processing time is
{\mathcal{O}}((|X|+\frac{1}{\epsilon})(\log|X|)^{2}(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{{\mathcal{O}}(d)}).
This accomplishes the first half (i.e., item (i)) of Lemma
10
. In the following, we will present the oracle for backtracking from
F
to
X
, which is the second half (item (ii)) of Lemma
10
.

Oracle for backtracking from
F
to
X
.

We now present the oracle
\textbf{Ora}^{X}_{F}
for backtracking from
F
to
X
. Given
U^{(j;i;k)}_{p}\subset F^{(j;i;k)}_{p}
for every
F^{(j;i;k)}_{p}
, oracle
\textbf{Ora}^{X}_{F}
works as follows:

•

Let
U^{j}_{k}:=\mathop{\dot{\cup}}\limits_{p}\left(\mathop{\dot{\cup}}\limits_{i}U^{(j;i;k)}_{p}\right)
for every
j
and
k
. Note that
U^{j}_{k}\subset F^{j}_{k}
and
\Phi^{(j;k)}_{3}
is a mapping from
F^{j}_{k}
to
\mathcal{H}^{(j;k)}
.
\textbf{Ora}^{X}_{F}
first uses
\Phi^{(j;k)}_{3}
to obtain
\Phi^{(j;k)}_{3}(U^{j}_{k})
. Let
V^{j}_{k}=\mathop{\dot{\cup}}\limits_{ele\in\Phi^{(j;k)}_{3}(U^{j}_{k})}ele
. Observe that
\Sigma(V^{j}_{k})=\Sigma(U^{j}_{k})=\sum_{p}\sum_{i}(\Sigma(U^{(j;i;k)}_{p}))
and
V^{j}_{k}\subset\bar{Y}^{j}_{k}
.

•

Let
V_{j}=\mathop{\dot{\cup}}\limits_{k}(V^{j}_{k})
for every
j
. Notice that
V_{j}\subset\bar{Y}_{j}
and
\Phi^{j}_{2}
is a mapping from
\bar{Y}_{j}
to
{Y}_{j}
. Then
\textbf{Ora}^{X}_{F}
uses
\Phi^{j}_{2}
to obtain
\Phi^{j}_{2}(V_{j})
. Let
W=\mathop{\dot{\cup}}\limits_{j}\Phi^{j}_{2}(V_{j})
. Observe that
|\Sigma(W)-\sum_{j}\Sigma(V_{j})|=|\sum_{j}\Sigma(\Phi^{j}_{2}(V_{j}))-\sum_{j}\Sigma(V_{j})|\leq\sum_{j}\epsilon\Sigma(\Phi^{j}_{2}(V_{j}))=\epsilon\Sigma(W)
and
W\subset\bar{Z}
.

•

Recall that
\Phi_{1}
is a mapping from
\bar{Z}
to
\mathcal{L}
. Finally,
\textbf{Ora}^{X}_{F}
uses
\Phi_{1}
to obtain
\Phi_{1}(W)
and returns
\mathop{\dot{\cup}}\limits_{ele\in\Phi_{1}(W)}ele
. Note that
\mathop{\dot{\cup}}\limits_{ele\in\Phi_{1}(W)}ele\subset X
and we have
\Sigma(\mathop{\dot{\cup}}\limits_{ele\in\Phi_{1}(W)}ele)=\frac{4}{\epsilon^{3}t}\Sigma(W)
.

The above backtracking procedure only takes linear time. Let
F^{\prime}
be the multiset-union of all
U^{(j;i;k)}_{p}
’s. To summarize, we have
|\frac{4}{\epsilon^{3}t}\Sigma(F^{\prime})-\Sigma(\mathop{\dot{\cup}}\limits_{ele\in\Phi_{1}(W)}ele)|\leq\epsilon\Sigma(\mathop{\dot{\cup}}\limits_{ele\in\Phi_{1}(W)}ele)
.

Till now, we complete the proof of Lemma
10
.

So far we have obtained all the prerequisites. In the subsequent 3 sections, we will present our main results in this paper.

6
An
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(n+\epsilon^{-\frac{5}{4}})
-time FPTAS for PARTITION.

The goal of this section is to prove the following theorem.

Theorem 1
.

There is an
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(n+\epsilon^{-\frac{5}{4}})
deterministic FPTAS for PARTITION.

Given a multiset
X=\{x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{n}\}\subset\mathbb{N}
, PARTITION is the same as SUBSET SUM on target
t=\frac{\Sigma(X)}{2}
. Let
OPT
be the optimal objective value of SUBSET SUM instance
(X,\frac{\Sigma(X)}{2})
. By Lemma
1
, we may assume
OPT\geq\frac{\Sigma(X)}{4}=\Theta(\Sigma(X))
. Notice that once we have found a subset
Y\subset X
satisfying

|\Sigma(Y)-OPT|\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)\Sigma(X)
, then one of
Y
and
X\backslash Y
is a
(1-\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon))
-approximation solution of
(X,\frac{\Sigma(X)}{2})
, thus the following lemma implies Theorem
1
directly.

Lemma 12
.

Given a multiset
X=\{x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{n}\}\subset\mathbb{N}
, in
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(n+\epsilon^{-\frac{5}{4}})
processing time, we can

(i).

Compute an
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)
-approximate set with cardinality of
{\mathcal{O}}({\epsilon}^{-1})
for
S(X)
;

(ii).

Meanwhile build an
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(n+\epsilon^{-\frac{5}{4}})
-time oracle for backtracking from this approximate set to
X
.

Note that an
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)
-approximate set for
S(X)
admits an additive error of
{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\Sigma(X))
, which solves PARTITION but not SUBSET SUM with target
t\ll\Sigma(X)
. Nevertheless, Lemma
12
also implies the following corollary.

Corollary 3
.

There is an
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(n+\epsilon^{-\frac{5}{4}})
deterministic weak
(1-\epsilon)
-approximation algorithm for SUBSET SUM if
t=\Theta(\Sigma(X))
, i.e.,
the target is some constant fraction of the total summation of elements.

Given a SUBSET SUM instance
(X,t)
where
t=\Theta(\Sigma(X))
and let
OPT
be the optimal objective value of
(X,t)
. Let
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
be a constant to be fixed later (in particular, we will choose
d=12
). Recall Lemma
10
, in
{\mathcal{O}}((|X|+\frac{1}{\epsilon})(\log|X|)^{2}(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{{\mathcal{O}}(d)})=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(|X|+\frac{1}{\epsilon})
time, we can reduce
(X,t)
to a SUBSET SUM instance
(F,\hat{t})
satisfying conditions
(I)(II)(II)(IV)
and meanwhile build an oracle for backtracking from
F
to
X
(see Lemma
10
in Section
5
). Condition
(II)
claims that the optimal objective value of
(F,\hat{t})
is at least
\frac{4(1-\epsilon)}{\epsilon^{3}\cdot t}\cdot(OPT-\frac{\epsilon t}{2})
, recall that
OPT\geq t/2
, then given any weak
(1-\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon))
-approximation of
(F,\hat{t})
, in linear time, the oracle will return a weak
(1-\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon))
-approximation of
(X,t)
. It thus suffices to consider
(F,\hat{t})
. That is, Lemma
12
, and hence Theorem
1
, follow from the following Lemma
13
.

Lemma 13
.

Given any SUBSET SUM instance
(X,t)
, where
t=\Theta(\Sigma(X))
and the optimal objective value of
(X,t)
is at least
{t}/{2}
. Let
d\geq 12
be an integer divisible by 4 and let
(F,\hat{t})
be a modified instance returned by Lemma
10
, where
F
satisfies the followings:

1.

\Sigma(F)=\Theta(\epsilon^{-3})
.

2.

|F|\leq|X|
and
F
has been divided into
{\mathcal{O}}((\log|X|)^{2}(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{{\mathcal{O}}(d)})
subgroups:
F^{(j;i;k)}_{p}
’s.

3.

Each
F^{(j;i;k)}_{p}
satisfies the followings:

•

Elements in
F^{(j;i;k)}_{p}
are distinct;

•

F^{(j;i;k)}_{p}\subset[\frac{(1-\epsilon)2^{p+j-1}}{\epsilon^{2}},\frac{(1+\epsilon)2^{p+j}}{\epsilon^{2}})
;

•

F^{(j;i;k)}_{p}=2^{p}\rho^{j}_{k}\overline{F}^{(j;i;k)}_{p}
, where
\overline{F}^{(j;i;k)}_{p}\subset\mathbb{N}_{+}\cap[\frac{1}{4\epsilon},\frac{1}{\epsilon}]

and
\overline{F}^{(j;i;k)}_{p}
is a set of
(\epsilon,d,d-1)
-smooth numbers, that is, every element in
\overline{F}^{(j;i;k)}_{p}
has been factorized as
h_{1}h_{2}\cdots h_{d}
, where
h_{d}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\cap[1,2\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{d}}]
and
h_{i}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\cap[\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{d}},2{\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{d}}}]
for
i=1,2,\cdots,d-1
.

Then in
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{5}{4}})
processing time, we can

(i).

Compute an
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)
-approximate set
C
with cardinality of
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
for
S(F)
.

(ii).

Meanwhile build an
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{5}{4}})
-time oracle for backtracking from
C
to
F
. Here
F
is the union of
F^{(j;i;k)}_{p}
’s and the oracle actually works as follows: given any
c\in C
, in
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{5}{4}})
processing time, the oracle will return
U^{(j;i;k)}_{p}\subset F^{(j;i;k)}_{p}
for every
F^{(j;i;k)}_{p}
. Let
F^{\prime}
be the multiset-union of all
U^{(j;i;k)}_{p}
’s, we have
F^{\prime}\subset F
and
|\Sigma(F^{\prime})-c|\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)\Sigma(F)
.

The rest of this section is dedicated to proving Lemma
13
. Recall Corollary
1
, which allows us to build the approximation set of the union of
F^{(j;i;k)}_{p}
’s from the approximate set of each
F^{(j;i;k)}_{p}
. Towards proving Lemma
13
, we only need to derive an algorithm that can solve the following
problem
-
\mathscr{P}
in
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{5}{4}})
time:

problem
-
\mathscr{P}
: for every
F_{p}^{(j;i;k)}
, within an additive error of
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\hat{t})
,
compute an
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)
-approximate set with cardinality of
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{5}{4}})
for
S(F_{p}^{(j;i;k)})
and build an
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{5}{4}})
-time oracle for backtracking from this approximate set to
F_{p}^{(j;i;k)}
.

To solve
problem
-
\mathscr{P}
, we only need to prove the following lemma
14
, where
M
represents an arbitrary
F_{p}^{(j;i;k)}
.

Lemma 14
.

Given
M\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
satisfying the following conditions:

1.

\Sigma(M)={\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-3})
;

2.

Each element in
M
is distinct;

3.

M=\beta\tilde{M}
, where
\beta
and
\tilde{M}
satisfy the followings:

•

\beta\in\Theta(\frac{1}{\epsilon^{1+\lambda}})
and
\beta\tilde{M}\subset[\frac{1-\epsilon}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda}},\frac{2(1+\epsilon)}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda}}]
, where
\lambda\geq 0
;

•

\tilde{M}\subset\mathbb{N}_{+}\cap[\frac{1}{4\epsilon},\frac{1}{\epsilon}]
and
\tilde{M}
is a set of
(\epsilon,d,d-1)
-smooth numbers, that is, every element in
\tilde{M}
has been factorized as
h_{1}h_{2}\cdots h_{d}
, where
h_{d}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\cap[1,2\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{d}}]
and
h_{i}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\cap[\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{d}},2{\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{d}}}]
for
i=1,2,\cdots,d-1
.

Here
d\geq 12
is an integer divisible by 4 (e.g.,
d=12
). Then in
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{5}{4}})
processing time, within an additive error of
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-2})
, we can compute an
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)
-approximate set with cardinality of
{\mathcal{O}}({\epsilon}^{-1})
for
S(M)
and build an
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{5}{4}})
-time oracle for backtracking from this approximate set to
M
.

Remark.
Here an additive error of
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-2})
is acceptable since
\hat{t}=\Theta(\epsilon^{-3})
and
F
is only divided into polylogarithmic groups, whereas an additive
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-2})
error per group gives overall
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon\hat{t})
error.

We give a very high-level description of the proof. To obtain an efficient algorithm, our main tool is Lemma
8
, whose running time depends on the summation of the input numbers, and thus it is important to reduce this sum. Consider
M
, since all of its elements share
\beta
as the common divisor, we can restrict our attention to
\tilde{M}
. Note that
\beta=\Theta(\frac{1}{\epsilon^{1+\lambda}})
. If
\lambda\geq 1/2
, and hence
\beta
is large, then
\Sigma(\tilde{M})
is small, and we can apply Lemma
8
directly. Otherwise,
\beta
is small, then
\Sigma(\tilde{M})={\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2-\lambda}})
is large, implying that
\tilde{M}
consists of many smooth numbers. In this case, we can exploit the additive combinatoric result from
[
9
]
, which roughly says that if there are sufficiently many distinct integers, then SUBSET SUM can be solved efficiently if the target
t
is in the “medium” range, that is,
t
is close to half of the total sum of integers (see Theorem
2
for a formal description of the additive combinatoric result. This result has also been leveraged before by Mucha et al.
[
32
]
). Then what if
t
is out of the medium range? In this case,
t
is either very small or very large, and by symmetry it suffices to consider the case when
t
is small. The crucial observation is that a small target value can only be the sum of a few numbers in
\tilde{M}
. Hence, we may adopt a “coarse” rounding to re-round the numbers, that is, instead of only introducing
{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)
-multiplicative error to each input number, we may introduce a larger error. Although per number the multiplicative error is
\Omega(\epsilon)
, but since only
o(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
numbers will be selected, we can guarantee that the summation of selected numbers gives additive
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-2})
error in total by a careful parameterized analysis.

In the following, we will give detailed proof of Lemma
14
. Note that

M=\beta\tilde{M}\subset[\frac{1-\epsilon}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda}},\frac{2(1+\epsilon)}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda}}]
and
\Sigma(M)={\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-3})
, we have
|M|=|\tilde{M}|={\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-1})
. We call
{M}
a large-value group if
\lambda\geq 1/2

, otherwise we call
{M}
a small-value group. Large-value groups and small-value groups will be handled separately.

6.1
Handling Large-Value Group.

We first consider the case that
M\subset[\frac{(1-\epsilon)}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda}},\frac{2(1+\epsilon)}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda}}]
where
\lambda\geq 1/2
.

Notice that elements in
\tilde{M}
are
(\epsilon,d,d-1)
-smooth numbers. According to Lemma
8
, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}\cap[0,d-1]
, in
T={\mathcal{O}}(d\cdot|\tilde{M}|+\Sigma(\tilde{M}){\epsilon^{\frac{k}{d}}}\cdot\log(|\tilde{M}|)\cdot\log(\Sigma(\tilde{M}){\epsilon^{\frac{k}{d}}})+{\epsilon^{-(1+\frac{k}{d})}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
processing time, we can compute an
{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{k})
-approximate set with cardinality of
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
for
S(\tilde{M})
. Denote by
C_{\tilde{M}}
this approximate set, in the meantime, we have built a
T^{(1)}
-time oracle for backtracking from
C_{\tilde{M}}
to
\tilde{M}
, where
T^{(1)}={\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma(\tilde{M}){\epsilon^{\frac{k}{d}}}\cdot\log(|\tilde{M}|)\cdot\log(\Sigma(\tilde{M}){\epsilon^{\frac{k}{d}}})+{\epsilon^{-(1+\frac{k}{d})}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
. It is easy to observe that
\beta C_{\tilde{M}}
is an
{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{k})
-approximate set with cardinality of
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
for
S({M})
and the oracle for backtracking from from
C_{\tilde{M}}
to
\tilde{M}
directly yields an
{\mathcal{O}}(T^{(1)})
-time oracle for backtracking from
\beta C_{\tilde{M}}
to
M
. Note that
|M|={\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
and
\Sigma(\tilde{M})={\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{3}{2}})
if
\lambda\geq\frac{1}{2}
. Recall that
d\geq 12
is an integer divisible by 4, by fixing
k
to
\frac{d}{4}
, we have
{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{k})=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
,
T={\mathcal{O}}(\frac{d}{\epsilon}+\epsilon^{-\frac{5}{4}}(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{2})=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{5}{4}})
and
T^{(1)}={\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{5}{4}}(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{2})=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{5}{4}})
. Thus Lemma
14
has been proved for case that
M
is a large-value group.

It remains to prove Lemma
14
for the case that
M
is a small-value group, which is the task of the following Subsection
6.2
.

6.2
Handling Small-Value Group

The goal of this section is to prove Lemma
14
for the case that
M\subset[\frac{1-\epsilon}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda}},\frac{2(1+\epsilon)}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda}}]
where
\lambda<1/2
.

Note that
M
contains at most
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
elements and these elements are different from each other, we call
M
a
dense
group if
|M|=\Omega(\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{2}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
, otherwise, we call
M
a
sparse
group. When
M
is sparse, i.e.,
|M|={\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{2}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
, note that
\tilde{M}\subset[\frac{1}{4\epsilon},\frac{1}{\epsilon}]
, we have
\Sigma(\tilde{M})\leq|M|\cdot\frac{1}{\epsilon}=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{3}{2}})
, same as the discussion for large-value group, Lemma
8
guarantees the correctness of Lemma
14
. Thus we only need to consider the dense group.

In the following, we assume
M\subset[\frac{1-\epsilon}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda}},\frac{2(1+\epsilon)}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda}}]
with
\lambda<\frac{1}{2}
and
|M|=\Omega(\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{2}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
. Define
L:=\frac{100\cdot\Sigma(M)\cdot\sqrt{\frac{1}{\epsilon}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}}{|M|}
. We approximate
S(M)
and build an oracle for backtracking in the following three steps: (1). handle
S(M)\cap(L,\Sigma(M)-L)
; (2). handle
S(M)\cap[0,L]
and
S(M)\cap[\Sigma(M)-L,\Sigma(M)]
; (3). handle
S(M).

Step 1: Handling
S(M)\cap(L,\Sigma(M)-L)
.

In this step, we aim to prove the following claim.

Claim 2
.

In
T_{mid}={\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
processing time, we can

(i).

Compute a subset
\Sigma_{mid}\subset S(M)\cap(L,\Sigma(M)-L)
satisfying the following conditions: (i).
|\Sigma_{mid}|={\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
; (ii). given any
s\in S(M)\cap(L,\Sigma(M)-L)
, there exists
s^{\prime}\in\Sigma_{mid}
such that
|s^{\prime}-s|\leq\epsilon\Sigma(M)
.

(ii).

Meanwhile build an
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
-time oracle for backtracking from
\Sigma_{mid}
to
M
. That is, given any
c\in\Sigma_{mid}
, in
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
time, the oracle will return
M_{c}\subset M
such that
\Sigma(M_{c})=c
.

Before proving Claim
2
, we first import the following theorem, which is derived by Galil and Margalit
[
9
]
.

Theorem 2

(CF. Theorem 6.1 from
[
9
]
)
.

Let
Z

be a set of
m
distinct numbers in the interval
(0,l]
such
that

m>1000\cdot\sqrt{l}\log l.

and let
L:=\frac{100\cdot\sum(Z)\sqrt{l}\log l}{m}
.
Then in
\mathcal{O}\left(m+(\frac{l}{m}\log l)^{2}+\frac{\sum(Z)\sqrt{l}(\log l)^{2}}{m^{2}}\right)
preprocessing time we can build a structure which allows us to solve the subset-sum problem for any given integer
t
in the interval
\left(L,\sum(Z)-L\right)
. Solving means finding a subset
B\subset Z
such that
\sum(B)\leq t
and there is no subset
C\subset Z
such that
\sum{(B)}<\sum{(C)}\leq t
. An optimal subset
B
is built in
O(\log l)
time per target number.

Now we are ready to prove Claim
2
.

Proof of Claim
2
.

Consider
\tilde{M}=(M)/\beta
. Recall that elements in
\tilde{M}
are different from each other and
|\tilde{M}|=|M|=\Omega(\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{2}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
, moreover,
\tilde{M}\subset\mathbb{N}\cap[\frac{1}{4\epsilon},\frac{1}{\epsilon}]
. Let
\tilde{L}=\frac{L}{\beta}=\frac{100\cdot\Sigma(\tilde{M})\sqrt{\frac{1}{\epsilon}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}}{|\tilde{M}|}
. According to Theorem
2
, in
\mathcal{O}(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
time, we can construct a data structure which allows us to solve the SUBSET SUM problem
(\tilde{M},c)
in
{\mathcal{O}}(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
time, where
c
is any integer in
(\tilde{L},\Sigma(\tilde{M})-\tilde{L})
. Let
\mathcal{C}=\{c_{1},c_{2},\cdots,c_{\iota}\}
denote the set of all different integers in
\left\{\lfloor\epsilon\Sigma(\tilde{M})\rfloor,\lfloor 2\epsilon\Sigma(\tilde{M})\rfloor,\cdots,\lfloor\Sigma(\tilde{M})\rfloor,\lfloor\Sigma(\tilde{M})-\tilde{L}\rfloor\right\}\cap(\tilde{L},\Sigma(\tilde{M})-\tilde{L})
. We solve the SUBSET SUM problem
(\tilde{M},c)
for every integer
c\in\mathcal{C}
. Denote by
E_{i}
a solution of the SUBSET SUM problem
(\tilde{M},c_{i})
, let
\mathcal{E}:=\{E_{1},E_{2},\cdots,E_{\iota}\}
and let
\Sigma_{mid}:=\{\beta\Sigma(E_{1}),\beta\Sigma(E_{2}),\cdots,\beta\Sigma(E_{\iota})\}
. We build a dictionary
\mathscr{D}
as follows: each element in
\mathscr{D}
is a key-value pair
(\beta\Sigma(E_{i}),\mathscr{D}[\beta\Sigma(E_{i})])
, where
\mathscr{D}[\beta\Sigma(E_{i})]=\beta E_{i}
and
i=1,2,\cdots,\iota
. The collection of keys of
\mathscr{D}
is
\Sigma_{mid}
.

In the next, we show that
\Sigma_{mid}
satisfies the conditions required in Claim
2
. Apparently,
|\Sigma_{mid}|={\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
.
Consider any
s\in S(M)\cap(L,\Sigma(M)-L)
, it follows that
\tilde{L}<\frac{s}{\beta}\leq\lfloor\Sigma(\tilde{M})-\tilde{L}\rfloor
and there exists some integer
k\in[0,\frac{1}{\epsilon}]
such that
k\epsilon\Sigma(\tilde{M})<\frac{s}{\beta}\leq(1+k)\epsilon\Sigma(\tilde{M})
, thus we have

\lfloor k\epsilon\Sigma(\tilde{M})\rfloor<\frac{s}{\beta}\leq\min\left\{\lfloor(1+k)\epsilon\Sigma(\tilde{M})\rfloor,\lfloor\Sigma(\tilde{M})-\tilde{L}\rfloor\right\}.

Observe that
\min\left\{\lfloor(1+k)\epsilon\Sigma(\tilde{M})\rfloor,\lfloor\Sigma(\tilde{M})-\tilde{L}\rfloor\right\}\in\mathcal{C}
, which guarantees that there exists some
E_{k}\in\mathcal{E}
satisfying
\Sigma(E_{k})\in[k\epsilon\Sigma(\tilde{M}),(1+k)\epsilon\Sigma(\tilde{M})]
, then
\beta\Sigma(E_{k})\in\Sigma_{mid}
and
|s-\beta\Sigma(E_{k})|\leq\beta\cdot\epsilon\Sigma(\tilde{M})=\epsilon\Sigma({M})
.

An
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
-time oracle for backtracking from
\Sigma_{mid}
to
M
works as follows: given any
c\in\Sigma_{mid}
, the oracle uses dictionary
\mathscr{D}
to return
\mathscr{D}[c]
. It is easy to see that
\mathscr{D}[c]\subset M
and
\Sigma(\mathscr{D}[c])=c
.∎∎

Step 2: Handling
S(M)\cap[0,L]
and
S(M)\cap[\Sigma(M)-L,\Sigma(M)]
.

In this step, we aim to prove the following claim.

Claim 3
.

In
T_{LR}=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{5}{4}})
processing time, we can

(i).

Compute a set
\Sigma_{LR}\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
satisfying the following conditions: (i).
|\Sigma_{LR}|={\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
; (ii). given any
s\in\left(S(M)\cap[0,L]\right)\cup\left(S(M)\cap[\Sigma(M)-L,\Sigma(M)]\right)
, there exists
s^{\prime}\in\Sigma_{LR}
such that
|s-s^{\prime}|\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)\Sigma(M)+\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-2})
.

(ii).

Meanwhile build an
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{5}{4}})
-time oracle for backtracking from
\Sigma_{LR}
to
M
. That is, given any
c\in\Sigma_{LR}
, in
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{5}{4}})
time, the oracle will return
M_{c}\subset M
such that
|c-\Sigma(M^{\prime})|\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)\Sigma(M)+\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-2})
.

Proof of Claim
3
.

Divide
M
into the following three disjoint groups:
M_{1}=M\cap[\frac{1-\epsilon}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda}},\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda}})
,
M_{2}=M\cap[\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda}},\frac{2}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda}})
and
M_{3}=M\cap[\frac{2}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda}},\frac{2(1+\epsilon)}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda}}]
. Since
\epsilon>0
is sufficiently small, we coulld assume that
\epsilon\leq\frac{1}{2}
, then
2M_{1},\frac{1}{2}M_{3}\subset[\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda}},\frac{2}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda}})
. Let
V_{1}
,
V_{2}
and
V_{3}
denote
2M_{1}
,
M_{2}
and
\frac{1}{2}M_{3}
, respectively.

Recall that
|M|={\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
, then
|V_{i}|={\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
for
i=1,2,3
. Given
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and let
\bar{d}<d
be the integer such that
\lambda+\frac{1}{2}\in(\frac{\bar{d}}{d},\frac{\bar{d}+1}{d}]
. Recall Lemma
6
, for each
V_{i}
, in
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon}(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{{\mathcal{O}}(\bar{d}+1)})
processing time, we can obtain a set
\Delta^{\prime}_{i}\subset\Theta(\epsilon^{-\frac{3}{2}})
with
|\Delta^{\prime}_{i}|={\mathcal{O}}((\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{{\mathcal{O}}(\bar{d}+1)})
and round every
y\in V_{i}
to the form
\rho^{\prime}h^{\prime}_{1}h^{\prime}_{2}\cdots h^{\prime}_{\bar{d}+1}
, where
\rho^{\prime}\in\Delta^{\prime}_{i}
and
h^{\prime}_{j}
’s satisfy the followings: (i)
h^{\prime}_{1}h^{\prime}_{2}\cdots h^{\prime}_{\bar{d}+1}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\cap[\frac{1}{4\epsilon^{\lambda+\frac{1}{2}}},\frac{1}{\epsilon^{\lambda+\frac{1}{2}}}]
; (ii)
h^{\prime}_{\bar{d}+1}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\cap[\frac{1}{2\epsilon^{\lambda+\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\bar{d}}{d}}},\frac{2}{\epsilon^{\lambda+\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\bar{d}}{d}}}]
and if
\bar{d}>0
then
h^{\prime}_{j}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\cap[\frac{1}{2\epsilon^{\frac{1}{d}}},\frac{2}{\epsilon^{\frac{1}{d}}}]
for every
1\leq i\leq\bar{d}
; (iii)
|y-\rho h^{\prime}_{1}h^{\prime}_{2}\cdots h^{\prime}_{\bar{d}+1}|\leq\epsilon^{\lambda+\frac{1}{2}}y
. Denote by
\overline{V}_{i}
the set of all such rounded elements obtained from
V_{i}
.

Let
\overline{V}=(\frac{1}{2}\overline{V}_{1})\dot{\cup}\overline{V}_{2}\dot{\cup}(2\overline{V}_{2})
. We have the following observation.

Observation 7
.

In
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{5}{4}})
time, we can compute an
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)
-approximate set with cardinality of
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
for
S(\overline{V})
and meanwhile build an
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{5}{4}})
-time oracle for backtracking from this approximate set to
\overline{V}
.

Proof.

Given any multiset
X
and any
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, note that if
C
is an
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)
-approximate set of

S(X)
, then
\gamma C
is an
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)
-approximate set of

S(\gamma X)
, moreover, a
T
-time oracle for backtracking from
C
to
X
directly yields an
{\mathcal{O}}(T)
-time oracle for backtracking from
\gamma C
to
\gamma X
.

Recall Corollary
1
, towards Observation
7
, it is sufficient to prove that for each
\overline{V}_{i}
, in
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{5}{4}})
processing time, one can compute an
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)
-approximate set with cardinality of
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
for
S(\overline{V}_{i})
and build an
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{5}{4}})
-time oracle for backtracking from this approximate set to
\overline{V}_{i}
. Consider each
\overline{V}_{i}
. Let
\Delta^{\prime}_{i}:=\{\rho^{\prime}_{1},\rho^{\prime}_{2},\cdots,\rho^{\prime}_{|\Delta^{\prime}_{i}|}\}
, we can divide
\overline{V}_{i}
into
|\Delta^{\prime}_{i}|={\mathcal{O}}((\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{{\mathcal{O}}(\bar{d}+1)})
groups
\overline{V}_{i}^{1},\overline{V}_{i}^{2},\cdots,\overline{V}_{i}^{|\Delta^{\prime}|}
such that
y\in\overline{V}_{i}^{k}
if and only if
y
is of the form
\rho^{\prime}_{k}h^{\prime}_{1}h^{\prime}_{2}\cdots h^{\prime}_{\bar{d}+1}
. Again by Corollary
1
, it is sufficent to prove that for each
\overline{V}^{k}_{i}
, in
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{5}{4}})
processing time, one can compute an
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)
-approximate set with cardinality of
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
for
S(\overline{V}^{k}_{i})
and build an
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{5}{4}})
-time oracle for backtracking from this approximate set to
\overline{V}^{k}_{i}
.

Note that elements in
\frac{\overline{V}_{i}^{k}}{\rho^{\prime}_{k}}
are
(\epsilon,d,\bar{d})
-smooth numbers. Recall Lemma
8
, for any
\tau\in\mathbb{N}\cap[0,\bar{d}]
, in
T^{k}_{i}={\mathcal{O}}\left(d\cdot\left|\frac{\overline{V}_{i}^{k}}{\rho^{\prime}_{k}}\right|+\Sigma(\frac{\overline{V}_{i}^{k}}{\rho^{\prime}_{k}}){\epsilon^{\frac{\tau}{d}}}\cdot\log(\left|\frac{\overline{V}_{i}^{k}}{\rho^{\prime}_{k}}\right|)\cdot\log(\Sigma(\frac{\overline{V}_{i}^{k}}{\rho^{\prime}_{k}}){\epsilon^{\frac{\tau}{d}}})+{\epsilon^{-(1+\frac{\tau}{d})}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right)
processing time, we can compute an
{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{k})
-approximate set with cardinality of
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
for
S(\frac{\overline{V}_{i}^{k}}{\rho^{\prime}_{k}})
. Denote by
C_{{\overline{V}_{i}^{k}}}
this approximate set, in the meantime, we have built a
\tilde{T}^{k}_{i}
-time oracle for backtracking from
C_{{\overline{V}_{i}^{k}}}
to
\frac{\overline{V}_{i}^{k}}{\rho^{\prime}_{k}}
, where
\tilde{T}^{k}_{i}={\mathcal{O}}\left(\Sigma(\frac{\overline{V}_{i}^{k}}{\rho^{\prime}_{k}}){\epsilon^{\frac{\tau}{d}}}\cdot\log(\left|\frac{\overline{V}_{i}^{k}}{\rho^{\prime}_{k}}\right|)\cdot\log(\Sigma(\frac{\overline{V}_{i}^{k}}{\rho^{\prime}_{k}}){\epsilon^{\frac{\tau}{d}}})+{\epsilon^{-(1+\frac{\tau}{d})}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right)
. It is easy to observe that
{\rho^{\prime}_{k}}C_{{\overline{V}_{i}^{k}}}
is an
{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{k})
-approximate set of
S(\overline{V}_{i}^{k})
and the oracle for backtracking from
C_{{\overline{V}_{i}^{k}}}
to
\frac{\overline{V}_{i}^{k}}{\rho^{\prime}_{k}}
directly yields an
{\mathcal{O}}(\tilde{T}^{k}_{i})
-time oracle for backtracking from
{\rho^{\prime}_{k}}C_{{\overline{V}_{i}^{k}}}
to
{\overline{V}_{i}^{k}}
.
It remains to determine
k
such that
T^{k}_{i}
and
\tilde{T}^{k}_{i}
attain their minimum values. Recall that
|M|={\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
, then
\left|\frac{\overline{V}_{i}^{k}}{\rho^{\prime}_{k}}\right|\leq|M|={\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
. Notice that
\Sigma(\overline{V}^{k}_{i})\leq(1+{\epsilon^{\lambda+\frac{1}{2}}})\Sigma(M)={\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-3})
and
\rho^{\prime}_{k}\in\Theta(\epsilon^{-\frac{3}{2}})
, then
\Sigma(\frac{\overline{V}_{i}^{k}}{\rho^{\prime}_{k}})={\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{3}{2}})
. Recall that
\lambda\geq 0
,
\frac{\bar{d}+1}{d}\geq\lambda+\frac{1}{2}
and
d\geq 12
is an integer divisible by 4, we have
\frac{d}{4}\leq\bar{d}
. By setting
\tau=\frac{d}{4}
, we have
T^{k}_{i}={\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{5}{4}}(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{2})
and
\tilde{T}^{k}_{i}={\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{5}{4}}(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{2})
. ∎∎

According to Observation
7
, in
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{5}{4}})
processing time, we can compute an
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)
-approximate set with cardinality of
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
for
S(\overline{V})
. Denote by
C_{\overline{V}}
this approximate set. In the meantime, we have built an
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{5}{4}})
-time oracle for backtracking from
C_{\overline{V}}
to
\overline{V}
. Denote by
\textbf{Ora}_{\overline{V}}
this oracle.

Define
\Sigma_{L}:=C_{\overline{V}}\cap[0,(1+\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{\lambda+\frac{1}{2}}))L+\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)\Sigma(\overline{V})]
and
\Sigma_{R}:=\{\Sigma(M)-s\ |\ s\in\Sigma_{L}\}
. Let
\Sigma_{LR}=\Sigma_{L}\cup\Sigma_{R}
. To summarize, by setting
d=12
, in total
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{5}{4}})
processing time, we can obtain
\Sigma_{LR}
with
|\Sigma_{LR}|={\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
. Moreover, we have the following observation.

Observation 8
.

Given any
s\in\left(S(M)\cap[0,L]\right)\cup\left(S(M)\cap[\Sigma(M)-L,\Sigma(M)]\right)
, there exists
s^{\prime}\in\Sigma_{LR}
such that
|s-s^{\prime}|\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)\Sigma(M)+\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-2})
.

Proof.

Note that
\overline{V}
is obtained by rounding elements in
M
. For any
\overline{V}^{\prime}\subset\overline{V}
, let
{M}^{\prime}\subset M
be the original subset corresponding to
\overline{V}^{\prime}
, it holds that
|\Sigma(\overline{V}^{\prime})-\Sigma({M}^{\prime})|\leq\epsilon^{\lambda+\frac{1}{2}}\Sigma({M}^{\prime})
, then
|\Sigma(\overline{V}^{\prime})-\Sigma({M}^{\prime})|\leq{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{\lambda+\frac{1}{2}})\Sigma(\overline{V}^{\prime})
.

We first show that given any
\bar{s}\in\Sigma_{L}
and any
\overline{V}^{\prime}\subset\overline{V}
satisfying
|\Sigma(\overline{V}^{\prime})-\bar{s}|\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)\Sigma(\overline{V})
, let
{M}^{\prime}\subset M
be original subset corresponding to
\overline{V}^{\prime}
, it always holds that
|\bar{s}-\Sigma({M}^{\prime})|\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)\Sigma(M)+\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-2})
. It suffices to observe the following:

\displaystyle|\bar{s}-\Sigma({M}^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\leq|\bar{s}-\Sigma(\overline{V}^{\prime})|+|\Sigma(\overline{V}^{\prime})-\Sigma({M}^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)\Sigma(\overline{V})+{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{\lambda+1/2})\Sigma(\overline{V}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)\Sigma(\overline{V})+{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{\lambda+1/2})(\bar{s}+\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)\Sigma(\overline{V}))

\displaystyle\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)\Sigma(\overline{V})+{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{\lambda+1/2})\left((1+\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{\lambda+1/2}))L+\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)\Sigma(\overline{V})\right)

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)\Sigma(\overline{V})+{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{\lambda+1/2})L\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)\Sigma(M)+{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{\lambda+1/2})L=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)\Sigma(M)+\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-2}).

Where the last inequality holds by the facts that
L=\frac{100\cdot\Sigma(M)\cdot\sqrt{\frac{1}{\epsilon}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}}{|M|}=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon^{5/2+\lambda}})
.

Now, we are ready to prove Observation
8
. Given any
s\in\left(S(M)\cap[0,L]\right)\cup\left(S(M)\cap[\Sigma(M)-L,\Sigma(M)]\right)
. If
s\in S(M)\cap[0,L]
, then there exists
s^{\prime}\in S(\overline{V})
such that
|s-s^{\prime}|\leq\epsilon^{\lambda+1/2}s\leq\epsilon^{\lambda+1/2}L.
For
s^{\prime}\in S(\overline{V})
, recall that
C_{\overline{V}}
is an
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)
-approximate set of
S(\overline{V})
, thus there exists
s^{\prime\prime}\in C_{\overline{V}}
such that
|s^{\prime\prime}-s^{\prime}|\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)\Sigma(\overline{V})
. To summarize, we have

|s^{\prime\prime}-s|\leq{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{\lambda+1/2})L+\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)\Sigma(\overline{V})\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-2})+\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)\Sigma(M).

It follows that
s^{\prime\prime}\leq s+{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{\lambda+1/2})L+\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)\Sigma(\overline{V})\leq(1+\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{\lambda+1/2}))L+\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)\Sigma(\overline{V})
, thus
s^{\prime\prime}\in\Sigma_{L}\in\Sigma_{LR}
.

Else if
s\in S(M)\cap[\Sigma(M)-L,\Sigma(M)]
, we have
\Sigma(M)-s\in S(M)\cap[0,L]
, then according to the above discussion, there exists
s^{\prime\prime}\in\Sigma_{L}
such that

|(\Sigma(M)-s^{\prime\prime})-s|=|(\Sigma(M)-s)-s^{\prime\prime}|\leq{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{\lambda+1/2})L+\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)\Sigma(V)\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-2})+\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)\Sigma(M).

It is easy to see that
\Sigma(M)-s^{\prime\prime}\in\Sigma_{R}\in\Sigma_{LR}
. ∎∎

It remains to give the oracle for backtracking from
\Sigma_{LR}
to
M
. Recall that
\Sigma_{L}\subset C_{\overline{V}}
and we have built an
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{5}{4}})
-time oracle
\textbf{Ora}_{\overline{V}}
for backtracking from
C_{\overline{V}}
to
\overline{V}
. An
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{5}{4}})
-time oracle
\textbf{Ora}_{LR}
for backtracking from
\Sigma_{LR}
to
M
works as follows. Given any
c\in\Sigma_{LR}
. If
c\in\Sigma_{L}
, oracle
\textbf{Ora}_{LR}
first uses
\textbf{Ora}_{\overline{V}}
to return a subset
V_{c}\subset\overline{V}
such that
|\Sigma(V_{c})-c|\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)\Sigma(\overline{V})
. Then
\textbf{Ora}_{LR}
returns
M_{c}
, which is the original subset in
M
that is corresponding to
V_{c}
. According to the above discussion, we have

|c-\Sigma(M_{c})|\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)\Sigma(M)+\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-2}).

Else if
c\in\Sigma_{R}
, we have
\Sigma(M)-c\in\Sigma_{L}
. Oracle
\textbf{Ora}_{LR}
first uses
\textbf{Ora}_{\overline{V}}
to return a subset
V^{\prime}\subset\overline{V}
such that
|\Sigma(V^{\prime})-(\Sigma(M)-c)|\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)\Sigma(\overline{V})
. Let
M^{\prime}
be the original subset in
M
that is corresponding to
V^{\prime}
. Then
\textbf{Ora}_{LR}
returns
M\backslash M^{\prime}
. According to the above discussion, we have

|\Sigma(M\backslash M^{\prime})-c|=|(\Sigma(M)-c)-\Sigma(M^{\prime})|\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)\Sigma(M)+\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-2}).

Till now, we complete the proof of Claim
7
.∎∎

Step 3: Handling
S(M)
.

Given
\Sigma_{mid}
and
\Sigma_{LR}
obtained in Step 1 and Step 2, respectively. Let
\Sigma_{union}=\Sigma_{mid}\cup\Sigma_{LR}
. Then we have the following Claim
4
, which follows directly from Claim
2
and Claim
3
.

Claim 4
.

With an additive error of
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}({\epsilon^{-2}})
,
\Sigma_{union}
is an
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)
-approximate set of
S({M})
. Moreover, we have built an
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{5}{4}})
-time oracle for backtracking from
\Sigma_{union}
to
M
.

Recall that the time to obtain
\Sigma_{mid}
and
\Sigma_{LR}
is
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{5}{4}})
. Moreover, we have
|\Sigma_{mid}|+|\Sigma_{LR}|={\mathcal{O}}(1/\epsilon)
. Thus the overall time to compute
\Sigma_{union}
is
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{5}{4}})
, and we have
|\Sigma_{union}|\leq|\Sigma_{mid}|+|\Sigma_{LR}|={\mathcal{O}}(1/\epsilon)
. Then combine with Claim
4
, we have proved Lemma
14
for the case that
M
is a dense small-value group. This completes the proof of Lemma
14
for the case
M
is a small-value group, furthermore, completes the proof of Lemma
14
. Till now, we have completed the proof of Theorem
1
.

7
An
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(n+\epsilon^{-\frac{3}{2}})
-time weak
(1-\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon))
-approximation algorithm for SUBSET SUM.

The goal of this section is to prove the following theorem.

Theorem 3
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
be an arbitrary even integer. There is a deterministic weak
(1-\epsilon)
-approximation algorithm for SUBSET SUM running in
{\mathcal{O}}((n+\epsilon^{-\frac{3}{2}-\frac{1}{d}})(\log n)^{2}(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{{\mathcal{O}}(d)})
time.

Remark.
Taking
d={\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{\frac{\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}}{\log\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}}})
, the overall running time is
\tilde{{\mathcal{O}}}(n+\epsilon^{-\frac{3}{2}})
where
\tilde{{\mathcal{O}}}
hides a factor of
2^{{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}})}=(\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{o(1)}
.

Given a multiset
X=\{x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{n}\}\subset\mathbb{N}
and a target
t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, we let
X^{*}
and
OPT
be an optimal solution and the optimal objective value of SUBSET SUM instance
(X,t)
, respectively. By Lemma
1
, we may assume that
OPT\geq{t}/{2}
. Then a subset
Y\subset X
satisfying
|\Sigma(Y)-OPT|\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)t
is a weak
(1-\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon))
-approximation solution of
(X,t)
. Thus the following lemma implies Theorem
3
directly.

Lemma 15
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
be an arbitrary even number. Given a multiset
X=\{x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{n}\}\subset\mathbb{N}
, in
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(n+\epsilon^{-\frac{3}{2}-\frac{1}{d}})
processing time, we can

(i).

Compute an
(\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon),t)
-approximate set with cardinality of
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{3}{2}})

for
S(X)
;

(ii).

Meanwhile build an
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(n+\epsilon^{-\frac{3}{2}-\frac{1}{d}})
-time oracle for backtracking from this approximate set to
X
.

Recall Lemma
10
, in
{\mathcal{O}}((|X+\frac{1}{\epsilon})(\log|X)^{2}(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{{\mathcal{O}}(d)})
time, we can reduce
(X,t)
to a SUBSET SUM instance
(F,\hat{t})
satisfying conditions
(I)(II)(II)(IV)

and meanwhile build an oracle for backtracking from
F
to
X
(see Lemma
10
in Section
5
). Condition
(II)
claims that the optimal objective value of
(F,\hat{t})
is at least
\frac{4(1-\epsilon)}{\epsilon^{3}\cdot t}\cdot(OPT-\frac{\epsilon t}{2})
, recall that
OPT\geq t/2
, then given any weak
(1-\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon))
-approximation of
(F,\hat{t})
, in linear time, the oracle will return a weak
(1-\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon))
-approximation of
(X,t)
. It thus suffices to consider
(F,\hat{t})
, that is, Lemma
15
and hence Theorem
3
, follows from the following Lemma
16
.

Lemma 16
.

Given any SUBSET SUM instance
(X,t)
, where the optimal objective value of
(X,t)
is at least
t/2
. Let
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
be an arbitrary fixed even number and let
(F,\hat{t})
a modified instance returned by Lemma
10
, where
F
and
\hat{t}
satisfy the followings:

1.

\Sigma(F)\leq\frac{4(1+\epsilon)\Sigma(X)}{\epsilon^{3}t}
and
\hat{t}=\frac{4(1+\epsilon)}{\epsilon^{3}}
.

2.

|F|\leq|X|
and
F
has been divided into
{\mathcal{O}}((\log|X|)^{2}(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{{\mathcal{O}}(d)})
subgroups:
F^{(j;i;k)}_{p}
’s.

3.

Each subgroup
F^{(j;i;k)}_{p}
satisfies the followings:

•

each element in
F^{(j;i;k)}_{p}
is distinct;

•

F^{(j;i;k)}_{p}\subset[\frac{(1-\epsilon)2^{p+j-1}}{\epsilon^{2}},\frac{(1+\epsilon)2^{p+j}}{\epsilon^{2}})
;

•

F^{(j;i;k)}_{p}=2^{p}\rho^{j}_{k}\overline{F}^{(j;i;k)}_{p}
, where
\overline{F}^{(j;i;k)}_{p}\subset\mathbb{N}_{+}\cap[\frac{1}{4\epsilon},\frac{1}{\epsilon}]
and
\overline{F}^{(j;i;k)}_{p}
is a set of
(\epsilon,d,d-1)
-smooth numbers, that is, every element in
\overline{F}^{(j;i;k)}_{p}
has been factorized as
h_{1}h_{2}\cdots h_{d}
, where
h_{d}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\cap[1,2\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{d}}]
and
h_{i}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\cap[\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{d}},2{\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{d}}}]
for
i=1,2,\cdots,d-1
.

Then in
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{3}{2}-\frac{1}{d}})
processing time, we can

(i).

Compute an
(\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon),\hat{t})
-approximate set
C
with cardinality of
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{3}{2}})
for
S(F)
.

(ii).

Meanwhile build an
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{3}{2}-\frac{1}{d}})
-time oracle for backtracking from
C
to
F
. The oracle actually works as follows: given any
c\in C
, in
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{3}{2}-\frac{1}{d}})
processing time, the oracle will return
U^{(j;i;k)}_{p}\subset F^{(j;i;k)}_{p}
for every
F^{(j;i;k)}_{p}
. Let
F^{\prime}
be the multiset-union of all
U^{(j;i;k)}_{p}
’s, we have
F^{\prime}\subset F
and
|\Sigma(F^{\prime})-c|\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)\hat{t}
.

The rest of this section is dedicated to proving Lemma
16
. Recall Corollary
2
, which allows us to build the approximation set of the union of
F^{(j;i;k)}_{p}
’s from the approximate set of each
F^{(j;i;k)}_{p}
.
Towards proving Lemma
16
, we only need to derive an algorithm that can solve the following
problem
-
\mathscr{Q}
in
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{3}{2}-\frac{1}{d}})
-time.

problem
-
\mathscr{Q}
: for every
F_{p}^{(j;i;k)}
, compute an
(\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon),\hat{t})
-approximate set with cardinality of
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{3}{2}})
for
S(F_{p}^{(j;i;k)})
and build an
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{3}{2}-\frac{1}{d}})
-time oracle for backtracking from this approximate set to
F_{p}^{(j;i;k)}
.

To solve
problem
-
\mathscr{Q}
, we only need to prove the following lemma
17
, where
M
represents an arbitrary
F_{p}^{(j;i;k)}
.

Lemma 17
.

Suppose we are given
M\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
satisfying the following conditions:

1.

Each element in
M
is distinct;

2.

M=\beta\tilde{M}
, where
\beta
and
\tilde{M}
satisfy the followings:

•

\beta\in\Theta(\frac{1}{\epsilon^{1+\lambda}})
and
\beta\tilde{M}\subset[\frac{(1-\epsilon)}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda}},\frac{2(1+\epsilon)}{\epsilon^{2+\lambda}}]
where
\lambda\geq 0
;

•

\tilde{M}\subset\mathbb{N}_{+}\cap[\frac{1}{4\epsilon},\frac{1}{\epsilon}]
and
\tilde{M}
is a set of
(\epsilon,d,d-1)
-smooth numbers, that is , every element in
\tilde{M}
has been factorized as
h_{1}h_{2}\cdots h_{d}
, where
h_{d}\in\mathbb{N}\cap[1,{2\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{d}}}]
and

h_{i}\in\mathbb{N}\cap[\frac{\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{d}}}{2},{2\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{d}}}]
for
i=1,2,\cdots,d-1
.

Here
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
is an arbitrary fixed
even integer. Then given any
\omega=\Theta(\epsilon^{-3})
, in
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{d}{\epsilon}+\frac{2^{{d}}}{\epsilon^{\frac{3}{2}+\frac{1}{d}}}(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{3})
processing time, we can compute an
(\epsilon,\omega)
-approximate set with cardinality of
{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{3}{2}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
for
S(M)
and build an
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{2^{{d}}}{\epsilon^{\frac{3}{2}+\frac{1}{d}}}(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{3})
-time oracle for backtracking from this approximate set to
M
.

The high-level proof idea of Lemma
17
resembles that of Lemma
14
. In particular, if
M
only contains a few distinct numbers, then we leverage Lemma
2
to handle it. Otherwise,
M
contains many numbers, then we again leverage the additive combinatoric result from
[
9
]
to deal with the case when the target
t
is in the medium range. If
t
is out of the medium range, we leverage Lemma
9
. Note that for general SUBSET SUM the target may be sufficiently smaller than the total sum of input numbers, therefore, unlike Lemma
14
, we cannot tolerate an additive error of
{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-2})
anymore, which means the idea of “coarse” rounding in proving Lemma
14
is inapplicable here. Therefore, we can only guarantee the running time of
\tilde{{\mathcal{O}}}(\epsilon^{-3/2})
.

It is easy to observe that
|M|={\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
. We call
M
a
dense
group if
|M|=\Omega(\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{2}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
, otherwise, we call
M
a
sparse
group. Sparse groups and dense groups will be handled separately.

7.1
Handling Sparse Group.

We first consider the case that
|M|={\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{2}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
.

Notice that
\tilde{M}\subset[\frac{1}{4\epsilon},\frac{1}{\epsilon}]
and
|\tilde{M}|={\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{2}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
, then we have
\Sigma(\tilde{M})={\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{3}{2}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
. If we regard each element in
\tilde{M}
as a set containing this single element, then according to Lemma
2
, in
{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{3}{2}}(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{3})
time, we can compute
S(\tilde{M})
and build an
{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{3}{2}}(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{3})
-time oracle for backtracking from
S(\tilde{M})
to
\tilde{M}
. Observe that
\left(\beta S(\tilde{M})\right)\cap[0,\omega]=S(M;[0,\omega])
and
S(M;[0,\omega])
is an
(\epsilon,\omega)
-approximate set for
S(M)
. Moreover, the oracle for backtracking from
S(\tilde{M})
to
\tilde{M}
directly yields an
{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{3}{2}}(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{3})
-time oracle for backtracking from
S(M;[0,\omega])
to
M
. Note that
S(\tilde{M})
is a set, we have
|S(\tilde{M})|\leq(S(\tilde{M}))^{\max}=\Sigma(\tilde{M})={\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{3}{2}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
. The time to compute
S(M;[0,\omega])
from
S(\tilde{M})
is
{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{3}{2}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
, then the overall processing time is
{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{3}{2}}(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{3})
. Thus Lemma
17
has been proved for the case that
M
is a sparse group.

It remains to prove Lemma
17
for the case that
M
is a dense group, which is the task of the following Subsection
7.2
.

7.2
Handling Dense Group

The goal of this section is to prove Lemma
17
for the case that
|M|=\Omega({\epsilon}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
.

In the following, we assume that
|M|=\Omega({\epsilon}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
. Define
L:=\frac{100\cdot\Sigma(M)\cdot\sqrt{\frac{1}{\epsilon}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}}{|M|}
. We approximate
S(M)
and build an oracle for backtracking in the following three steps:
(1). handle
S(M)\cap[0,L]
and
S(M)\cap[\Sigma(M)-L,\Sigma(M)]
; (2). handle
S(M)\cap(L,\Sigma(M)-L)
; (3). handle
S(M)
.

Step 1: Handling
S(M)\cap[0,L]
and
S(M)\cap[\Sigma(M)-L,\Sigma(M)]
.

In this step, we aim to prove the following claim.

Claim 5
.

In
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{d}{\epsilon}+\frac{2^{{d}}}{\epsilon^{\frac{3}{2}+\frac{1}{d}}}(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{2})
processing time, we can:

(i).

Compute
S(M)\cap\left([0,L]\cup[\Sigma(M)-L,\Sigma(M)]\right)
;

(ii).

Meanwhile build an
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{2^{{d}}}{\epsilon^{\frac{3}{2}+\frac{1}{d}}}(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{2})
-time oracle for backtracking from
S(M)\cap\left([0,L]\cup[\Sigma(M)-L,\Sigma(M)]\right)
to
M
. That is, given any
c\in S(M)\cap\left([0,L]\cup[\Sigma(M)-L,\Sigma(M)]\right)
, in
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{2^{{d}}}{\epsilon^{\frac{3}{2}+\frac{1}{d}}}(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{2})
time, the oracle will return
M_{c}\subset M
such that
\Sigma(M_{c})=c
.

Moreover, it holds that
\left|S(M)\cap\left([0,L]\cup[\Sigma(M)-L,\Sigma(M)]\right)\right|={\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{3}{2}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
.

Proof.

We first consider
S(M;[0,L])
. Notice that elements in
\tilde{M}
are
(\epsilon,d,d-1)
-smooth numbers. Recall Lemma
9
, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}\cap[0,d-1]
, in
T_{L}={\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon}+(d-1)\cdot|\tilde{M}|+\Sigma(\tilde{M}){\epsilon^{\frac{k}{d}}}\log(|\tilde{M}|)\log(\Sigma(\tilde{M}){\epsilon^{\frac{k}{d}}})+2^{2k+1}{\epsilon^{\frac{-1}{d}}}{\frac{L}{\beta}}\log({\frac{L}{\beta}}))
processing time, we can compute
S(\tilde{M};[0,\frac{L}{\beta}])
, and meanwhile build a
T^{\prime}_{L}
-time oracle for backtracking from
S(\tilde{M};[0,\frac{L}{\beta}])
to
\tilde{M}
, where
T^{\prime}_{L}={\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma(\tilde{M}){\epsilon^{\frac{k}{d}}}\log(|\tilde{M}|)\log(\Sigma(\tilde{M}){\epsilon^{\frac{k}{d}}})+2^{2k+1}{\epsilon^{\frac{-1}{d}}}{\frac{L}{\beta}}\log({\frac{L}{\beta}})))
. It is easy to observe that
S({M};[0,{L}])=\beta S(\tilde{M};[0,\frac{L}{\beta}])
and the oracle for backtracking from
S(\tilde{M};[0,\frac{L}{\beta}])
to
\tilde{M}
directly yields an
{\mathcal{O}}(T^{\prime}_{L})
-time oracle for backtracking from
S({M};[0,{L}])
to
M
. Recall that
L=\frac{100\cdot\Sigma(M)\cdot\sqrt{\frac{1}{\epsilon}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}}{|M|}
, then
\frac{L}{\beta}=\frac{100\cdot\Sigma(\tilde{M})\cdot\sqrt{\frac{1}{\epsilon}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}}{|\tilde{M}|}
. Since
\tilde{M}\subset[\frac{1}{4\epsilon},\frac{1}{\epsilon}]
and
|\tilde{M}|=|M|={\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
, we have
\frac{L}{\beta}={\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{3}{2}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
and
\Sigma(\tilde{M})={\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}})
.
Note that
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
is divisible by
2
, by fixing
k
to
\frac{d}{2}
, we have
T_{L}={\mathcal{O}}(\frac{d}{\epsilon}+\frac{2^{{d}}}{\epsilon^{\frac{3}{2}+\frac{1}{d}}}(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{2})
and
T^{\prime}_{L}={\mathcal{O}}(\frac{2^{{d}}}{\epsilon^{\frac{3}{2}+\frac{1}{d}}}(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{2})
. Note that
S(\tilde{M};[0,\frac{L}{\beta}])
is a set, thus
|S(M;[0,L])|=|S(\tilde{M};[0,\frac{L}{\beta}])|={\mathcal{O}}(\frac{L}{\beta})={\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{3}{2}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
.

Note that given any
E\subset M
with
\Sigma(E)\in[0,L]
, then
M\backslash E
satisfies
\Sigma(M\backslash E)\in[\Sigma(M)-L,\Sigma(M)]
, and conversely, given any
E^{\prime}\subset M
with
\Sigma(E^{\prime})\in[\Sigma(M)-L,\Sigma(M)]
, then
M\backslash E^{\prime}
satisfies
\Sigma(M\backslash E^{\prime})\in[0,L]
. Thus
S(M)\cap[\Sigma(M)-L,\Sigma(M)]=\left\{\Sigma(M)-c:c\in S(M;[0,L])\right\}
, which implies that given
S(M;[0,L])
, in
|S(M;[0,L])|={\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{3}{2}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
processing time, we can obtain
S(M)\cap[\Sigma(M)-L,\Sigma(M)]
from
S(M;[0,L])
. Observe that

\left|S(M)\cap[\Sigma(M)-L,\Sigma(M)]\right|=\left|S(M;[0,L])\right|={\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{3}{2}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
, we have
\left|S(M)\cap\left([0,L]\cup[\Sigma(M)-L,\Sigma(M)]\right)\right|={\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{3}{2}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
.
Moreover, given any
T^{\prime}_{L}
-time oracle for backtracking from
S(M;[0,L])
to
M
, then this oracle directly yields an
{\mathcal{O}}(T^{\prime}_{L})
-time oracle for backtracking from
S(M)\cap[\Sigma(M)-L,\Sigma(M)]
to
M
.

To summarize, in overall
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{d}{\epsilon}+\frac{2^{{d}}}{\epsilon^{\frac{3}{2}+\frac{1}{d}}}(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{2})
time, we can compute
S(M)\cap\left([0,L]\cup[\Sigma(M)-L,\Sigma(M)]\right)
and build an
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{2^{{d}}}{\epsilon^{\frac{3}{2}+\frac{1}{d}}}(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{2})
-time oracle for backtracking from
S(M)\cap\left([0,L]\cup[\Sigma(M)-L,\Sigma(M)]\right)
to
M
. ∎∎

Step 2: Handling
S(M)\cap(L,\Sigma(M)-L)
.

In this step, we aim to prove the following claim.

Claim 6
.

In
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
processing time, we can:

(i).

Compute a subset
\Gamma_{mid}\subset S(M)\cap(L,\Sigma(M)-L)
satisfying the following conditions: (1).
|\Gamma_{mid}|={\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
; (2).
\Gamma_{mid}\subset[0,\omega]
; (3). Given any
s\in S(M)\cap(L,\Sigma(M)-L)\cap[0,\omega]
, there exists
s^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{mid}
such that
|s^{\prime}-s|\leq\epsilon\omega
.

(ii).

Meanwhile build an
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
-time oracle for backtracking from
\Gamma_{mid}
to
M
. That is, given any
c\in\Gamma_{mid}
, in
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
time, the oracle will return
M_{c}\subset M
such that
\Sigma(M_{c})=c
.

Proof.

Recall that
\tilde{M}\subset[\frac{1}{4\epsilon},\frac{1}{\epsilon}]
and each element in
\tilde{M}
is distinct, moreover,
\frac{100\cdot\Sigma(\tilde{M})\cdot\sqrt{\frac{1}{\epsilon}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}}{|\tilde{M}|}=\frac{L}{\beta}
. Notice that we now consider the case that
|\tilde{M}|=|M|=\Omega(\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{2}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
, recall Theorem
2
, in
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
time, we can construct a data structure which allows us to solve the SUBSET SUM problem
(\tilde{M},c)
in
{\mathcal{O}}(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
time, where
c
is any integer in
(\frac{L}{\beta},\Sigma(\tilde{M})-\frac{L}{\beta})
.

We define a set
\mathcal{C}
as follows: if
\frac{\omega}{\beta}\leq\frac{L}{\beta}
, let
\mathcal{C}=\emptyset
; else let
\mathcal{C}=\{c_{1},c_{2},\cdots,c_{\iota}\}
denote the set of all different integers in
\left\{\lfloor\epsilon\frac{\omega}{\beta}\rfloor,\lfloor 2\epsilon\frac{\omega}{\beta}\rfloor,\cdots,\lfloor\frac{\omega}{\beta}\rfloor,\lfloor\min\{\frac{\omega}{\beta},\Sigma(\tilde{M})-\frac{L}{\beta}\}\rfloor\right\}\cap\left(\frac{L}{\beta},\min\{\frac{\omega}{\beta},\Sigma(\tilde{M})-\frac{L}{\beta}\}\right)
. Then we solve SUBSET SUM problem
(\tilde{M},c)
for every integer
c\in\mathcal{C}
. Denote by
E_{i}
the solution of SUBSET SUM problem
(\tilde{M},c_{i})
, let
\mathcal{E}:=\{E_{1},E_{2},\cdots,E_{\iota}\}
and let
\Gamma_{mid}:=\{\beta\Sigma(E_{1}),\beta\Sigma(E_{2}),\cdots,\beta\Sigma(E_{\iota})\}
. We build a dictionary
\mathscr{D}
as follows: each element in
\mathscr{D}
is a key-value pair
(\beta\Sigma(E_{i}),\mathscr{D}[\beta\Sigma(E_{i})])
, where
\mathscr{D}[\beta\Sigma(E_{i})]=\beta E_{i}
and
i=1,2,\cdots,\iota
. The collection of keys of
\mathscr{D}
is
\Gamma_{mid}
.

In the next, we show that
\Gamma_{mid}
satisfies the conditions required in Claim
6
. Apparently,
|\Gamma_{mid}|={\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
and
\Gamma_{mid}\subset[0,\omega]
. Consider any
s\in S(M)\cap(L,\Sigma(M)-L)\cap[0,\omega]
, it follows that
\frac{s}{\beta}\in S(\tilde{M})\cap(\frac{L}{\beta},\Sigma(\tilde{M})-\frac{L}{\beta})\cap[0,\frac{\omega}{\beta}]
and there exists some integer
k\in[0,\frac{1}{\epsilon}]
such that
k\epsilon\frac{\omega}{\beta}<\frac{s}{\beta}\leq(1+k)\epsilon\frac{\omega}{\beta}
, thus we have

\left\lfloor k\epsilon\frac{\omega}{\beta}\right\rfloor<\frac{s}{\beta}\leq\min\left\{\left\lfloor(1+k)\epsilon\frac{\omega}{\beta}\right\rfloor,\left\lfloor\min\left\{\frac{\omega}{\beta},\sum\left(\tilde{M}\right)-\frac{L}{\beta}\right\}\right\rfloor\right\}.

Observe that
\min\left\{\left\lfloor(1+k)\epsilon\frac{\omega}{\beta}\right\rfloor,\left\lfloor\min\left\{\frac{\omega}{\beta},\sum\left(\tilde{M}\right)-\frac{L}{\beta}\right\}\right\rfloor\right\}\in\mathcal{C}
, which guarantees that there exists some
E_{k}\in\mathcal{E}
satisfying
\Sigma(E_{k})\in[k\epsilon\frac{\omega}{\beta},(k+1)\epsilon\frac{\omega}{\beta}]
, then
\beta\Sigma(E_{k})\in\Gamma_{mid}
and
|s-\beta\Sigma(E_{k})|\leq\beta\cdot\epsilon\frac{\omega}{\beta}=\epsilon\omega
.

An
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
-time oracle for backtracking from
\Gamma_{mid}
to
M
works as follows: given any
c\in\Gamma_{mid}
, the oracle uses dictionary
\mathscr{D}
to return
\mathscr{D}[c]
. It is easy to see that
\mathscr{D}[c]\subset M
and
\Sigma(\mathscr{D}[c])=c
.∎∎

Step 3: Handling
S(M)
.

Given
\Gamma_{mid}
, which is obtained in Step 2. Let
\Gamma_{LR}=\left(S(M)\cap([0,L]\cup[\Sigma(M)-L,\Sigma(M)])\right)\cap[0,\omega]
and let
\Gamma=\Gamma_{mid}\cup\left(S(M)\cap([0,L]\cup[\Sigma(M)-L,\Sigma(M)])\right)
. Then we have the
following Claim
7
, which follows directly from Claim
5
and Claim
6
.

Claim 7
.

\Gamma
is an
(\epsilon,\omega)
-approximate set of
S(M)
. Moreover, we can build an

{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{2^{{d}}}{\epsilon^{\frac{3}{2}+\frac{1}{d}}}(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{2})
-time oracle for backtracking from
\Gamma
to
M
.

According to Claim
5
and Claim
6
, we have
|\Gamma_{LR}|={\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{3}{2}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
and
|\Gamma_{mid}|={\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})
, it follows that
|\Gamma|={\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-\frac{3}{2}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
. Recall that the total time for computing
\Gamma_{LR}
and
\Gamma_{mid}
is
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{d}{\epsilon}+\frac{2^{{d}}}{\epsilon^{\frac{3}{2}+\frac{1}{d}}}(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{2})
, thus the overall time for computing
\Gamma
is
{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{d}{\epsilon}+\frac{2^{{d}}}{\epsilon^{\frac{3}{2}+\frac{1}{d}}}(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{2})
. Combine with Claim
7
, we have proved Lemma
17
for the case that
M
is dense, which completes the proof of Lemma
17
, furthermore, completes the proof of Lemma
16
. Till now, we have completed the proof of Theorem
3
.

8
An
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(n+\epsilon^{-1})
-time weak
(1-\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon))
-approximation algorithm for UNBOUNDED SUBSET SUM.

The goal of this section is to prove the following Theorem.

Theorem 4
.

There is a deterministic weak
(1-\epsilon)
-approximation algorithm for UNBOUNDED SUBSET SUM running in
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(n+{\epsilon}^{-1})
time.

Given any UNBOUNDED SUBSET SUM instance un-
(X,t)
, where
X=\{x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{n}\}
is a set of different positive integers and
t>0
is a fixed constant. Let
OPT
be the optimal objective value of the instance un-
(X,t)
, our goal is to find a multiset
X^{\prime}
with
set(X^{\prime})\subset X
such that

(1-\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon))OPT\leq\Sigma(X^{\prime})\leq(1+\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon))t,

moreover, the time to determine
X^{\prime}
is
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(n+\epsilon^{-1})
.

Without loss of generality, we can assume that
0<x\leq(1+\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon))t
for every
x\in X
. Moreover, we have the following useful preliminary lemma.

Lemma 18
.

Given any UNBOUNDED SUBSET SUM instance un-
(X,t)
, where
0<x\leq(1+\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon))t
for every
x\in X
. Let
OPT
be the optimal objective value of un-
(X,t)
. In
{\mathcal{O}}(|X|)
processing time, we can either find a weak
(1-\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon))
-approximation solution for un-
(X,t)
, or assert that
\epsilon t<x<t
for every
x\in X
.

Proof.

Note that if there exists some
x\in X
with
t\leq x\leq(1+\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon))t
, then
\{x\}
yields a weak
(1-\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon))
-approximation solution for un-
(X,t)
. If there exists some
x\in X
with
0<x\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)t
, then a multiset
X^{\prime}
with
set(X^{\prime})=\{x\}
and
|X^{\prime}|=\lfloor\frac{t}{x}\rfloor+1
yields a weak
(1-\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon))
-approximation solution for un-
(X,t)
.

It is easy to see that, in
{\mathcal{O}}(|X|)
time, we can determine whether there exists some
x\in X
satisfying
t\leq x\leq(1+\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon))t
or
0<x\leq\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)t
. ∎∎

The rest of this section is dedicated to proving Theorem
4
. Consider any UNBOUNDED SUBSET SUM instance un-
(X,t)
, where
\epsilon t<x<t
for every
x\in X
. In Section
8.1
, we focus on simplifying
\text{un-}(X,t)
to obtain a BOUNDED SUBSET SUM instance
(Y,\hat{t})
, where
Y
is divided into
{\mathcal{O}}(\log|X|(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{2})
subgroups:
Y^{j}_{k}
’s. In particular, we derive Lemma
20
which guarantees that towards approximating un-
(X,t)
, it is sufficient to consider
(Y,\hat{t})
. In Section
8.2
, we focus on approximating
(Y,\hat{t})
. Corollary
2
guarantees that towards approximating
(Y,\hat{t})
, it is sufficient to consider each
Y^{j}_{k}
. Subsection
8.2.1
and Subsection
8.2.2
are dedicated to approximating
Y^{j}_{k}
for two separate cases.

Before we move on to the details, we briefly present the main idea. The crucial observation that leads to an almost linear time algorithm for UNBOUNDED SUBSET SUM is the following sparsification lemma by Klein
[
26
]
on the exact algorithm for UNBOUNDED SUBSET SUM.

Lemma 19

(CF. Corollary 1. from
[
26
]
)
.

Given a set
X=\{x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{n}\}\subset\mathbb{N}
. If there is a feasible solution to the following integer program:

\displaystyle\mathbf{IP_{t}:}

\displaystyle\sum^{n}_{i=1}v_{i}x_{i}=t

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}}=(v_{1},v_{2},\cdots,v_{n})\in\mathbb{N}^{n},

Then there is a solution
\textstyle\bf v
to
\mathbf{IP_{t}}
such that
\text{supp}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}})\leq\log_{2}(X^{min})+1
, where
\text{supp}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}})
denotes the number of nonzero coordinates of
\textstyle\bf v
, i.e.,
\text{supp}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}})=|\{i:v_{i}\neq 0\}|
.

Lemma
19
implies that, for UNBOUNDED SUBSET SUM problem, any subset-sum, including
OPT
, can be achieved by a sparse solution
\textstyle\bf v
in the sense its support
\text{supp}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}})
is bounded by a logarithmic value. More precisely, given
(X,t)
as an input, we let
T(X):=\{\sum^{n}_{i=1}v_{i}x_{i}|{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}}\in\mathbb{N}^{n}\}
. Suppose
x_{i}=(1/\epsilon)^{{\mathcal{O}}(1)}
for simplicity, then
T(X)=T_{sparse}(X)
by Lemma
19
, where
T_{sparse}(X):=\{\sum^{n}_{i=1}v_{i}x_{i}|{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}}\in\mathbb{N}^{n},\text{supp}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}})={\mathcal{O}}(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})\}
. Now consider an arbitrary
\sum^{n}_{i=1}v_{i}x_{i}\in T_{sparse}(X)
. Those nonzero
v_{i}
’s do not necessarily take the same value. However, by writing each nonzero
v_{i}
into a binary number,
v_{i}=\sum_{h}\gamma_{h}(v_{i})\cdot 2^{h}
where
\gamma_{h}(v_{i})\in\{0,1\}
, we have the following equivalent expression:

\sum^{n}_{i=1}v_{i}x_{i}=\sum_{h}2^{h}\sum^{n}_{i=1}\gamma_{h}(v_{i})x_{i},\quad\quad\text{supp}(\gamma_{h}(v_{1}),\gamma_{h}(v_{2}),\cdots,\gamma_{h}(v_{n}))={\mathcal{O}}(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}).

Therefore, let
G_{\log}
stands for the set of sums of at most
{\mathcal{O}}(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
elements of
X
, then the above equation implies that
T_{sparse}(X)\subset G_{\log}\oplus 2G_{\log}\oplus\cdots\oplus 2^{h}G_{\log}\cdots
. On the other hand,
G_{\log}\oplus 2G_{\log}\oplus\cdots\oplus 2^{h}G_{\log}\cdots\subset T(X)=T_{sparse}(X)
, hence
T_{sparse}(X)=G_{\log}\oplus 2G_{\log}\oplus\cdots\oplus 2^{h}G_{\log}\cdots
. Using the above equation and the fact that
G_{\log}
can be approximated in nearly linear time, Theorem
4
can be proved. In the subsequent subsections, we provide the details.

8.1
Preprocessing the UNBOUDED SUBSET SUM instance.

Given any UNBOUNDED SUBSET SUM instance un-
(X,t)
, where
X=\{x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{n}\}
is a set of distinct positive integers and
t>0
is a fixed constant. Assuming that
\epsilon t<x<t
for every
x\in X
, the goal of this section is to simplify
(X,t)
. Formally, we have the following lemma.

Lemma 20
.

Given any UNBOUNDED SUBSET SUM instance un-
(X,t)
, where
\epsilon t<x<t
for every
x\in X
.
Let
OPT
be the optimal objective value of un-
(X,t)
. In
{\mathcal{O}}\left((|X|+\frac{1}{\epsilon})(\log|X|)^{2}(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{2}\right)
time, the followings can be achieved:

(i).

We can obtain a modified BOUNDED SUBSET SUM instance
(Y,\hat{t})
satisfying the following conditions:

(A)

\hat{t}=(1+\epsilon)\epsilon^{-3}
.

(B)

The optimal objective value of
(Y,\hat{t})
is at least
\frac{(1-\epsilon)OPT}{\epsilon^{3}t}
.

(C)

Y
is divided into
{\mathcal{O}}(\log|X|(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{2})
groups:
Y^{j}_{k}
’s, i.e.,
Y=\mathop{\dot{\cup}}\limits_{j}(\mathop{\dot{\cup}}\limits_{k}Y^{j}_{k})
.

(D)

set(Y^{j}_{k})
is explicitly given for every
Y^{j}_{k}
and
\mathop{\sum}\limits_{j}\mathop{\sum}\limits_{k}|set(Y^{j}_{k})|\leq|X|
.

(E)

Each subgroup
Y^{j}_{k}
satisfies the following conditions:

(a)

Y^{j}_{k}\subset[\frac{2^{j-1}(1-\epsilon)}{\epsilon^{2}},\frac{2^{j}(1+\epsilon)}{\epsilon^{2}}]
;

(b)

Y^{j}_{k}=\rho^{j}_{k}\overline{Y}^{j}_{k}
, where
\rho^{j}_{k}=\Omega(\epsilon^{-1})
and
\overline{Y}^{j}_{k}\subset\mathbb{N}_{+}\cap[\frac{1}{2\epsilon},\frac{1}{\epsilon}]
;

(c)

Let
n^{j}_{k}=\lfloor\frac{\hat{t}}{(Y^{j}_{k})^{\min}}\rfloor
and let

\displaystyle l^{j}_{k}=\begin{cases}&2^{1+pow(\log_{2}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})+1)}\hskip 25.60747pt\ \text{if }n^{j}_{k}\leq 2^{1+pow(\log_{2}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})+1)}\\
&2n^{j}_{k}\cdot 2^{1+pow(\log_{2}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})+1)}\hskip 5.69054pt\ \text{if }n^{j}_{k}>2^{1+pow(\log_{2}(\frac{1}{\epsilon})+1)}\end{cases}

then
Y^{j}_{k}
consists of
l^{j}_{k}
copies of
set(Y^{j}_{k})
, i.e., every element of
Y^{j}_{k}
has the same multiplicity, which is
l^{j}_{k}
.

(ii).

Meanwhile, we can build an oracle for backtracking from
Y
to
X
. Precisely, for each
Y^{j}_{k}
, given any
y\in Y^{j}_{k}
, in
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
time, the oracle will return
x^{\prime}\in X
such that
|x^{\prime}-\epsilon^{3}ty|\leq\epsilon x^{\prime}
.

Rermark.
We briefly explain how Lemma
20
is leveraged to prove Theorem
4
. Roughly speaking, Lemma
20
reduces the UNBOUNDED SUBSET SUM to the bounded version (but has a special structure) so that we may utilize techniques developed in previous sections. More precisely, consider the second part of Lemma
20
, for any subset
U^{j}_{k}\subset Y^{j}_{k}
, if
card_{U^{j}_{k}}[y]
is known for every
y\in set(Y^{j}_{k})
, then in
{\mathcal{O}}(|set(Y^{j}_{k})|)
time, the oracle will return a multiset
X^{j}_{k}
such that
set(X^{j}_{k})\subset X
and
|\Sigma(X^{j}_{k})-\epsilon^{3}t\Sigma(U^{j}_{k})|\leq\epsilon\Sigma(X^{j}_{k})
. Furthermore, given subset
U^{j}_{k}\subset Y^{j}_{k}
for every
Y^{j}_{k}
and let
U^{\prime}
be the multiset-union of all these
U^{j}_{k}
’s. For each
U^{j}_{k}
, if
card_{U^{j}_{k}}[y]
is known for every
y\in set(Y^{j}_{k})
, then in
{\mathcal{O}}(\sum_{j}\sum_{k}|set(Y^{j}_{k})|)={\mathcal{O}}(|X|)
time, the oracle will return a multiset
X^{\prime}
such that
set(X^{\prime})\subset X
and
|\Sigma(X^{\prime})-\epsilon^{3}t\Sigma(U^{\prime})|\leq\epsilon\Sigma(X^{\prime})
. Moreover, note that the optimal objective value of
(Y,\hat{t})
is at least
\frac{(1-\epsilon)OPT}{\epsilon^{3}t}
, if
U^{\prime}
is a weak
(1-\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon))
-approximation solution of
(Y,\hat{t})
, then
X^{\prime}
is a weak
(1-\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon))
-approximation solution of un-
(X,t)
. Thus towards proving Theorem
4
, it is sufficient to consider
(Y,\hat{t})
, which shall be handled in the next subsection, Subsection
8.2
.

The rest of this section is dedicated to proving Lemma
20
